# SUMMARY OF THE WHOLE WORK.

**PART ONE** *contains three treatises.*

*The first of these teaches the three principles of Physics, which indicate the Theory of Chemistry.* <note>original: "tria Physices docet principia." In the 16th century, "Physics" referred to the study of nature (Natural Philosophy). The "three principles" likely refer to the Paracelsian Tria Prima: Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury.</note>
*The second teaches the three principles of the practice of Chemistry.*
*The third contains manual practice and the application to the work.* <note>The "work" (original: "opus") refers to the *Magnum Opus* or Great Work—the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note>

**THE SECOND PART** *teaches the seven philosophical steps, through which the minds of students may be able to reach the peak and the goal of truth and its virtues in this art.* <note>These "seven steps" are the traditional stages of alchemical transformation, such as calcination, dissolution, and fermentation.</note>

->Added are:<-

*The fabric of the world.*
*The pilgrimage of common life.*
*The path of error.*
*Also, the path of truth.*

*Furthermore, certain summaries have been added to individual chapters, along with Dialogues, by which certain metaphysical matters are elucidated through Physics and Chemistry.* <note>original: "Colloquia." These dialogues were a common pedagogical tool used to explain complex spiritual and physical concepts.</note>

->Also, the supernatural artifice.<-

<summary>This page provides a table of contents for the book, detailing a two-part structure that moves from the theoretical "principles of physics" to the practical "manual practice" and "seven philosophical steps" of alchemy.</summary>

## TO THE PIOUS AND LEARNED
## PHYSICIANS, WHO HAVE GAINED
## profit from the books of Doctor Theo-
## phrastus Paracelsus, or from reading
## them, Gerard Dorn

->S. D. P.<-
<note>original: "S. D. P." (Salutem Dicit Plurimam). A standard Latin greeting meaning "sends heartiest greetings."</note>

I know it is not hidden from you, brothers, with what great disgrace the ignorant across the world treat our teacher **Theophrastus Paracelsus**, either because they see that he easily surpassed any <term>Physicians</term> <note>original: "Medicos." In this era, "Physicians" were university-educated doctors who focused on internal medicine and theory.</note> of our age, as well as the ancients, as much in **experience** as in **art**, or because he deservedly reproached ignorant Physicians, <term>Natural Philosophers</term>, <note>original: "Physicos." Scholars of "Physics" or natural philosophy who studied the laws of nature.</note> and **surgeons** for their enormous errors. And these people do not see how shamefully, being conscious of their own state, they admit their ignorance, since he strives not so much to carp at the ignorant, as to teach them, and he takes care to greatly praise and exhort everyone who is learned in their **art**. Learned men do not take that reproach upon themselves, as if it did not concern them, but they have tried to imitate his admirable experience rather than revile it. On the contrary, those who to themselves—

<summary>In this dedication, Gerard Dorn defends the legacy of Paracelsus, arguing that his harsh criticisms were aimed only at incompetent practitioners, while truly learned physicians should embrace his advancements in medical experience.</summary>

<keywords>Gerard Dorn, Paracelsus, medical education, defense of alchemy, physicians vs surgeons, medical errors, history of medicine</keywords>

*seem wise to themselves, they are accustomed to approve of nothing of which they are ignorant: moved by an excessively crude <term>envy</term> <note>original: "inuidia." In this context, it refers to a malicious jealousy that seeks to undermine another's success.</note>, such people sharpen their teeth against those who try to benefit everyone through their own labor. Therefore, I urge you, brothers (lest such a great crime remain unpunished), take up your pen against those who are not ashamed only to corrupt the medical art, but even to persecute those whose every effort is constantly focused on recovering the truth buried in this art.*

*Our <term>teacher</term> <note>original: "praeceptor." Here referring to Paracelsus, whom Dorn viewed as his primary intellectual mentor.</note> certainly achieved this, when, having traveled through countless regions, he alone restored <term>chemical medicine</term> <note>original: "Medicinam chymisticam." A precursor to modern pharmacology, focusing on the use of minerals and chemicals rather than just herbs.</note> to our age (and not without the greatest expense). *Come, good men, let us resist, lest this vile heap of critics, increased by example, should presume to overthrow the good arts along with their pious and true <term>Professors</term> <note>original: "Professoribus." Not necessarily university faculty, but those who "profess" or openly practice and teach these truths.</note>—if not by right, then at least by injury. Not because they can achieve anything by themselves, but because they see themselves as entirely destitute of strength, they give themselves up as prey to giants; for they know that these monsters of men [do] nothing...*

# TO THE READER
## PREFACE.

*You have here, excellent reader, the theory and practice of the entire chemical <term>chymical art</term> <note>original: "Chymisticæ." In the 16th century, "chemistry" and "alchemy" were not yet distinct disciplines; the term encompassed both laboratory science and spiritual philosophy.</note>, of which you cannot remain ignorant (unless you so choose) by reading this little compendium. Read and reread it many times with a calm and attentive mind, passing over nothing casually or without due consideration. For if you gather together the scattered thoughts of our mind, nothing of those things which are beautiful in this art will ever be able to remain hidden from you. You will find here, beneath a hard shell, the liquid <note>original: "laticem," a poetic word for a flowing stream or juice, here used to describe the essential core of a remedy.</note> of true medicine, with which you may—by the help of God—relieve yourself, as well as your friends and the poor, from bodily illnesses. May you not regret the labors of performing the **practice** <note>original: "praxin," referring to the physical work in the laboratory as opposed to theoretical study.</note> which we have described for you; for by building, we ourselves have been made builders in this art, not by reading alone. If it should happen by chance that you do not achieve your desire on the first or second attempt, do not lose heart, but repeat the work a third and fourth time. It happened to me quite often that, even while making errors, I eventually mastered that which my mind sought. Nothing prevents a person from reaching success in this art more than faint-heartedness and impatience. I would only wish to warn you of this one thing now: that in the beginning, you do not exceed the limits of medicine for the human body; for if you seek the metamorphosis <note>original: "metamorphosin," the transformation or transmutation of one substance into another.</note> of common metals, you will surely lament your error. Farewell;*
->*judge our labors*<-
->*fairly and kindly.*<-

<vocab>Chemical art, medicine, practice, theory, transformation (transmutation), alchemy</vocab>

<summary>Gerard Dorn introduces his manual by emphasizing that chemical knowledge is gained through repeated laboratory practice rather than just reading. He warns the reader to focus on healing human illness rather than the greed-driven pursuit of turning common metals into gold.</summary>

<keywords>chemical art, alchemy, laboratory practice, medical healing, transmutation, perseverance, Gerard Dorn, Paracelsian medicine</keywords>

<page-num>8</page-num>
<header>OF THE CHEMICAL ART</header>

### What Chemical Philosophy Is. CHAPTER I.

Chemical Philosophy <note>original: "Chymistica Philosophia." In the 16th century, this referred to a holistic study of nature that combined what we now call chemistry with spiritual and philosophical insights.</note> teaches how to investigate the hidden forms of things according to the actual truth of the matter, rather than by mere appearance. The true disciples of this art enter upon two paths: **Opinion** and **Experiment**. For those who happen to rely on only one of these, it is inevitable that they will deceive themselves and others by their teaching or their works. However, whoever has examined both and judges one by the other will not be frustrated in their labor, nor will those to whom they bring help or counsel. **Opinion** <note>original: "Opinio." In this context, it refers to theoretical hypotheses or logical deductions that have not yet been proven in the laboratory.</note> indeed presupposes doubtful things which nevertheless seem true through philosophical reasoning; however, these must finally be verified by experience. Therefore, it seemed safest to me in compiling this little work to maintain both paths, changing only the names to **Theory** and **Practice**. 

But before I proceed further, I will not be reluctant to hand over the "key" <note>A common alchemical metaphor for a fundamental secret or method that allows the practitioner to understand all other mysteries.</note> by which nothing may remain closed to those who are called, but rather the storehouses of nature’s secrets may be unlocked. That which is given freely by the **Most High** <note>original: "Altissimo," a reference to God as the source of all wisdom.</note> to those who fear Him, receive you also freely—you who both fear and love the Lord. By the study of things, knowledge is acquired. Love follows knowledge. From desire or delight, persistence is born. Constant repetition brings experience. Experience gives virtue, and persistence gives power.

->By this, the wonderful work is perfected in<-
->the nature of things, which<-
->is as follows:<-

<meta>catchword: Nature</meta>

<summary>Dorn defines Chemical Philosophy as the search for hidden truths through the dual paths of Theory (Opinion) and Practice (Experiment), warning that one is useless without the other. He frames this knowledge as a divine gift granted to those who combine study with a love for God.</summary>
<keywords>Chemical Philosophy, Theory and Practice, Opinion, Experiment, Nature's Secrets, Gerard Dorn, Divine Wisdom</keywords>

<page-num>9</page-num>
<header>BOOK ONE.</header>
<sig>A v</sig>

### *What Nature Is.* CHAPTER II.

Among the Chemists <note>original: "Chymistas." In the 16th century, this term referred to practitioners of alchemy who sought to understand the hidden workings of the natural world.</note>, Nature is defined as the stimulation of the Heavens combined with the Elements toward the generation of all things that exist. This craftsmanship is perfected by a twofold instrument: namely, **disposition** and **influx** <note>original: "influxu." This refers to the celestial influences or "rays" believed to flow from the stars and planets into the material world.</note>. Nature, by breaking down matter <note>original: "corrumpendo." In alchemical theory, "corruption" is the necessary decay or dissolution of a substance that precedes its transformation into something new.</note>, prepares it to be receptive to the power of form and celestial influences; eventually, at its appointed time, this bursts forth into action and a specific form. This is the meaning of that philosophical maxim: *Form acts according to the disposition of the matter.* <note>original: "Secundùm dispositionem materię agit forma." This means that the spiritual "form" of a thing can only manifest as well as the physical material allows.</note>

### *What Form Is.* CHAPTER III.

**Form** is the action and power of the ethereal realm exerted upon the elemental realm. Through the mutual transformation of the elements, Nature prepares the matter, enclosing the form within it just as a seed is held in a womb; pregnant with this power, it produces its offspring—like a mother—into a specific state of being. This is what the philosophers mean when they say: *Form is drawn out from the potential of matter.* <note>original: "Forma deducitur de potentia materiæ." This suggests that the blueprint for a thing's perfection is already hidden within its raw material.</note> For matter itself is capable of giving birth to that which form has generated within it. 

One must conclude, therefore, that **form** is the active agent working upon **matter**, which is the passive recipient <note>original: "patientem," literally "suffering" or "enduring" the action.</note> of its action; between these two, a magnetic nature <term>magnetic nature (natura magnetica): a mediating force of attraction that binds the spiritual "form" to the physical "matter."</term> acts as the mediating craftsman. This force never ceases to stir up specific forms from the universal form of the whole, and it is no more idle than the very movement of the heavens.

<summary>Dorn defines Nature as a celestial-elemental interaction and Form as the active, seed-like power that manifests within prepared matter via a "magnetic" mediating force.</summary>
<keywords>Nature, Form, Matter, Celestial Influx, Disposition, Magnetic Nature, Alchemy, Generation</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues Gerard Dorn's exploration of the foundations of the "Chemical Art." Having defined Nature and Form on the previous page as the active, celestial forces that shape the world, he now turns to "Matter"—the physical substance upon which those forces act. He presents a simplified elemental theory where Fire and Water are the primary opposing forces.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>
<header>OF THE CHEMICAL ART</header>

### *What Matter Is.* CHAPTER IV.

**Matter**, for us, is the entire elemental realm. Although it is divided into four parts (which we call **elements**) — fire, air, water, and earth — there are nevertheless two principal ones: namely, fire and water. This is because air seems to be nothing other than water that was released <note>original: "resoluta." In this context, it suggests a substance being broken down or evaporated into a finer state.</note> through heat during the creation of the world; earth, meanwhile, is water thickened, or rather dried out, by heat and dryness.

Fire, by its own nature, strives as much as it can to convert water into itself. To accomplish this more easily, it employs two means according to the qualities of both: first, it uses its heat to consume coldness, and then its dryness to consume the watery moisture. We see clouds suspended by the heat of fire, holding a middle position between water and fire. However, if the heat of the fire—which is weaker near the surface <note>The "surface" refers to the lower atmosphere, further from the "sphere of fire" which ancient and medieval cosmology placed above the air.</note> than in the center of its own sphere—is overcome by the coldness of the water, this middle substance is converted into the opposing extreme by the victor. Thus, fire (as nature ordains) is frustrated in its effort: while it strives to consume one thing, it generates another from it, which in turn returns to that from which it was originally produced.

This **circulation of transformation** is the primary cause of generation <note>original: "generationis." The process by which new life or new substances are brought into being.</note>. We see something similar when fire acts upon bodies composed by nature. Let wood be an example: when it is brought to the fire, its **sulfur** <note>original: "sulphur." In alchemy, sulfur represents the principle of combustibility—the part of a substance that is able to burn.</note> does not catch flame until all the watery moisture has been converted into air; however, once this moisture is consumed, likewise...

<meta>catchword: fire</meta>

<summary>Dorn defines matter as the elemental realm, arguing that the four elements are ultimately derived from the interaction of Fire and Water. He explains that the constant "circulation" or recycling of these elements is the fundamental process that allows for the creation of all physical things.</summary>
<keywords>matter, elements, fire, water, air, earth, circulation, generation, sulfur, alchemy, transformation</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues Gerard Dorn's discussion on the transformation of elements, specifically focusing on the behavior of fire. He argues that nothing in nature is truly "lost" or consumed to the point of non-existence, but rather changes form in a continuous cycle, setting the stage for his defense of the "quintessence."</meta>

# BOOK ONE.
### A vj

...fire passes into air, and finally into itself, while the more solid parts of the wood fall away into ash or earth: therefore it must not be said that the elements of such wood have been consumed, but rather that, separated from their composite state, each has returned to its own sphere. Concerning this matter, a doubtful question was raised among philosophers: whether fire consumes more than it generates? It seems sound to say that the operations of ethereal fire <note>original: "ignis ætherei." This refers to the celestial fire of the sun and stars, which was believed to drive the generation of life and matter on Earth.</note> and elemental fire <note>original: "ignis elementaris." This refers to terrestrial fire, such as a hearth flame, which breaks substances down.</note> are equal, because if more were consumed by elemental fire than generated by ethereal fire, a vacuum could be designated in the nature of things: which would be entirely absurd. If indeed ethereal fire generated more than elemental fire consumed, the surplus could not be contained in its already determined place: thus the belly <note>original: "aluum." This suggests the "womb" or "internal cavity" of the natural world.</note> of nature would necessarily burst. 

Briefly, however, it shall be answered that nothing is consumed by fire, but by way of transformation, that which leaves one element enters another. Thus, wise Nature carefully provides for new forms to be awakened daily from elements—mutually transformed in this way—and from Form itself: so that what was deformed through corruption might be reformed through generation; and let the operation of nature be circular, just as are the celestial motions. 

Most excessively curious natural philosophers <note>original: "physiologi." In the 16th century, this referred to those who studied the "physics" or internal workings of nature.</note>, not yet experienced in practical things, are accustomed to thrust this argument upon us, by which they attack the <term>quintessence</term> <note>original: "quintam essentiam." Literally the "fifth essence," a purified substance beyond the four elements, central to alchemical medicine.</note> of the chemists; truly, they will not overcome it, unless they bring more valid arguments. Bodies (in-
<meta>catchword: (in-</meta>

<summary>Dorn argues that fire does not destroy matter but merely returns its components to their original elemental spheres, maintaining a perfect balance in nature's "circular" operation. He uses this principle of conservation to defend the alchemical concept of the quintessence against skeptical natural philosophers.</summary>
<keywords>conservation of matter, ethereal fire, elemental fire, transformation, nature's cycle, quintessence, alchemical theory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the composition of matter. # OF THE CHYMICAL ART
### Page 12

...they say) natural things consist of four elements, where then will the **fifth element** <note>original: "quintum elementum," often called the Quintessence, a celestial substance distinct from the four terrestrial elements.</note> be? We respond that all natural bodies consist of the four elements together with **Form**, which is also a distinct thing, and indeed the most powerful part of any composite. Nor [do they consist] solely of all these, because a true union cannot occur except through some **bond** <term>vinculum: a spiritual or physical "glue" that allows opposing qualities to stay joined</term>; otherwise, there would only be contact or a clashing together. For the **primary qualities** <note>In Aristotelian physics, these are Hot, Cold, Moist, and Dry.</note>, because of their mutual repugnance, cannot come together into one. For this reason, it was necessary for them to be composed into elements possessing two qualities, so that they might be joined more easily. By this bond, Nature composes all bodies. Philosophers are accustomed to hand this down to us in a riddle, saying:

> Make peace between enemies, and you will have the whole **magistery** <term>magisterium: the mastery of the chymical art or the "Great Work" itself</term>, namely the chymical one.

Concluding, therefore, regarding the elements, we say they are threefold: namely, **Simple**, **Composite**, and **Decomposite**. **Simple** elements are those which are neither corrupted nor undergo composition; it is not *from* these, but *through* these that all other inferior elements exert their powers, which they also receive from them. **Composite** elements are those which are put together and undergo corruption. **Decomposite** elements are those which already meet in composition through corruption. Whoever knows how to convert these—the impure into pure, the composite into simple—will be the greatest
-> chymist, and a good artisan <-
-> of wonders. <-

## On Corruption. CHAPTER V.

Chymical corruption is nothing other than the resolution of a whole composed by nature into its own parts. Indeed, if any composite is resolved, it must necessarily be dissolved into that from which it was first composed. Therefore, natural bodies are resolved into the elements and into the **fifth essence** <note>original: "quintam essentiam," the quintessence or purest part of a substance, distinct from the four earthly elements.</note>, from which they consist. We should not think that this contradicts the opinion of **Paracelsus** <note>The influential Swiss physician and alchemist (1493–1541) who challenged traditional medicine.</note> who said that every natural body can be resolved by art into only three things: namely, into *Salt, Sulfur, and Liquor*; because air is always found mixed with either the liquor or the sulfur. Thus, it is not within the power of the art to enclose pure air, entirely separated from other elements, in any vessel. Although nature leaves nothing empty, but fills it with air composed from the beginning of creation, yet that air is not separated from the other elements by art, but is mixed among them. It is daily evident to the eyes of chymists that nothing can be drawn from bodies by the art of separation except *water, oil, and earth*. And although oil is clearer at the beginning of **distillation** <term>stillatio: the process of heating a substance to capture its vapors and condense them back into liquid</term> than at the end, it cannot be said to be different in nature, but only in its **accidents** <note>In philosophy, "accidents" are non-essential qualities (like color or temperature) that can change without changing what the thing actually is.</note>: that is, it is more burnt at the end than it was at the very beginning.

It remains for us now to say that our [chymical] corruption differs from natural corruption in this: that it pulls individuals away from their species toward their genus. No **artisan** <note>original: "artifex," referring here to the chemist or alchemist working in the laboratory.</note>, once the specific **form** <note>The organizing principle or "soul" of a substance that gives it its specific identity.</note> of things has been destroyed, will be able to introduce that same form back into that matter. Nature, howe...

<summary>This page defines "chymical corruption" as the process of breaking down a substance into its fundamental components (elements and quintessence). It reconciles the traditional four-element theory with Paracelsus’s "Tria Prima" (Salt, Sulfur, and Liquor/Mercury) by explaining that air is always mixed into the other parts.</summary>

<keywords>corruption, resolution, Paracelsus, Tria Prima, Salt, Sulfur, Liquor, elements, distillation, form</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 13 regarding the "accidents" or external qualities of matter. It transitions from the process of "corruption" (the breakdown of substances) to "generation" (the formation of new substances), using the philosophical principle that the end of one thing is the beginning of another.</meta>

[...accidents], it can call forth daily from the elements—separated from any bodies in whatever way—forms similar to the previous ones, as well as diverse ones; and with its greatest effort, it strives to expand genera into species for the preservation of individuals. But because the corruption of one is the generation of another (as the **Academicians** <note>original: "Academici," referring here to Scholastic philosophers or followers of Aristotle.</note> say), we shall rightly speak of generation.

### *On Generation. CHAPTER VI.*

**Generation** <term>generatio: in this context, the alchemical process of a new substance coming into being</term> is said to be the hiding of the manifest and, soon after, the manifestation of the hidden. For all bodies are enclosed by three dimensions: namely, manifest **Height**, hidden **Depth**, and **Breadth** which is the medium between these. The **Philosophers** <note>A common term for ancient alchemists and sages.</note> hand this down to us secretly in these words: *There is no passage from one extreme to another, except through a medium* <note>original: "Non sit transitus de extremo ad extremum, nisi per medium."</note>. This means that from the manifest form of any body, the part hidden from us cannot become known except through the corruption of the manifest: by which the property of the hidden is revealed. This will be demonstrated geometrically, so that it may be better understood physically.

Suppose we are facing one side—or rather the surface—of some solid geometric body, such as the one designated by the letter **A**. It is placed in such a position that the remaining planes are not entirely visible. We can measure the length of that surface which is manifest or facing us, which let us suppose to be 5 feet. Such also is the height of the remaining surfaces opposite to each other, **b**, **c**, and **f**, whose planes are not...

In a very similar way, <term>chymists</term> <note>A term for early chemists and alchemists who studied the transformation of matter.</note> are accustomed to investigating the <term>latent forms</term> <note>The hidden, internal qualities or essences of a substance that are not immediately visible.</note> of natural bodies by means of a conversion—namely, the turning of manifest forms into hidden ones. This conversion occurs through the <term>corruption</term> <note>In alchemical terms, "corruption" is the breaking down or "death" of a substance's current state so a new one can emerge.</note> of a specific form and the <term>generation</term> <note>The creation or "birth" of a new substance.</note> of a more general form: specifically, the conversion of decomposed elements into compounds, and of impure things into pure.

### On the Excellence of the Ternary. CHAPTER VII.

By the sacred **Ternary** <note>The number three.</note>, the entire world was constructed in order, number, and measure through **Unity**. For **Unity** is not a number, but a covenant of harmony. The **Binary** <note>The number two.</note>, however, is the first number and the fountain and origin of discord; it was cut off from Unity through the taking on of matter, and it cannot be joined back into one again except by a bond that is most indissoluble: which is only the "One." Truly, he labors in vain who strives to couple equals with equals, for equals come together into one only by means of an odd number. **God delights in an odd number.** <note>original: "Numero Deus impare gaudet" — a famous line from Virgil’s Eclogues, often used by alchemists to suggest that odd numbers (like 3 or 7) possess divine or creative power.</note> Therefore, the Unary overcomes and animates the Binary (that is, one and one) through the simplicity of unity, turning it into a Ternary with a bond that is indeed unbreakable; for "one" cannot be divided into any parts. For one and one cannot be joined by two, since the two which ought to bind—

### On the Elementary World. CHAPTER VIII.

The **elementary world** <note>The physical realm composed of the four elements: Earth, Air, Fire, and Water.</note> is perceived to consist chiefly of three parts: **Minerals**, **Vegetables** <note>A historical term for all plant life.</note>, and **Animals**. Aside from these, there is nothing in this world (perceptible to the senses) that we are able to find. Therefore, making my start from minerals, as the foundation for the other parts, I shall proceed to the remaining material bodies. 

**Earth** is indeed the foundation and the vessel of all things, both of heavenly influences and of solid bodies. All celestial movements occur around its center. All heavy things are drawn toward its center, unless they are held back by the solid mass of the earth. And from its center, all light things flee <note>This reflects the Aristotelian view of the universe, where "heavy" elements like earth move toward the center of the world, and "light" elements like fire move away from it.</note>. As **Hermes Trismegistus** <note>The legendary author of the Hermetic Corpus, considered the founding father of alchemy.</note> says in that most secret of secrets (which they call the **Emerald Tablet** <note>A foundational alchemical text said to contain the secret of the "one substance."</note>):

> The Earth is the nurse. <note>original: *Terra nutrix*</note>

And a little further below:

> It ascends from the earth into heaven, and again descends into the earth: thus it receives the power of superior and inferior things.

In the same place...

### On the Elementary World. CHAPTER VIII.

The elementary world is seen to consist chiefly of three parts: **Minerals**, **Vegetables** <note>Refers to the plant kingdom.</note>, and **Animals**. ### *On Minerals. CHAPTER IX.*

**Minerals** are those things which are found hardened into various forms and species within the bowels of the earth, through the mixture of earth and water according to their different proportions. Of these there are three kinds: namely, **stones**, **metals**, and **intermediate minerals**. <note>Intermediate minerals (*media mineralia*) often included substances like salts, vitriols, and alums that shared properties of both stones and metals.</note> And although all these are generated from the proportional mixture of earth and water, they nonetheless consist of the four elements, just as plants and animals do: for it was said above in chapter five that every liquid and sulfur <note>term: <term>sulphur</term>In this context, sulfur refers to the "combustible principle," one of the three prime components of matter in alchemical theory.</note> contain hidden air within them.

**Stones** are fashioned by nature from much earth and little water, to a greater or lesser degree; therefore, they are not **fusible** <note>Capable of being melted.</note>, but rather **friable** <note>Easily crumbled or reduced to powder.</note> in fire. That some turn out clearer and more transparent than others happens because of a greater purification and clarification of the earth performed by nature: since we see even by art that the driest ashes can pass into transparent **glass**, yet art, even with the most careful emulation, cannot imitate the smallest work that was most easy for nature. # BOOK ONE

[...me-]tals, <note>Completing the word "metalla" from the previous page.</note> indeed, on the contrary, are forged from much water transmuted into **quicksilver** <note>original: "argentum vivum." In alchemy, this is the "feminine," cold, and fluid principle of matter.</note> and a little earth converted by nature into **sulfur**: <note>The "masculine," hot, and combustible principle.</note> which is why they are liquefiable rather than brittle. By long-lasting decoction <note>A process of slow heating or "cooking" within the earth's crust.</note> the water is thickened, provided it does not evaporate; on the other hand, the earth becomes clear: for hidden things are contrary to manifest things, and these to those, always and in all things.

The metallic principles differ in a threefold state: they are either *most remote*, such as the mixture of earth with water; or *remote*, such as **vitriol**; <term>vitriol: a glassy substance or sulfate mineral, considered an intermediate stage in metal formation.</term> or they are *immediate*, such as **quicksilver** and **sulfur**. From these last two, depending on the purity of both, or the impurity of one or the other, or of both, nature is said to generate **metal**—whether noble <note>Such as gold or silver.</note> or less noble, or entirely base—by subterranean heat, over a span of perhaps thousands of years.

Thus it happens that the longer minerals have been under the obedience of **celestial influences**, <note>The belief that planets and stars radiated "influences" that governed the growth of specific metals (e.g., the Sun for gold, Venus for copper).</note> the more they are rightly thought to have acquired most effective celestial virtues—possessing a firmer and stronger power to restore and preserve bodily strengths among those things that have physical power—and they are considered more noble to the extent that their metallic form is less overwhelmed by its matter.

The general form of metals is the same for all, but due to the varied disposition of the matter (as was said above in the second chapter), it breaks out into various specific forms. But if the universal form is joined to matter arranged with a balanced proportion of parts, [it results] in the most noble species of all, and in...

# THE CHEMICAL ART

### [OF GOLD AND SILVER]

...gold must break forth: for the place now contains equal parts: nature also knows how to apply a proportionate heat, as an instrument. Gold, therefore, is no colder than its heat, nor hotter than its cold; likewise, it is no drier than its moisture, nor more moist than its dryness, but is in every way tempered, unified, and compacted by nature with such perfection that it is thought to yield to no artificial fire whatsoever. 

Yet by natural fire, the philosophers say, it can be resolved into its "first matter"; <note>The primary, undifferentiated substance from which all things are made.</note> unless it is so resolved, it would be impossible for it to be digested in the human stomach. It is said not to be burned by even the most vehement fire—which overcomes other metals and consumes stones—but rather to be rendered purer and more perfect: until it reaches the highest degree of its perfection; then, the philosophers report, it rejoices in the fire like a **salamander**. <note>In alchemy, the salamander is a mythical lizard-like creature capable of living within fire without being consumed, representing the "sulfur" that survives the furnace.</note> I believe they [the philosophers] mean not common coinage, but gold so purified by **antimony** <note>A brittle, silvery-white metalloid used by alchemists to "eat" or strip away base metals from gold during the refining process.</note> that no other mixed metal remains within it. 

Therefore, all those who attempt to make **drinkable gold** <note>original: "aurum potabile." A legendary medicinal elixir sought after for its supposed life-extending and curative powers.</note> (as **Marsilio Ficino** <note>1433–1499; an influential Italian scholar and priest who led the revival of Neoplatonism and wrote extensively on medicine and the "spirit" of the world.</note> says) seem to labor in vain, unless they first resolve the gold into its first matter—that is, into **quicksilver** <note>original: "argentum vivum," or mercury.</note> and **sulfur**: which is the first, closest, and most immediate matter of all metals. 

This, then, is the conclusion regarding gold: that by its perfection and its likeness to the celestial Sun, it has been granted the name of the **earthly Sun** by the philosophers; who also [call] pure silver the **earthly Moon**...

...they have named [silver] (which goldsmiths call **cupelled**) <note>Refining in a cupel, a shallow, porous dish, allows precious metals to be separated from base metals like lead under high heat.</note>: stating it is diminished from the perfection of gold only in its color, consistency, and weight. For every natural body to be perfect, it is required that it neither lacks in anything nor exceeds the limits of perfection: for perfection holds the middle ground in all things. 

Now, the middle between black and white is pale, just as between white and the greater, perfect red is **citrine**. <note>A lemon-yellow or golden-yellow color, representing a specific stage of the alchemical work.</note> Hence it is that gold, holding the middle between white and red, has a citrine color, neither exceeding nor diminished. Citrine is nothing other than a determined and middle proportion of red and white color. 

From this it also happens that all those who think silver and the other diminished, common metals can be **transmuted** into gold by means of gold itself are deceived, as are those who listen to them willingly. For gold has no more color than it needs to color its own quicksilver. Experience has taught learned men that a **medicine from gold** <note>original: "medicina ex auro." <meta>This page continues the argument from page 21 regarding the nature of gold. The previous page ended with the syllable "Fo-", which here completes the phrase "fieri factum est" (it was made to happen/produced).</meta>

->ARTIS CHYMISTICAE<-
->THE CHYMICAL ART<-

...it was produced by this art, more gold than the weight of that gold from which such a **medicine** <note>original: "medicina." In an alchemical context, this refers to the Elixir or the "Philosopher's Stone," a substance capable of "curing" imperfect metals and turning them into gold.</note> was extracted. Therefore, the advice of those who suggest that one should seek in any place that which is not naturally contained within it seems foolish: such as looking for an overabundant color in gold, or a superfluous weight, which it might bestow upon other metals. Even if gold possessed such things, it would no longer be gold, but something more deeply colored than gold, and not a perfect metal.

But if this perfection of **transmutation** <note>The alchemical process of changing one element or metal into another.</note> is not found in gold for the reasons already stated, it will certainly not be found in any other mineral; for that which was not found in a state of perfection in a perfect thing should not be sought in something more imperfect. Nor will it be found in **vegetables**, <note>Plant-based matter.</note> and even less so in **animals**, which by their nature are more distant from gold and are less suitable for the generation of gold than gold itself. For **nature rejoices in a nature similar to itself**, <note>original: "natura quoque sibi simili natura gaudet." A core alchemical principle stating that like affects like and that substances only interact with things of a similar nature.</note> and is transmuted into such a nature more easily than into a foreign one. 

However, since the highest perfection—or a perfect medicine—is required for the transmutation of an imperfect body, this cannot be elicited from any source more closely than from that which is supremely perfect and of its own nature. Indeed, the most potent and most related things are always to be chosen in all matters to ensure an easier transition. Furthermore, since every perfection of bodies depends upon the **temperament** <term>Temperamentum: The balanced proportion of the four primary qualities (hot, cold, moist, and dry) that determines the nature of a substance.</term> of their constituent parts, nothing will be more tempered than gold, or more closely related to this work. For this reason, this perfection of transmutation must be sought from gold, or from nothing at all. Without a doubt, those philosophical men [sought] no [gold...]

<meta>catchword: fossilis (of a mineral/fossil)</meta>

<summary>This page argues that the "medicine" for transmuting metals must be derived from gold itself, as nature only acts upon things of a similar nature. It dismisses the possibility of finding the secret of transmutation in plants, animals, or inferior minerals.</summary>

<keywords>transmutation, medicine, elixir, temperament, nature rejoices in nature, perfection of gold, alchemy, minerals</keywords>

# BOOK ONE.

...be found in natural, or common gold. Those who are greedy or thirsty for gold describe transmutations not of common minerals or metals, but of their own "philosophical" ones. These philosophers, caring for their own age and for posterity, have attempted with great effort—by considering the **temperament** <note>The specific balance of the four primary qualities: hot, cold, moist, and dry. In alchemy, a "perfect" temperament means a perfectly balanced substance.</note> of natural gold—to discover the temperament of artificial gold. This they have extracted not only from one body, but from various sources, including vegetables, minerals, and animals. 

Therefore, the **gold of the philosophers** is a certain tempered **medicine** <note>In alchemy, a "medicine" (medicina) refers to the Elixir or the Philosopher's Stone, which was believed to "heal" base metals of their imperfections to turn them into gold.</note> compiled by art. It is like a "form" separated from its body and reduced to such a degree of subtlety that it acts upon any object presented to it—much like a heavenly influence—within the matter prepared for it by nature. 

Indeed, a universal form, once it has taken on a specific character, retains an active power even when it is separated from an individual thing. It can then impress this power onto another foreign body through the virtue of unity, whose forces it has already assumed; it then makes all things one with itself. This remains true even if this medicine was drawn from an individual that was not itself perfectly tempered. For by art, the elements of any and all things can be reduced to a single, balanced temperament. 

Now we must speak of the remaining lesser metals, such as **Tin**, which the philosophers dedicated to **Jupiter** because it is hot and moist; **Copper** or bronze <note>original: "Cupro vel aëre." In Latin, *aes* can refer to copper, bronze, or brass.</note>, which they dedicated to **Venus**, being cold (though slightly warm) and moist; **Lead**, which they dedicated to **Saturn**, being cold and dry; and **Iron**, which they dedicated to **Mars**, being hot and dry, due to the similarity of these stated qualities. These likewise...

<summary>This page defines "Philosophical Gold" as a highly refined alchemical medicine or Elixir that can unify and balance different elements. It also lists the seven metals and their corresponding planetary qualities according to Aristotelian physics.</summary>
<keywords>Philosophical Gold, Medicine, Alchemy, Elixir, Temperament, Jupiter, Venus, Saturn, Mars, Tin, Copper, Lead, Iron, Four Qualities</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "Philosophers' Gold" and the "medicine" (the Elixir) from the previous page. # OF THE CHEMICAL ART

...the four **complexions** <note>In historical medicine and alchemy, a "complexion" (or temperament) refers to the specific balance of the four primary qualities: hot, cold, moist, and dry.</note>, not the **humors** <note>The four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) used in traditional medicine to explain human health.</note>, in the **elemented body** <note>The "elemented body" refers to the elemental composition (earth, air, fire, water) of a substance, rather than the "physical" or anatomical body studied by traditional physicians.</note> rather than the physical body of physicians, they seem to fit together quite neatly because of the aforementioned qualities. Therefore, whenever the **Choleric**, **Melancholic**, **Phlegmatic**, or **Sanguine** states could be reduced to a balanced state by a perfectly tempered **medicine** <note>In this context, "medicine" refers to the Philosopher's Stone or Elixir, which was believed to "cure" base metals of their imbalances to make them gold.</note>, the Philosophers considered that **Copper**, **Iron**, **Tin**, or **Lead** had been transmuted into gold.

Thus, under the names of common metals, the wise ones hid their medicines, physical secrets, and philosophies. They even prepared these from common metals for the transmutation of their own—that is, the **philosophical metals**—so that by using such **equivocation** <note>The use of ambiguous language or code-names (like using "Lead" to mean a specific alchemical state) to hide secrets from the uninitiated.</note>, such a great treasure of medicine might remain hidden from the ignorant masses. 

Meanwhile, even learned physicians, who made no small profession of philosophy, were scarcely able to understand this; until **Aureolus Theophrastus Paracelsus** <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), known as Paracelsus, was a Swiss physician and alchemist who revolutionized medicine by emphasizing chemistry over traditional Greek humorism.</note>, a Doctor of both medicines, a Swiss and noble man, of a genius more divine than human, uncovered the whole matter. He did so through long travels and through the company of learned men in every place; he then published it in countless books for the favor of learned men and for the common benefit, writing primarily in **German** <note>Paracelsus was famous for writing his medical and alchemical treatises in the vernacular German of the people, rather than the scholarly Latin, to make knowledge more accessible.</note>, so that he might also provide for those who were unskilled in the Latin tongue. 

Following the generosity of this teacher, I have wished to treat this matter perhaps more openly; for I would desire others to profit as much through me as I have through

<meta>catchword: itself/himself.</meta>

<summary>This page explains that the transmutation of base metals into gold is analogous to balancing the four temperaments in medicine and credits Paracelsus with revealing these alchemical secrets to the public in the German language.</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsus, complexions, humors, base metals, transmutation, philosophical metals, medicine, alchemy, German language, elements</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on how philosophers used common metal names as codes for their medicines. It transitions from the "perfectly tempered" medicines to a classification of materials into "extremes" (metals and stones) and "intermediates" (marcasites and minerals), concluding with the beginning of a chapter on the plant kingdom.</meta>

->**BOOK ONE**<-

...itself. Now it remains for me to speak about the **intermediate minerals** <note>Substances that fall between the categories of "stone" and "metal."</note> and their corresponding extremes, such as all the **marcasites** <term>marchasitæ: a general term in early chemistry for metallic sulfides, such as iron pyrite or "fool’s gold."</term>. There are found golden marcasites, as well as silver, tin, and lead varieties (this last one I believe to be **antimony** <note>original: "antimonium vel Stibium." At this time, these terms referred to the mineral stibnite, used both in medicine and to purify gold.</note>), which I have seen; the others are still unknown to me, such as the copper and iron ones. There are also **magnesia** <note>In this context, likely manganese ore or a form of talc, rather than modern magnesium.</note>, **tutty** <term>tucia: zinc oxide collected from the flues of smelting furnaces, used in salves and brass-making.</term>, **orpiment** <term>auripigmentum: literally "gold pigment," a yellow arsenic sulfide used as a pigment and in alchemy.</term>, **arsenic**, many salts, **alums** <note>A group of hydrated double sulfates, traditionally used as fixatives for dyes.</note>, **sulfurs**, and others of that kind. These are more effective for **physical transmutation** <note>The actual chemical changing of one substance into another.</note> than for that common metamorphosis which, if not unknown to philosophers, is at least neglected.

For this reason, they are called "intermediate" minerals, because they do not entirely resemble a metal, nor do they resemble a stone in every respect, but they have a great deal of affinity with both. Concerning the other of the extremes—namely, stone—it would be superfluous to treat it specifically, since this has been discussed abundantly enough by **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), author of *Natural History*.</note>, **Albertus Magnus** <note>A 13th-century scholar and bishop known for his extensive writings on minerals and alchemy.</note>, and other most approved authors.

It only befits my purpose to state the difference between a metal and a stone: a stone neither melts nor is it **ductile** <note>Able to be hammered thin or drawn into wire without breaking.</note> under a hammer; a metal, however, is workable under the hammer and can be liquefied by fire. The intermediate substances, however, do melt, but they cannot be drawn out; instead, they shatter into fragments. In this shattering, they resemble stone, but in their melting, they resemble metal.

### Of Vegetables. CHAPTER X.

Those things are called **vegetables** <note>In the language of the time, "vegetables" referred to the entire plant kingdom, including trees and herbs.</note> which take their nourishment from the earth through a fixed root. These are of three kinds: namely, herbs, which are vegetables, or...

## THE CHYMICAL ART

...within a year or a little more, not being preserved in the earth; instead, once the leaves and stalks have dried, they are propagated by nature through the decay of the seed cast into the earth. **Arboreal vegetables** <note>Trees</note> are the largest, which rise up through solid roots into solid trunks and branches, and are preserved for many years. **Shrub-like vegetables** <term>Plantalia: a category of plants intermediate between herbs and trees, such as bushes or woody perennials.</term> are the middle ground between these, larger than herbs but much smaller than trees, yet reflecting the form of both: they stay green and are preserved longer than herbs, but indeed perish sooner than trees. 

But because these things are known to all **philosophers** <note>In this context, the author refers to natural philosophers or early scientists.</note>, however mediocre, I thought they should be passed over briefly; noting only this: just as they provide nourishment to living beings, they likewise provide a **medicine** <term>medicamentum: a substance used for healing or chemical transformation.</term> once they have been purified by art from their corrupting matter. For the power of the [essential] form is hindered by the matter that restrains its action. It will be better to separate this [essence] outside the stomach, rather than in the stomach of the sick person, which is already too weak to perform this separation. 

Truly, if they are meant to be **nourishment** <term>alimentum</term>, they must be administered with the body [of the plant]: for the more general parts of the body—such as the natural liquid, **sulfur**, and **balsam** <note>In Paracelsian chemistry, "balsam" refers to a preservative internal liquid that protects the body from decay.</note>—each must be nourished by its like. For excesses and deficiencies causing an imbalance <note>original: "intemperiem," referring to the humoral theory where health is a balance of heat, cold, moisture, and dryness.</note> must be reduced to equality by their contrary—that is, by a tempered substance. This must be most diligently considered by the physician, both in preparing vegetable medicines and those drawn from the parts of animals.

->BOOK ONE.<-
<sig>B 2</sig>

### On Animals. CHAPTER 11.

**Living beings** <note>original: *Animantia*</note> are bodies which, being moved naturally by their own power, take in nourishment through a mouth. **Chymists** <note>The author uses this term for early modern scientists who combined chemistry and alchemy.</note> not without reason consider motion in natural things to be threefold: **Corrupting**, **generating**, and **animating**. **Philosophers** state that motion occurs either by its own nature or by accident. Motion that occurs by its own nature is natural: it is either **causing**, such as the motion of the **first mover** <term>primum mobile: in the ancient world-view, this was the outermost sphere of the heavens that set all other spheres in motion</term> and the **Zodiac**, or it is **caused**, such as the motion of things toward their birth and decay. 

Motion which occurs by accident, or by change of place, is either **rectilinear** <note>moving in a straight line</note> or **curvilinear** <note>moving in a curve</note>. Rectilinear motion is perpendicular, oblique, or transverse. Perpendicular motion is carried either upward or downward. Curvilinear motion is either circular or winding. Circular motion is revolving or rotating. The knowledge of these motions is not useless in this art <note>the "Art" refers to Alchemy or Chymistry</note>, but because these matters belong to the Philosopher and the **Geometer**—which a chymist is also required to be—let what has been said of them so far suffice. 

Animals have this in common with vegetables: both take in nourishment for their growth—the former through a mouth, the latter through a root—though plants are in turn inferior to animals in terms of their senses. There are three types of living beings: **creeping things** <note>original: *reptilium*. In this period, this category included any animal that crawls on the earth, not just modern reptiles.</note> on the land, **flying things** in the air, and **swimming things** beneath the waters. In **brute animals** <note>Non-human animals.</note>, the motion of the soul is twofold: **vegetative** <note>the biological drive for growth and nutrition</note> and **sensitive** <note>the ability to perceive and move</note>. In the **human soul**, the motion is held to be threefold, for to the two previous types is added the motion of the **rational soul**, which is eternal.

<vocab>Living beings, motion, Chymists, Philosophers, Zodiac, rectilinear, curvilinear, vegetative, sensitive, rational</vocab>

<summary>This page defines animals as self-moving bodies that consume food via a mouth and classifies the different types of motion—natural, accidental, and geometric—that a chymist must understand. It further distinguishes between plants, animals, and humans based on the complexity of their "soul" or animating force.</summary>

## SECOND BOOK.

### *On Chymical practice* CHAPTER I.

After the **chymical art** <note>original: *chymistica arte*; referring to early chemistry and alchemy</note> has been treated theoretically, as far as is right, it now remains to prove by experiment, or by the senses, what the chymist can attain through imagination or the intellect. The **practice** <note>original: *praxis*</note> of chymists is a demonstration made perceptible to the senses from hidden things—specifically those things which the **Philosopher** <note>Referring to the natural philosopher or the idealized scientist of the era</note> attained regarding the latent forms of natural things through certain and known reasons of philosophy and the intellect. For I do not think one should rely so heavily on the writings of authors alone that, if experiment teaches something better, it should not be preferred over the opinions of those who often happen to assert and judge matters not thoroughly examined, which are inconsistent even with themselves.

The **chymical artifice** <note>The practical skill or craft of the chemist</note> revolves chiefly around three things: namely, the **place**, the **instrument**, and the **operation**. The **place** is twofold: specifically, that of the **furnaces**, and of the **vessels** containing the matter itself. The **instrument** is also double, such as the **corrupting fire** <term>ignis corrumpens: fire used to break down or decompose substances</term>, and the **generating fire** <term>ignis generans: a gentle, creative heat used to foster growth or transformation</term>. Likewise, the **operation** is double: **Solution** <note>original: *Solutio*; the process of dissolving or breaking down</note> and **congelation** <note>original: *congelatio*; the process of thickening or solidifying</note>.

---

### *On the Chymical place.* CHAPTER II.

The **furnace** itself, or the **vessel** containing the matter, is considered by chymists to be the proper **place**. Furnaces vary according to different craftsmen, and vessels for each person's...

...formed in various ways according to one's judgment. But according to the three perpendicular motions—namely upwards, downwards, and sideways—we shall first set forth a triple and unique furnace, never seen before. In this furnace, three different operations can be performed using only a single **corrupting fire** <term>ignis corrupens: a high-heat fire used to break down or decompose substances</term>. Next, we shall describe another (which they have called the *accidia* <note>original: *accidiae*; likely referring to a "lazy furnace" or Athanor, designed to maintain heat for long periods with little attention</note>); with this one, four operations are also completed, likewise using a single **generating fire** <term>ignis generans: a gentle, steady heat used to foster chemical "growth" or slow transformation</term>.

### *On the construction of the triple furnace.* CAP. III.

It will be worthwhile to demonstrate the structure of this excellent furnace before arriving at the operations themselves. Let two walls be erected, built of bricks laid flat, separated from each other by the distance of a cubit <term>cubit: an ancient unit of measure, roughly 18 inches</term>, a foot and a half high and of the same width, such as can be seen marked with the letter B. From one wall to the other, let four iron bars be stretched across, by which the bricks forming the first floor <term>solarium: here referring to a horizontal structural platform or partition within the furnace</term> are supported, which is designated by the letter R. In the middle of the said floor, let an open, round hole be left, four fingers wide, through which the neck of an inverted earthenware **courd** <term>cucurbita: a gourd-shaped glass or ceramic vessel used in distillation</term>—which lacks a bottom and has a hole in its base at the neck—may project downwards, marked by the letter E. This can be closed with a pointed lid, marked with the letter F. Upon this base, three walls [should be built], either two opposite ones with the bricks erected on their sides, or [bricks] lying...

After the cucurbit has been placed in the furnace, the side that had remained open for inserting the cucurbit can be closed up, leaving one or two holes—or even more—through which the coals <note>original: *carbones*</note> can be stirred with an iron rod. Upon these walls, four other iron bars are again to be placed, upon which bricks may form a base with a round hole in the middle through which the coals can be fed; then, that hole must be closed with a removable brick. ### Regarding the construction of another furnace, which they call sloth. CHAPTER 4.

Four bricks are to be set upright on their sides in a square, positioned so they do not touch one another, leaving a gap of four finger-widths. However, on one side, two fragments of brick will suffice, placed at the corners so that a small opening <term>hostiolo: a small door or aperture</term> is left between them through which the ashes can be removed. Atop this structure, we shall place iron rods in the form of a grate <term>craticulæ: a grid or iron grating</term>; in this way, we will have built the first chamber, marked with the letter **A**. Above this grate, we will build similar walls, but twice as high—that is, two stones tall—leaving a small door through which the coals can be stirred with an iron tool; this is marked with the letter **B**. The four corners of the lower chamber must be closed off, but those of the upper chamber <term>concamerationis: a vaulted chamber or partitioned space</term> are to be left open; through these apertures, the heat may flow into the side furnaces. These four little doors shall be closed by iron plates <note>original: "spatulæ"</note>, marked with the letters **A A**, which can be raised and lowered. Above this furnace, we shall erect a small tower <term>turriculam: a tower-like structure, often called an athanor</term> <note>The "acedia" or "slothful" furnace was designed to burn for long periods without constant attention, as the charcoal in the tower would automatically drop onto the grate as the bottom layer was consumed.</note> made of earthenware, or preferably copper, being wider at the base than at the top, marked with the letter **C**. This may be closed with an earthen lid marked **D** once the tower has been filled to the top with charcoal. Within the upper chamber, we shall place several burning coals, which—

# BOOK TWO.

### *On vessels.* CHAPTER V.

I consider vessels not to be mere chemical tools, but rather to hold the second place of importance after furnaces, regarding the matter and substance of the work. This is no different from how nature utilizes the globe of the earth and its caverns as vessels for the <term>decoction</term> <note>original: "decoctione"; the process of heating or boiling a substance to extract its essence or ripen it</note> or the generation of minerals. It is the same in animals, where the <term>matrix</term> <note>original: "matrice"; a place where something is generated or "mother-earth"</note> acts as a vessel, and the uterus serves as a furnace. These chemical vessels are made partly of glass or the most compact potter's clay, and partly of copper or other purer metals. Their forms can be seen above, where we have illustrated the furnaces.

### *On the Chemists' instrument.* CHAPTER VI.

The proper instrument of chemists, as it is for nature, is fire. This is considered in three ways by practitioners. Fire is either external or internal. External fire is either violent, or tempered through three degrees, or very mild and "nature-like," such as nature itself uses in the generation of things. Internal fire is specific to every substance and is naturally innate; the chemist must take this into account as well. 

Chemists use violent fire during <term>calcination</term> <note>original: "calcinandum"; reducing a substance to a powder through intense heat</note>, or for <term>subliming</term> <note>original: "sublimandum"; heating a solid until it becomes a vapor and then solidifies again</note> drier substances, or for melting metals. They use tempered fire by degrees during distillation, both by ascent <note>rising vapors</note> and by descent <note>distilling downwards</note>. They use fire similar to nature's heat while they <term>putrefy</term> <note>breaking down organic matter through warmth and moisture</note>, <term>digest</term> <note>applying steady, gentle heat over a long period to mature a substance</note>, congeal, or <term>fix</term> <note>making a volatile substance stable so it can resist fire</note> through circulation. These operations are defined in the following chapter.

<sig>B vii</sig>

It defines the primary actions of the chemist—dissolution and coagulation—and explains how they function in processes like distillation and sublimation.</meta>

->OF THE CHEMICAL ART<-
->*On operation.* CHAPTER VII.<-

<meta>The catchword 'lare' at the bottom of the page corresponds to the final syllables of the word 'appellare' (to call), which is the last word of this page's text.</meta>

Operation is defined as the application of hands and instruments for the execution of a chemical matter. The chemical work is twofold: namely, **dissolution** and **coagulation**. The dissolution of a body is <term>calcination</term> <note>original: "calcinatio"; the process of reducing a solid substance to a powder or ash through intense heat</note>. Coagulation is the condensation of a spirit that has been driven into a vapor.

Dissolution is typically performed in various ways according to the skill and judgment of the practitioner: either by itself, or together with its companion process—namely, coagulation. It is performed by itself when a body is placed within an earthenware vessel and, through the unrestrained exhalation of its internal spirit, it is crumbled into a very dry powder. This is also often done with the material lying in a pottery jar, constantly stirred, until it is dried into a powder.

Indeed, dissolution and coagulation occur at the same time when a body is placed in an earthen or glass <term>cucurbit</term> <note>original: "cucurbita"; a gourd-shaped vessel used for boiling or distilling</note> and dried, while the vapor is so retained by a glass <term>beaked helm</term> <note>original: "galeam rostratam"; the top part of a still, also known as an alembic, featuring a spout or "beak" to direct runoff</note> that the spirit is released into the air and then coagulated again into a watery body, to be collected in a glass vessel placed below. By this method, the earthy body is dissolved into powder, and the watery part into an aerial spirit—which is then condensed back into a liquid state—all through a single operation. The common people call this "distillation," but the Philosopher properly calls it **separation**.

Dissolution can also occur simultaneously with coagulation by using a closed <term>reverberatory vessel</term> <note>original: "vas reuerberatoriũ"; a vessel designed to reflect heat back onto its contents</note>, consisting of two earthenware vessels glued together with a large amount of <term>lute</term> <note>original: "luto"; a specialized clay or cement used to seal joints and make vessels airtight</note> so that the material cannot escape or "breathe." Using a reverberatory furnace, one might call this process **sublimation**...

**Vocabulary:** Operation, Chemical, Dissolution, coagulation, calcination, distillation, sublimation, cucurbit, reverberatory.

### SECOND BOOK. 39

...they are accustomed to call. There is also another type of dissolution performed by itself, which they call **putrefaction** <note>original: "putrefactionem"; in alchemy, this refers to the breakdown or "rotting" of a substance to release its essential components</note>, when a dry body, having been sprinkled again with much of its own water, is sealed in a double <term>cucurbit</term> <note>original: "cucurbita"; a gourd-shaped boiling flask</note> and placed in a furnace of **sloth** <note>original: "acediæ furno"; likely an Athanor, a furnace designed to maintain a steady, "lazy" heat for long periods without constant attention</note>. But if the body is soaked with only a little water, or at least a lesser amount, it will be congealed by the same artifice; with the dry overcoming the moist, it will then be called **congealing** performed by itself.

Dissolution, which can be called putrefaction, also occurs in a **bain-marie** <note>original: "balneo Mariæ"; literally "Mary’s Bath," a gentle heating method using a container of hot water</note> or sea-bath, in a double vessel suspended over vapor, or immersed in water only up to its middle, yet enclosed on all sides by vapor.

The last and most beautiful type of dissolution performed by itself occurs when the moist part, having been frequently purified through **rectification** <note>original: "rectificatum"; the process of refining a liquid by repeating the distillation process</note> and rarefied, is hidden away in a double glass vessel or a **pelican** <note>original: "pellicano"; a specialized circulatory vessel with two "arms" that return condensing liquid to the body, named for its resemblance to a pelican pecking its breast</note>. With the top part of the vessel standing out from the furnace, it is agitated by a slow fire until no more vapor ascends, but it remains fixed at the bottom. This operation they are accustomed to call **circulation** and the **fixation of the spirit**.

**Rectification** is nothing other (as is clear from above) than the repetition of distillations performed with glass instruments. There are also dissolutions that occur without any fire, such as those of calcined powders, salts, and others, placed upon marble or a glass plate in a very damp cellar, or in a linen 
->**Hippocrates' sleeve** placed with a<-
->vessel beneath to catch<-
->the liquid.<- 
<note>original: "manica Hippocratis"; a conical fabric filter bag used by early chemists and apothecaries to strain liquids</note>

OF THE CHEMICAL ART

# THIRD BOOK.
## *On the Operative Laboratory.* CHAPTER I.

In the preceding treatise, we defined the operations of the chemists; in this one, however, we shall bring those operations into action, insofar as they serve the practice of medicine. For it is not our intention to treat individually those matters which have already been written about by others in the past; rather, we shall strive to propose certain general methods of operation to students, by which they may most easily grasp all the preparations of natural medicines. 

Therefore, by demonstrating the use of a triple furnace, we will teach how to distill the three parts of the world—namely the vegetable, mineral, and animal—and how to prepare them for natural medicine. For the mineral part, we shall take **Roman vitriol** <term>Roman vitriol</term> <note>original: "vitriolum Romanum"; a sulfate mineral, often copper or iron sulfate, used to produce powerful medicinal "oils" or acids</note>, which we will prepare into an oil in the middle **reverberatory furnace** <term>reverberatory furnace</term> <note>original: "fornace reverberatoria"; a furnace designed to reflect or "reverberate" heat from the roof onto the material being processed</note>. With that same fire, wine will be distilled into a **quintessence** <term>quintessence</term> <note>original: "quintam essentiam"; the "fifth essence," considered the purest and most potent extract of a substance, stripped of its grosser elements</note> in the upper furnace. Meanwhile, in the lowest **descending furnace** <term>descending furnace</term> <note>original: "descensoria"; a furnace where the distilled product flows downward into a collector, often used for harder materials</note>, the more solid parts of animals will be turned into oil and water.

Whoever knows how to rightly imitate these three operations from the demonstration we provide, there is nothing that might come into his mind which he should despair of being able to prepare by **natural reason** <note>original: "physica ratione"; a method based on the laws of nature and logical principles of natural philosophy</note>, and not by **empirical trial** <note>original: "empirica"; here referring to "hit-or-miss" experimentation performed without a theoretical understanding of the underlying nature of the materials</note>. I say this so that the difference between natural and empirical preparations may be seen by all. For it befits a truly natural man <note>a physician or natural philosopher</note> to prepare his own substances <note>original: "indiuidua"; specific individual components or materials</note>...

<summary>This page introduces the practical application of alchemy in medicine, outlining a "triple furnace" method to process mineral, vegetable, and animal substances simultaneously. The author emphasizes the importance of working through "natural reason" (scientific principle) rather than "empirical" guesswork.</summary>

(called "Simples" <term>Simples</term> <note>original: "Simplicia"; in pharmacy, a "simple" is a medicine made of only one constituent, usually a single herb or mineral, rather than a compound mixture</note> by the pharmacist) without the mixture or addition of any other thing which might change the nature of the substance being prepared. Finally, he will be able to mix or blend juices or powders of this kind—having first been purified on their own—into a medicine if he so chooses; but the simpler a medicine is, the more potent it will be. Nor should it frighten us that some physicians once wrote that "simple" medicines kill, since they use that term ("simple") differently than chemists <note>original: "chymiſtæ"</note> do. Physicians say Rhubarb <note>original: "Reubararum"</note> is a "simple," but chemists assert it is a compound, which they demonstrate through both art and experiment, not through words alone. 

Empirical practitioners, however, while attempting to imitate physicians without a true knowledge of physics <note>original: "phyſices"; here referring to the study of natural laws and the properties of matter</note>, are for the most part unable to achieve what they desire; for this reason, they are forced to mix raw bodies with other bodies before preparation, and to process them together. I will not, however, deny that chemists mix physical bodies with "Spirits" <term>Spirits</term> <note>original: "Spiritibus"; in alchemy, the volatile, active essence of a substance that can be raised by distillation</note> in a physical manner, so that like may attract like. Once this is done, they discard the physical bodies as being entirely unfit for medicine. They perform a far more excellent work: they separate the spirits mixed in this way from each other through a most subtle path. Now let us come to the matter at hand, and turn vitriol into an oil in such a way that the medicine is both excellent and most simple.

The joints must be protected with strong lute, so they do not breathe <note>meaning "so they do not leak or vent gas"</note>. Next, the fire must be kindled under the gourd-vessel, and even gradually increased through four degrees, each of which should last 2 hours, until it is completely buried in glowing coals. That violent fire must be continued for as long as it takes for the retort, which was previously dark while filled with spirits, to now gradually clear, so that what it contains may be seen within. Once this is observed, one must stop; and when the fire has gone out on its own, the receiving vessel should be separated, and whatever oil <term>oil</term> <note>original: "olei"; in this context, "oil of vitriol" or concentrated sulfuric acid, named for its oily consistency</note> it contains should be removed and stored in a solid glass container,

This oil is as yet known to few, because the ancients concealed the method of its preparation with many riddles and, as a **most secret mystery** <note>original: "arcanum secretissimum"</note>, bound it with the deepest silence. They extol its powers with the highest praise, even above the **quintessence** <term>quintessence</term> <note>original: "quintā essentiam"; the "fifth essence," a purified substance believed to hold the concentrated life-force of a material</note> of wine. Just like that essence, they say this oil draws to itself the powers of all bodies and spirits to which it is joined. For this reason, those who have experienced its general potency usually employ it as a cure for all diseases. Its universal nature is restricted by the specific form of whatever it is added to, such as distilled waters or the oils of substances which **physicians** <note>original: "medici"</note> write can heal illnesses. However, lest I seem to have omitted anything regarding the revelation of this most excellent medicine, I shall recount some of its powers; from these, anyone who is skilled in the medical art...

Its **dosage** is such that you should administer eight drops to the robust, four to those of moderate health, and three to the weak, along with the carriers mentioned before, while ordering the patient to **sweat**, and that they do not eat or drink for four hours afterward, unless it is done because of extreme weakness.

### *On the preparation of the quintessence of wine. CHAPTER III.*

At the end of the serpentine, you shall apply a **glass vessel** to receive the liquid. ### *On the distillation of oils by descent, from animal parts, and more solid materials, such as wood and others*. CHAPTER IV.

You should place the bones of any animal, or fragments of wood—such as guaiacum, juniper and its berries, ivy, ash—and other solid fragments into an inverted earthen **cucurbit** <term>cucurbit</term> <note>a gourd-shaped chemical vessel</note> [marked] **E**, closed with its own cover **F**, which is also earthen and sealed with clay <note>original: "lutatam"; hermetically sealed with a paste of clay and other binders</note>, and buried in ashes so that it cannot be seen; but let the ashes rise two fingers' width above it. With a vessel **D** placed underneath, you may receive the liquid using the same fire from the middle furnace. But if that heat is not intense enough, add more ashes over the cucurbit; if the heat is less than is proper, let the pot be uncovered by removing the ashes with an iron rod. Behold, you now have all the distillations that are required in this **chymical art** <term>chymistica</term>, and with a single furnace you have completed three different operations, even with only a single fire. If you wish, you can build each furnace separately in the same form as you see in this triple furnace.
