# OF THE CHEMICAL ART

Now we shall speak of the **quintessence** <term>quintessence</term> <note>original: "vini quinta essentia"; the "fifth essence," considered the most refined and medicinal core of the wine</note> of wine. Take **rectified** <note>distilled multiple times to increase purity and strength</note> **burning water** <term>burning water</term> <note>original: "aquam ardentem"; high-proof distilled alcohol or spirit of wine</note> and place it in a double vessel [marked] **M**, **N**, filled to the half or third part. Then, once the joints of the vessels have first been sealed with clay, place this **cucurbit** <term>cucurbit</term> <note>original: "cucurbitam"; a gourd-shaped distillation flask</note> into a **bath of Mary** <term>bath of Mary</term> <note>original: "balneo maris"; a water bath used to provide gentle, indirect heat</note>—specifically a copper vessel [marked] **f.** filled with water. Using the heat of the middle furnace, allow the vapors to rise and descend as many times as necessary until they come to rest and that spirit is **fixed** <note>the process of making a volatile substance stable and resistant to heat</note>. Let it no longer fill the **helmet** <term>helmet</term> <note>original: "galeam"; the cap or still-head of an alembic where vapors condense</note> with vapors, but remain at the bottom, wonderfully clear and fragrant, exceeding all sweetness of scent.

In the **second side furnace**, anything requiring a slow fire can be distilled using a **glass cucurbit** **K**, covered with its own beaked and sealed **helmet** **L**, in a **bath of Mary** or a copper cup filled with ashes **T**.

In the **third side furnace** **G**, we can **putrefy** <note>a process of gentle, prolonged heating used to break down or "digest" substances, making their components easier to separate</note> some matter so that it is more obedient to distillation and separation. This is done with a **glass cucurbit** **I**, sealed with **strong clay** (which they call **clay of wisdom** <term>clay of wisdom</term> <note>original: "luto sapientiae"; a specialized alchemical sealant made of clay, hair, or dung designed to be airtight and heat-resistant</note>). It is placed upon three dry **earthen stands** <term>earthen stands</term> <note>original: "pinnacidia terrea"; small ceramic supports or trivets used to elevate a vessel above the floor of the furnace</note> in such a way that it does not touch the bottom, but is held up three or four finger-widths high.

In the **fourth side furnace** **H**, we can either **fix** a few spirits into their "earth" <note>the solid residue of a substance</note>, or **congeal** them, or **dissolve** a small amount of solid matter into a large amount of liquid. This uses a long-necked vial [marked] **O** placed upon **earthen stands**, just as was said before concerning the third furnace. Note this one thing: the third and fourth furnaces employed for these operations should be covered at the top, and al-

### *On the powers of the quintessence in*
### *general. CHAPTER VI.*

**I**t is a wonder and quite incredible to the common people that the **spirit of wine** <term>spirit of wine</term> <note>high-proof distilled alcohol</note>, once drawn from its body and entirely separated, has the power to extract any other spirits from their own bodies—whether vegetable, mineral, or animal—through simple **infusion** <term>infusion</term> <note>the process of soaking a substance in a liquid to extract its medicinal or chemical properties</note> alone. However, the chemical philosophers hold nothing to be more true or more proven by experiment than this: that active forces, when separated from their passive parts, act upon any compound. Through the sharpness of the acting spirit, they dissolve that compound and separate the spirit from it by **symbolization** <term>symbolization</term> <note>original: "symbolisationem"; the alchemical principle of "sympathy," where substances with similar internal natures or "symbols" are naturally attracted to one another</note>. This occurs because like is attracted to like when unimpeded, and a spirit naturally desires to be freed from its bodily chains and return to its origin, or to be joined to something similar to itself. Wherefore...

My purpose here is not to teach medicine, but the chemical art, for those who take pleasure in better arts than those which have been handed down by some regarding the preparation of medicines.

### *On the simple distillation of metals.*
### CHAPTER VII.

**A**lmost all chemists teach that metals are to be distilled, some using vinegar distilled many times, or a sharp **lye** <term>lye</term> <note>original: "lixiuio"; a strongly alkaline solution used as a solvent</note>; most use waters or juices drawn from sharpening agents. But I will now show a method for distilling any metals without any mixture, but by themselves—a method which I believe has been handed down by no one until now, or at least if it has, I do not remember reading it anywhere. Every metal that is consumed by fire will be distilled by evaporation, if...

From there, it will be sent down into a receiving vessel placed below to catch the liquid, leaving the body of the metal brittle and crumbly.

That metal is consumed by evaporation is witnessed by those who cast bells and **war engines** <note>original: "tormentorum bellicorum"; referring here to cannons and heavy artillery</note>. For after the metal has been melted, if they wait too long before pouring it, the weight is sometimes so diminished that it does not suffice for even half the work—even though it would have overflowed the mold if it had been poured immediately. This method of distilling metals is not to be looked down upon; rather, it should be noted most diligently, because it can sometimes be used for another, better work, if you are wise <note>original: "si sapis"; a common alchemical phrase hinting at deeper hermetic secrets or the "Great Work"</note>.

### On the distillation of those things which abound most in juice. CHAPTER VIII.

**A**bove, we spoke of how dry and less succulent things ought to be distilled by **descent** <term>descent</term> <note>original: "descensum"; a method where the heat is applied from above and the distilled liquid flows downward into a receiver</note>; now we shall discuss those which are full of juice. These, in the opposite manner (because they are raised more easily than others), must be distilled by **ascent** <term>ascent</term> <note>original: "ascensum"; the standard method where vapors rise up into a condenser</note>. This should be done using glass instruments, or at least earthenware ones glazed on the inside, and not those made of copper or lead—unless they are later rectified in glass more frequently, as was said above regarding distilled wine.

### *On the distillation of seeds and of all dry things. CHAPTER IX.*

Let these be placed in furnace G or H upon earthen stands <note>original: "pinnacidia"; small ceramic tiles or pedestals used to support vessels inside a furnace</note>, so that the helmet <note>original: "galea"; the alembic head or cap of the distilling vessel</note> alone projects outside the furnace cover, and with a slow fire, let the water mixed with oil be distilled until the powder becomes dry; then let it be moistened again. If, indeed, the oil does not flow with the water, let the fire be increased; let this be repeated quite often until no oil flows with the water. You shall separate the water from the oil by means of a Mary’s bath <note>A water bath used for gentle, even heating</note>; let all the water ascend, while only the oil remains below in the bottom of the cucurbit <note>A gourd-shaped glass vessel</note>, which can be rectified <note>Refined or concentrated</note> by sunning or digestion, as was said above. It will be advisable to receive the oil through a water cooling-vessel <note>original: "refrigeratorium aqueum"; a condenser filled with water to cool the vapors</note> to avoid scorching. Some are also accustomed to use glass retorts <note>A flask with a long, downward-pointing neck</note> so that the spirit may flow out more easily, since it is not raised in them to such a great height as in upright cucurbits.

### *The distillation of solidified liquids.*

### CHAPTER X.

Solidified liquids <note>original: "Liquores concreti"; substances that are liquid when heated but solid at room temperature, such as resins, waxes, or fats</note>, which melt by fire, can be distilled by themselves. They are found in two general types: either vegetable, such as gums of every kind, or mineral, such as jet <note>original: "gagates"; a minor gemstone and mineral of organic origin</note> and subterranean coal. Vegetable ones include frankincense <note>original: "thus"</note>, myrrh, labdanum <note>A resin from the Cistus shrub</note>, storax calamita <note>A fragrant resin from the Styrax tree</note>, mastic, juniper gum, and camphor. Once ground, they are placed in simple distilled water for infusion, buried underground in an exceedingly damp place for several days; and when they have been dissolved, let them be distilled just as was described above regarding dry seeds.

<page-num>56</page-num>
<header>OF THE CHYMICAL ART <note>original: "Artis Chymisticae"</note></header>

Some add wine or alcohol <note>original: "ardentem aquam"; literally "burning water," referring to distilled spirits or aqua vitae</note>, but in my judgment, these change the nature of the materials. Minerals, such as jet <note>original: "gagares," a variation of "gagates," a minor mineraloid used in alchemy</note>, bitumen, and mineral coal <note>original: "carbo subterraneus"</note>, can be distilled by themselves in a glass retort protected by a strong lute <term>lute: a clay or paste used to seal joints and protect glass from direct heat</term> over a slow fire at first; then, once the heat is increased, let the liquid be collected. These oils can also be extracted by descent <note>A distillation method where the substance is heated from above or in a specialized vessel so the oil drips downward into a receiver below the heat source</note>.

### *The distillation of boiling liquids.*
#### CHAPTER XI.

Certain things are very difficult to distill because of their bubbling <note>original: "ebullitionem"; referring to the tendency of thick substances to foam up and boil over into the condenser</note>. It is therefore necessary to find a way to restrain their "bodies," <note>The physical matter remaining in the vessel</note> so that they do not escape into the helmet <note>original: "galeam"; the alembic head or cap of the distilling vessel</note> along with their spirits. Of this kind are honey, wax, turpentine, and resins; when these are placed in a tall cucurbit <note>A gourd-shaped glass or ceramic vessel</note>, small river pebbles should be placed on top of them to hold back the foam. One should then proceed with a moderate fire until the liquids flow out. The first liquid is usually white, because it is mixed in some way with phlegm <term>phlegm: in alchemy, the watery, tasteless, and inactive portion of a distillate</term>. The second is yellowish. The third, however, is red, because it possesses more fiery powers than the second. Some people put in fragments of bricks or tiles, but while they think they have made a simple oil, they actually have a composite one, more like "oil of bricks" <note>original: "oleum laterinum"; a medicinal oil traditionally made by distilling bricks that had been soaked in oil</note> than the oil of the substance for which it is named. Others throw in tree leaves and fragments of wood, but all these things seem to contribute something of their own nature to the result, which the aforementioned pebbles do not do. Thus far, all the preparations—

...[instructio]ns <note>The text begins mid-word; likely "operationes" or "rationes," referring to the methods previously discussed.</note> necessary for medical practice have been set down by us so exactly that the art of preparing natural medicines from any mixed bodies—that is, those composed of elements, which are commonly called "simples"—cannot remain hidden from anyone endowed with talent. That I have placed their titles under a riddle <note>original: "sub ænigmate"</note> was done not so that they might remain hidden, but rather so that clever readers, through practice, may become accustomed to the obscure writings of the philosophers, and thus in this little work, the final parts may correspond to the first. Receive this, therefore, with a joyful heart, excellent readers; read and re-read, and it shall come to pass that you are not frustrated in your labor.

## *A very easy riddle, through which that medicinal and mineral stone is revealed.*

### CHAPTER XII.

*You shall visit the interior of the earth; by rectifying, you will find the hidden stone, the true medicine.* <note>This is the famous alchemical acrostic for V.I.T.R.I.O.L.: "Visita Interiora Terrae Rectificando Invenies Occultum Lapidem." In alchemy, Vitriol (iron or copper sulfate) was considered a primary material for the Great Work.</note>

#### *The method of preparation.*

We shall calcine <term>calcine: to heat a substance to high temperatures in the air to oxidize it or reduce it to a powder</term> six pounds of this stone—which is now revealed to us—in an earthenware pot in the following manner. We shall build a furnace of fired bricks around the pot, placing the bricks one above the other so that they do not touch each other <note>This creates gaps for airflow, essential for maintaining the high heat of the coals</note>; in the middle of this, the pot should be placed upon a fragment of brick. The pot is to be open at the top, with lit coals also placed around the pot,

### Page 60

Then we must have a <term>cucurbit</term> <note>a gourd-shaped earthenware vessel used for distillation</note> made of baked earth, composed of such material that it can withstand a most vehement fire—similar to that used for the melting of metals—for several hours without damage. Such vessels can be made at Cologne and Haguenau in Germany, on account of the excellent quality of the earth in those places. But if such vessels cannot be obtained, let us at least ensure that the potters mix one third part of their clay with two thirds of powder crushed from the fragments of goldsmiths' crucibles. These vessels should have a very narrow opening, so that the neck of a glass <term>retort</term> <note>a vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids</note> can fit inside them; you may see their form in the illustration of the triple furnace.

We place such a cucurbit, filled halfway or slightly less with the aforementioned powder, in the middle <term>reverberatory</term> <note>a type of furnace that reflects heat back onto the material being heated</note> section of the triple furnace, positioned crosswise, just as we described previously regarding <term>vitriol</term> <note>original: "vitriolo"; a term for various sulfate minerals or sulfuric acid</note>. Let the long neck of the cucurbit project through a hole in the side of the furnace for the distance of five or six finger-widths, so that it may enter a large glass retort (of the kind we used for the vitriol). Let the joint be sealed with a paste made of egg whites and kneaded flour, and let the furnace hole around the protruding neck of the cucurbit be reinforced with clay, so that the heat does not escape from there.

Soon we must place lit coals into the furnace—only a few at first—and thus with a slow fire draw out all the <term>phlegm</term> <note>the watery, inactive part of a substance produced during distillation</note>. ... [that] one part has ascended <note>original: "ascendisse"; referring to the vapor rising and condensing</note>, which we shall set aside. After having at last moved another receiving vessel, we will continue until we have extracted another similar part: which we shall also separate, removed from the other. As many such parts are to be extracted until we perceive by taste that nothing or little of the <term>sulphurous powers</term> <note>original: "virium sulphurearum"; in alchemy, the "sulphur" of a substance was its flammable, oily, and active principle</note> remains in the distilled liquor. This will be best proven if the liquor, when moved to the fire, catches flame just like sulphur; if it does not, one must stop, and whatever residue remains in the bottom of the <term>cucurbit</term> <note>a gourd-shaped glass or ceramic vessel used in distillation</note>—consisting of dregs and <term>phlegm</term> <note>original: "phlegmatis"; the watery, inactive, and tasteless portion of a distillate</note>—we shall completely empty out. 

We shall again distill sixteen fresh parts as before. This is to be repeated so many times until we have the greatest part of this liquor, from the first as well as the second, third, and fourth (if there are so many) distillations. Next, we shall fill the cucurbit to the middle, as before, with the first distillation: and when we have extracted the middle part of it, having removed the receiver, let the remaining half be received in another vessel. What has now been done for the first distillation ought to be done for the second, third, and the others. Thus, the first and second distillations of the first distillation will be held separately, and likewise the first and second of the second and third, and so on for the rest. 

This distillation can even be repeated a third time in a copper vessel or glazed earthenware as before, by putting in a single <term>flagon</term> <note>original: "lagenam"; a vessel used here as a unit of measure</note>, if you take the first part of the first: when half of it has ascended from it, let it be placed aside, and it shall be called from the first...

Reduced into an ethereal substance, the first, second, and third flasks can be administered against any diseases. The dosage shall be at the discretion of the physician.

### *The third enigma, through which the stone most powerful for medicine—and called "animal" by the philosophers—is revealed.*

#### CHAPTER XIIII.

We present a price, both cheap and dear <note>original: "Precium vile carum"; a common alchemical paradox referring to the "Prima Materia," which is found everywhere and is cheap, yet is spiritually the most precious substance</note>, to be devoured by the caduceus-bearing dragon <note>original: "draconi caducifero"; the dragon carrying the staff of Hermes, representing Mercury or quicksilver in its volatile state</note>, for a sixth part of itself. Having tasted this, that restless serpent soon halts its step, and driven into a deep sleep, it is given to be absorbed by waters flowing from two fountains, one white and one green: until the next summer returns, which by its vehement heat dries the waters, and the dead corpse appears in the depths of the sea. When this corpse is cast into the fire, it revives, and having taken up wings again, it flies off into the air; yet it leaves its offspring in the fire: which it brings forth from the conception of the "price." This offspring, because it was born in the fire, must be nourished by fire like a Salamander <note>original: "Salamantra"; in alchemy, the salamander was a symbol of the "fixed" substance that can endure fire without being consumed</note>, until—

->OF THE CHYMICAL ART<-

...and now having become a youth, he emerges exceedingly red and full of blood <note>original: "rubicundus, & sanguineus"; this refers to the *Rubedo* or Reddening, the final stage of the alchemical process symbolizing perfection and vitality</note>. The philosophers have discovered that this soul, being healthy in every way, when poured into a sick body, restores it to be clean from every stain by the simplicity of its virtue. This is the most perfect and most simple medicine of all medicines, leaving nothing corrupt in the human body; rather, it revives all languid things and transmutes into a balanced state <note>original: "temperiem"; referring to the Four Humors or qualities being brought into perfect equilibrium</note> whatever was unbalanced and imperfect. By the simplicity of its union, it makes peace between mortal enemies <note>referring to the opposing elements of Fire, Water, Earth, and Air</note>, and through concord—God granting—it grants life in this age to the half-dead and bestows health upon sick bodies.

### Conclusion of the former work. CHAPTER XV.

All those who are ignorant of this art, even though they may be most learned from the Universities, are accustomed to disapprove of it; and what wonder? since they have never heard of such a thing nor experienced it. From this it does not follow that the art is to be condemned because learned men have been ignorant of it. Indeed, many things have come to light in our age which were unknown to the ancients, just as many things have now become hidden from us...

<header>BOOK THREE</header>

...to us, things which were most familiar to the ancient fathers <note>original: "patribus antiquis"; here referring to the biblical patriarchs or the earliest philosophers who were believed by alchemists to possess divine secrets</note>. What does not pass into oblivion through the injury of time, as well as the laziness of men? Yet, the thing itself does not perish along with the memory of it; rather, after it has lain buried for a long time, it will emerge by the will of God—whether for those people in that era, or for others in this century. For within His breast, all praiseworthy and good arts rest from eternity alongside His wisdom. Almighty God distributes His gifts to whom He wishes, and He takes them away from the ungrateful who abuse them. 

There is no doubt that God revealed some medicine to the ancient fathers through His Holy Spirit, by which they might guard against the corruption of the flesh <note>The "corruption of the flesh" refers to aging, disease, and the physical decay resulting from the Fall of Man</note>, and especially to those with whom He spoke and made a covenant. But after the vain desire of men to be wise by their own power led them to seek salvation from themselves and in things other than Him—in whom alone is salvation and redemption—their minds were blinded. Led into a labyrinth, they try now this herb, now that one; tomorrow this root and then another; the day after, some mixture. Thus, by experimenting day by day, they never arrived at the goal, nor do they possess any true certainty in their teachings. For those who remain stuck in that wavering labyrinth, it never happens that they reach Corinth <note>original: "adire Corinthum"; a reference to the classical proverb *Non cuivis homini contingit adire Corinthum* (It is not every man's lot to get to Corinth), meaning that not everyone can achieve a difficult or elite goal—in this case, the true "Universal Medicine"</note>. 

And what seems even more absurd is that men are never content with their own lot. God gave to every nation what He wished it to possess, both for nourishment and for medicine. What follows from this? Our own ini- <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "ini-", likely leading to *iniquitas* (iniquity) or *iniuria* (injury/wrongdoing) on the following page</note>

<header>OF THE ALCHEMICAL ART</header>

...accustomed to despise what is familiar, and always to long for foreign things. We have among us—and more powerfully within ourselves, though not derived from ourselves—whatever we seek outside of us or from others. Let us see how the Pagan Gentiles <note>The author uses "Gentiles Pagani" to refer to pre-Christian Greek and Roman philosophers whose natural observations are used here to shame contemporary Christians who have forgotten these "natural" truths.</note> might argue against us Christians in this matter. For they say that nature seeks a nature similar to itself and rejoices in its own nature; if it is joined to a foreign nature, the work of nature is destroyed. This is a wondrous saying to those who do not well consider what nature is, but to those who know nature, nothing can be more true.

Nature is threefold, namely: **Universal**, **particular**, and **mixed**. 

**Universal nature** is suitable for all things and exists through all things. 

**Particular nature** is suitable for that which is similar to itself, and is threefold by reason of its parts. 

**Mixed nature** corresponds more to matter and less to spirit, for which reason it can be said to act with impediment. 

Upon this distinction, the entire medicine of our age depends. Therefore, according to this, we approve less of those things which were taken from outside the nature of a climate <note>In historical medicine, "climate" referred to specific geographical zones. It was believed that plants and minerals from one's own "climate" were more biocompatible than exotic imports.</note> for use in medicine than those which are of the same climate. In the same way, a particular nature from a different climate is less suitable than one from the same. 

A universal nature from a different climate operates sometimes more and sometimes less, according to the various influences of the heavens; nevertheless, it is more beneficial than the particular or mixed nature of one's own climate. And according to this reasoning, medicine will be threefold, namely: 
1. The **metaphysical** of the unary <note>original: "vnarii"; referring to the Monad or Unity, the highest form of spiritual medicine.</note>, 
2. The **physical** of the ternary <note>original: "ternarii"; referring to the three alchemical principles of Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury.</note>, 
3. And the **common** of the binary <note>original: "binarii"; referring to dualities or simple mixtures.</note>. 

This last one has obtained the name of "simple" <note>A "simple" in historical pharmacy is a medicine made from a single herb or ingredient, as opposed to a "compound."</note> in this century, which nonetheless...

<summary>This page defines three levels of Nature—Universal, Particular, and Mixed—and argues that medicine is most effective when it aligns with the patient's local climate and these natural categories. It introduces a hierarchy of medicine: Metaphysical (Unity), Physical (Ternary), and Common (Binary/Simples).</summary>

# BOOK THREE

...was once bestowed upon the two former, and is more suitable. Hence it follows that those who have applied themselves only to the **medicine of mixtures** <term>medicina mixtorum: standard medical practice involving the mixing of various herbs and minerals to balance the body's humors</term>, although they possess sharp minds, they cannot grasp the knowledge of that **universal medicine** <term>medicina universalis: a theoretical "cure-all" or "Panacea" in alchemy that aims to restore the vital spirit rather than just treating physical symptoms</term> which is able to heal all diseases without distinction. They are led by this reasoning: "No medicine," they say, "can be designated that is not either hot and dry, or hot and moist." How then, they ask, can a hot and dry medicine expel an excess of hot and dry disease? For this ought to be done with a cold and moist medicine, according to the rule: **opposites are cured by opposites** <note>original: *contraria contrariis curantur*. This was the fundamental principle of Galenic medicine, which held that a fever (hot/dry) must be treated with cooling and moistening agents.</note>.

But the major premise of their argument must be denied, because in every mixture or individual thing, there is found something other than the hot, cold, dry, moist, or the physical element itself. That which is separated from all of these by our art is neither moist, nor dry, nor cold, and certainly not hot; rather, it is **something ethereal** <note>original: *aethereum quid*</note>, like the **heaven** <note>original: *cœlum*</note> separated from the lower elements. For this reason, this medicine is named "Heaven" by the philosophers, because it acts upon our bodies in no other way than the heaven acts upon the lower realms. Furthermore, it is of an incorruptible nature compared to the body from which it was taken, just as the heaven is incorruptible compared to the elements.

For the elements are dead in themselves, and unless they are quickened by the soul or the **spirit of the world** <term>spiritus mundi: in alchemical philosophy, a subtle substance or "breath" that permeates the universe and gives life to matter</term>, they produce nothing. For example, earth that has not been under the open sky, however rich it may be, produces nothing; but when placed under the naked breeze, it brings forth its own herbs and little animals, yet nothing...

<summary>The author argues for the existence of a "universal medicine" or "Heavenly" quintessence that transcends the traditional medical qualities of hot, cold, wet, and dry. He posits that this spiritual essence, or spiritus mundi, is the true life-giving force within all matter.</summary>

<keywords>universal medicine, quintessence, Galenic medicine, four qualities, spirit of the world, alchemy, elements, incorruptibility</keywords>

->### ON THE CHYMICAL ART<-

...so solid in the globe of the earth that it could prevent this **soul** <term>anima: in alchemy, the "soul" is the formal, animating principle or vital spark that gives a substance its specific properties</term> from penetrating into its center, all the way: the **generation of metals** <term>generatio metallorum: the natural process by which metals were believed to grow like plants within the earth's crust</term> teaches us this, occurring as it does in the bowels of the earth which have no opening or breathing hole. We see, therefore, the difference between the generation of vegetables and animals—which does not happen except by means of **free air**—and the generation of metals, which cannot occur except in **vessels closed on all sides**: for if an exit lay open for the **metallic vapors**, they would never congeal into metal, but would vanish into the air. 

From this, we judge that the soul in metals is more penetrative than in animals and vegetables: since it has penetrated very solid stony vessels, which the most sharp spirits of others cannot penetrate. Whoever thinks the elements generate by themselves is no more considered a Philosopher than a fool who would want to assert that a woman is naturally impregnated without a man. Although the element or **subterranean fire** burns the earth into **sulfur** <note>In alchemy, Sulfur represents the active, combustible, and "masculine" principle of matter, not just the yellow mineral.</note>, and rouses the water into airy vapor, they nevertheless cannot be animated except through the penetrating soul, of which we spoke above. **Elemental fire** has an active power for **corruption** <term>corruptio: the philosophical process of breaking down a substance's current form so that a new one can be generated</term>, not for generation, as stated above. 

Again, he who denies that elemental fire has its primary origin in the center of the earth, from which it naturally rises to the concavity of its **sphere**, is not worthy of the title of philosopher, because he admits...

->### BOOK THREE<-

...[to be] ignorant of the fact that the **fire of corruption** <note>original: "ignis corruptionis"; in alchemical theory, this is the destructive heat that breaks down old forms so that new life can be generated.</note> exercises mastery over the rest of the elements. The benefit of **generation**, however, is owed to heaven, not to the elements; by this we can conclude that all generation occurs between heaven and the elements. Now we must see what kind of offspring comes from such a birth; certainly, it is the end of generation, just as the end of corruption is generation. But to speak more plainly, the offspring is a **mixture** <term>mixtum: a compound or substance formed by the union of celestial and elemental influences</term> produced from the substance of both parents for its own perfection. Every individual is a **perfect mixture**, because although it may sometimes be deformed in some part, this is not to be ascribed to nature nor to form (which do not happen to err) but to **matter** (as was said above) that is ill-disposed <note>The author argues that nature's "blueprint" or "form" is always perfect, but the physical "matter" it works with may be flawed or resistant, leading to biological defects.</note>. Therefore, those seem to err greatly who call individuals—and especially vegetables—"simples": unless perhaps they do this in respect to medicines composed of many mixtures and individuals, and thus they dream that any mixture is a single simple. From this comes the name of **Simplicists** <term>Simplicistarum: herbalists or apothecaries who specialized in "simples," which were individual medicinal plants used as remedies</term> applied to those who devote their effort to identifying vegetables. Now let us return to that **simple medicine**. Whoever denies that such a medicine can be given—one which is able to indifferently correct any corruption within its own corruptibility—denies that heaven sustains or governs the lower things. But lest we seem to want to prostitute the secrets of nature with allurements, let a few words suffice for these **sons of wisdom** <term>sapientiae filiis: a traditional term for initiates or practitioners of alchemy who possess true spiritual understanding</term>; they will easily understand what we mean by these things...

<summary>This page explains that all created things (offspring) are a mixture of heavenly influence and elemental matter, arguing that while nature's "form" is perfect, defects arise from the matter itself. It also critiques "Simplicists" (herbalists) and asserts the existence of a universal "simple medicine" derived from celestial power.</summary>

# OF THE CHYMICAL ART

...join <note>original: "conjungunt"; completing the sentence from the previous page regarding how herbalists mistakenly group substances.</note> them. But those who are truly ignorant of our **art** <term>ars chymistica: the "chymical art" or alchemy, which involves the purification of both matter and the practitioner’s soul</term> and mock it, let them flee far from here, because it is set for their ruin; so that seeking they may not find, and hearing they may not understand. One must not cast roses and pearls before swine, lest they trample them under their feet as if they were beets unfamiliar to their lips <note>The author uses "beets" (betas) as a metaphor for common, coarse food that swine would prefer over the "pearls" of philosophical wisdom.</note>. The **sons of wisdom** <term>sapientiae filii: a traditional term for alchemists or seekers of divine knowledge</term> draw their nourishment by the spirit, not by the mouth, so that they may live by the mind more than by the body. In the meantime, however, they desire that there be a **healthy mind in a healthy body** <note>original: "mens sana in corpore sano"; a famous Roman proverb emphasizing that physical health is the proper vessel for mental or spiritual work.</note>, until, being released from the bond of nature, these three—united again into one—may live forever.

# TO THE KIND READER
## THE AUTHOR’S PREFACE.

*After I had finished the Key to Chemical Philosophy <note>original: "Chymisticæ philosophiæ clauem." In the 16th century, "Chemical Philosophy" referred to Alchemy, which combined laboratory work with spiritual and cosmological theory.</note>, compiled with such skill that every single chapter might require a separate treatise of its own, I could not help but offer an explanation of the first chapter to the dedicated lovers of this philosophy. I do this so that I may discover with what sort of reception they will welcome our labors. If I perceive that these works are pleasing and acceptable to them, they shall eventually be allowed to see greater things than have proceeded from my humble nightly studies <term>Lucubrations: from "lucubratiunculis," meaning scholarly works produced by candlelight or through late-night effort; Dorn uses the diminutive form here as a gesture of modesty.</term> until now. But if they are not well-received, I leave it to others to test their own wits in this matter, should they wish to provide anything more useful or more excellent. For my part, I shall consider that I have done enough by opening the way for all through the handing down of the "key" and the demonstration of the various stages. I know well enough that many of those people who are accustomed to approve of nothing except what is either their own, or at least familiar to them—or perhaps what they have heard in the Universities <note>Dorn, a follower of Paracelsus, often criticized the "Universities" for clinging to the rigid, theoretical teachings of Galen and Aristotle while ignoring the practical, experimental "mysteries of nature."</note>—will sharpen their teeth against those who search into the more secret mysteries of nature. Yet I do not wonder at this, when I notice how such people take it very badly that after their own studies—attained through long labor and no small expense, through which they have already grown gray hairs—certain new things should come to light. These new ideas, if not unheard of, are at least little or not at all understood by them, yet they are far more excellent than their own; they are moreover so proven by the most ancient authorities and by recent experience to anyone of sounder judgment that they cannot be shaken by any arguments. Worn out by old age* <note>The sentence continues on the next page: "Senio confectos..."</note>

<vocab>Chemical philosophy, key, mysteries of nature, nightly studies, Universities</vocab>

<summary>In this preface, Gerard Dorn introduces his commentary on the first chapter of his alchemical system, expressing a desire to gauge the reader's interest before publishing more advanced works. He defends his "new" findings against the inevitable criticism of university-trained traditionalists who resent his departure from established academic dogma.</summary>
<keywords>Gerard Dorn, Chemical Philosophy, Alchemical Key, Academic vs. Alchemical knowledge, Secrets of Nature, Scholasticism, Paracelsianism</keywords>

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface where Dorn defends his departure from traditional University teachings in favor of experience and the "moral" (spiritual) application of physical philosophy.</meta>

## PREFACE.

Those who are ashamed to learn—especially those who are accustomed to teaching others—are moved by a boorish rather than a philosophical sense of shame. Have we not also spent a lifetime of labor and great expense in the Universities? Yet we are not afraid to pull back our foot from lesser things, in order to plant it upon more stable ground. We do not for this reason (as some of our colleagues might think) despise the things taught in the Universities, nor do we reject them entirely as if they were worthless. We say only this: that we have learned far better things through experience than from the attendance of many lectures.

I do not doubt that there will be many who, when they are unable to criticize the subject matter itself, will seek the smallest excuse—be it unrefined language or the order of the treatment—to gnaw at what they are unable to tear down. But to respond to both points: it is certain that those who are most intent upon the actual substance of things care very little for useless formalities. They see that a man’s longest lifespan is insufficient to truly know a subject, and they know that in the profession of truth there is no room for a "cloak" <note>original: "pallio". Here, Dorn refers to the academic or rhetorical 'cloak'—the outward trappings of status and flowery language that hide a lack of real substance.</note>. Truth is rarely colored by elegance of speech or long-windedness.

Lest I seem to anyone to have drifted from the physical principles set down in my *Key* <note>Referring back to his previous work mentioned on the prior page, the "Key to Chemical Philosophy."</note> into the realm of moral philosophy, I shall reveal this secret to the learned. Those things which sometimes (though not always) appear to be physical are, to true philosophers, moral <note>In the Paracelsian tradition, "moral" often refers to the spiritual or inward transformation of the practitioner, suggesting that alchemical processes are metaphors for the refinement of the human soul.</note>. While they remain hidden to most readers, they are perfectly clear to the philosophers themselves; yet to others, they are regarded as the most secret of mysteries. By this art they have veiled the Art <term>The Art: A common term for Alchemy, also known as the "Great Art" or "Royal Art."</term> from those who mock everything that their own intellect is unable to grasp.

<center>D</center>

<summary>In this preface, Dorn defends his experimental approach against academic critics, arguing that true knowledge comes from experience rather than university lectures. He explains that his alchemical "physics" actually contains "moral" or spiritual truths intended only for the wise.</summary>

<keywords>Gerard Dorn, Universities vs. Experience, Alchemy as Moral Philosophy, The Art, Academic Criticism, Paracelsian thought</keywords>

->### TO THE READER<-

*You may also think that I have done this so that, after the delivery of the art and its Key, I might soon propose steps as means by which your studies may be promoted step-by-step toward higher things. It would be impossible for any literate person, no matter how learned, to be able to reach this art—which is by nature most secret—unless he has made a transition through all these philosophical steps, whether morally <note>In the context of Paracelsian alchemy, "morally" (moraliter) refers to the internal, spiritual, or psychological transformation of the practitioner, rather than just outward ethics.</note> or at least physically; though it is more successful when done in both ways. I do not think there will be a lack of others who, while they dare to accuse philosophers of tautology <note>The repetition of the same idea in different words, often seen by critics as redundant.</note> or superfluous repetition, will betray their own ignorance in this art. For it is characteristic of this philosophy to first run through things briefly which are later explained more broadly, and finally to repeat them in a certain recapitulation. This is done so that the minds of the readers may be strengthened by the frequency of the subject matter, rather than being forced by long-windedness to believe things that have not been gently presented. All philosophers have reached this art through a certain studious love; they also advise others toward it, but they do not compel them. Nor should you think that mountains of gold lie hidden here, or that so-highly boasted Philosopher's Stone <term>Lapis Philosophorum: The "Philosopher's Stone," the legendary substance of alchemy. Dorn is reinterpreting it here as a medicinal and spiritual goal rather than a way to get rich.</term>, which (as some assert) is able to transmute all inferior metals into mintable gold. Instead, I propose that you seek a physical stone by which the less perfect "physical metals" of the human body (you may consider these to be diseases) may be changed into most perfect metaphysical metals, which are called Elixirs <note>original: "Eleysiria". An archaic spelling for Elixirs, referring to the "Universal Medicine" or tinctures used for healing.</note>, and the medicine of all things.*

**Philosophy, Philosopher's Stone, Metaphysics, Elixirs, Medicine**

<summary>Dorn explains that his work uses repetition and "philosophical steps" to lead the reader toward a secret art. He clarifies that his "Philosopher's Stone" is not for making gold, but is a metaphysical medicine intended to cure the "base metals" (diseases) of the human body.</summary>

<header>P R A E F A T I O. 75</header>

*is most simple. For this reason, in so many places, all philosophers urge those who turn themselves to these studies not to place literal faith in the writings of the philosophers, but rather to interpret them allegorically.* <note>Dorn warns that alchemical texts are coded; taking instructions literally leads to failure.</note>

*The ancients placed the greatest riches in the health of mind and body: this is clear from the fact that many of those who handed down this art to us willingly observed the strictest poverty, such as Raymundus Lullius.* <note>original: "Raymundus Lullius." Known in English as Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), a Majorcan philosopher and polymath to whom many later alchemical works were attributed.</note> *Others lived modestly, like Hermes Trismegistus,* <note>The legendary "thrice-great" founder of Hermetic philosophy.</note> *or lived splendidly on goods they possessed beforehand—not from this art, but by paternal inheritance—such as Geber of the Arabs,* <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan, a foundational figure in Arabic alchemy.</note> *who was a most wealthy king by his father's riches. Whatever the case may be, I know this: while I was still a junior disciple of the art, desiring to be enriched by this mastery along with others, after many labors, foul-smelling breaths harmful to health,* <note>Dorn is describing the toxic fumes and physical toll of traditional laboratory alchemy.</note> *and no small expense, I lost both my oil and my labor.* <note>original: "oleum & operam perdidisse." A Latin idiom meaning to waste time, effort, and money on a fruitless task.</note> *Yet that could not pull my mind away from that part of philosophy; rather, both because I read the very weighty authorities of such great men, and because I saw wonderful natural operations through experience, I was persuaded to steer the prow of this ship in another direction.*

*And when I noticed that metallic medicines do not transmute metals, but only tint and illuminate them: I judged that the philosophers wrote their art concerning "philosophical tinctures,"* <term>tincture: in alchemy, a potent liquid or substance believed to be able to "tinge" or transform the nature of a base material or a diseased body.</term> *and I likewise wished to experience those tinctures which could be applied to the human body: especially because formerly of medical matters...*

A classical idiom referring to Minerva, the goddess of wisdom and weaving; the author implies that had he known better, he would have applied a different intellectual approach or guiding principle to his work.</note> *At last, advanced by the teachings of **Aureolus Theophrastus Paracelsus**,* <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), known as Paracelsus. Farewell.*

<meta>This page begins the first chapter of the main treatise, moving from the general preface into the definitions of "Speculative Philosophy." As anticipated by the catchword on page 76, Dorn begins by defining the relationship between the mind, the soul, and the physical body in the context of alchemical self-improvement.</meta>

# ON SPECULATIVE PHILOSOPHY. CHAPTER I.

**Speculative Philosophy** is defined as the voluntary withdrawal of a well-composed mind from the body, so that the <term>spirit</term> <note>original: *animus*. In this philosophical context, *animus* refers to the rational, higher part of the human soul associated with the intellect, as opposed to the *anima*, which is the vital life-force.</note> can more easily be engaged in the pursuit of truth. To speculate well and truly, a good disposition of the body is required, so that it does not hinder the mind. This state can be acquired in two ways: namely, by nature or by art. That which is natural can be assisted by art, and one may even obtain that which was denied by nature in this regard: primarily through the use of **reason**, then by **sobriety of diet**, and by <term>Philosophical medicines</term> <note>These are alchemical preparations—often called "spagyric" medicines—intended to purify the body's physical substance to make it a more suitable vessel for the spirit.</note>—using these remedies either individually or all together.

However, so that it may be better understood what these things mean, we must examine every term of the definition, down to the last one. A **mind** is said to be "well-composed" whenever the spirit is joined to the **soul** <note>original: *anima*. Dorn is describing a tripartite structure of the human being: Body, Soul (*anima*), and Spirit/Mind (*animus*).</note> by such a bond that it is able to restrain the appetites of the body. A "good disposition" of the body should be understood as occurring when the body is less subject to its own corrupt appetites; the best state is achieved when its component parts have acquired an excellent <term>temperament</term>, <note>original: *temperaturam*. In pre-modern medicine, this refers to the perfect balance of the four qualities—hot, cold, moist, and dry—within the body.</note> for then it craves its own desires less and becomes more obedient to the mind. Whatever the body craves is corrupt. Yet, it can crave nothing except through the faculty of the soul, which is the mover of the body. The soul moves simply, without any conception of good or evil; the body receives this motion, and through its own corrupt...

D iij

<vocab>Speculative Philosophy, mind, body, spirit, soul, reason, sobriety, Philosophical medicines</vocab>

<summary>Dorn defines Speculative Philosophy as the mental withdrawal from the physical body to seek truth, arguing that a purified, "well-composed" body is a necessary tool for intellectual and spiritual work. He introduces the requirement of balancing the body's temperament through diet and alchemical medicine to ensure it obeys the higher spirit.</summary>
<keywords>Speculative Philosophy, animus, anima, body and mind, alchemy, temperament, philosophical medicines, reason</keywords>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "well-composed mind" from page 77, specifically how the soul acts as a bridge between the spiritual mind and the physical body. It outlines the tripartite structure of the human being (Spirit, Soul, and Body) and the faculties of the higher mind.</meta>

->S P E C V L A T I V E<-

...its nature desires to pursue evil, unless it is resisted by the **spirit** <note>original: *animus*. In this context, the higher, rational part of the soul.</note>, which urges nothing but the good. Therefore, the **soul** <note>original: *anima*. The vital life-force or mediating principle.</note> is situated between good and evil; it has its choice of good through the good, and of evil through evil. This occurs only by the permission of the good, so that such evil may happen on account of the constant resistance made by the soul when it is dragged by the body, as opposed to the pull of the spirit moved by the good. This can properly be called the stubbornness of evil against the spirit that urges every good.

In order that these things may be better understood, it must be known that man consists of three most powerful parts: namely, the **Spirit** <note>original: *Animus*</note>, the **Soul**, and the **Body**. The Spirit is called the "breath of life." The Soul is indeed the instrument of the spirit, just as the Body is the instrument of the soul. The soul is the life of the body and the mediating substance between the spirit and the body. If the soul adheres more to the spirit than to the body, the **Mind** <note>original: *Mens*</note> and the **inner man** arise; if, on the contrary, it clings more to the body than to the spirit, no mind is formed, but it is called the **outer man** and an abyss of darkness.

The spirit is also delighted by three things: **Reason**, **Intellect**, and **Memory**. Reason represents the image of speculation to the intellect; the intellect then presents it to the memory to be preserved in secret. **Reason** is the first, eternal, perpetual, and inviolable order of the Mind, in which man has been made a participant by the gift of God. Along with this is the **Intellect**, which is the grasping of reason; and likewise **Memory**, which can be called the preservation of both. The Soul consists of two parts: namely, **Motion** and **Sense**. Motion is,

...either natural or accidental; but because I have dealt with this in our *Clavis* <note>original: *Clave nostra*. Likely referring to a specific work by the author, perhaps titled "The Key."</note>, I pass over it briefly here. **Sensation** <term>Sensus: the faculty of perceiving external stimuli through the five senses</term> is the perception of a thing either seen, heard, tasted, smelled, or touched: For the members of the body, through which sensations are perceived by the **soul** <note>original: *anima*</note>, we properly call the **organs** of the senses. The perception of the senses recalls to the **memory** the knowledge of past things through the representation of present ones. Brute animals <note>Animals guided by instinct rather than human reason.</note> have this in common with humans: for they recall things only when present objects are set before them, recollecting them through sensation.

There is also another, far more excellent root of memory, which does not rely on any mediating representation, but cultivates the said memory solely through the diligent activity of the ever-active **spirit** <note>original: *spiritus*</note>, and makes it flourish perpetually: this continuation of memory follows the cultivation of **reason**, and it belongs to man alone. Therefore, sensation is common to the animal, while **reason** is specifically intellectual: for brutes do not understand except through a present sensation; but rational animals understand through the **intellect** moved by reason, and sensation, whether as a subsequent or mediating factor.

Furthermore, the **spirit** <note>original: *animus*</note> and the **body** are opposites in their natures—contraries that can never be joined except through the mediating **soul** <note>original: *anima*</note>, which shares in both these antagonistic parts; but because one of the extremes is perfect and the other imperfect, if movement occurs from the perfect through the medium, it passes into the perfection of the imperfect, and vice-versa. By this principle **God** created all things, so that from three into one they might con-

...may come together: for he himself is the author of peace and union. The two and the one are enemies. Let the **binary** <note>The number two, which in this context represents duality, opposition, or the division between the physical and spiritual.</note> be rejected, so that the **ternary** <note>The number three, representing a state of harmony or the synthesis of spirit, soul, and body into a single unit.</note> may come together into one, a matter which was spoken of more broadly in *The Key* <note>original: *Clave*; likely referring to a previous volume or chapter by the author.</note>; I will not repeat it more fully here. The **spirit** <note>original: *animus*; the highest, unchanging part of the human essence.</note> is equal in all, but the **mind** <note>original: *mens*; here used to describe the realized or active state of the spirit.</note> is not equal in all. There are very few who have a **soul** <note>original: *anima*; the mediating life force.</note> joined with the spirit; there are many who participate in the mind in some measure, according to a greater or lesser degree; and there are most who, cherishing the body in the soul and the soul in the body, do not recognize the spirit—to these, no mind remains, but only madness.

Therefore, the mind is well received whenever the spirit and the soul, agreeing together, are so received by the body that from these three, one harmonious and inseparable whole is made. But this friendship can in no way happen except through separation, without which there is no **metaphysical union** <term>coniunctio metaphysica: a spiritual or philosophical joining that transcends the physical realm.</term> The One is alone; if it remains alone, it is impossible for it to be joined with another. If, however, it is to be joined with another, it is necessary that this "other" be separated from that One, since there is nothing besides that One.

And because the parts of the One symbolize <note>original: *symbolisant*; they share a common nature or "token" of the same source.</note> or have a **sympathy** <note>original: *sympathiam*; a natural resonance or attraction between things of the same origin.</note> with the One, they easily come together into one; but they do so more easily the less they are separated from the One by intervals of space or time. Therefore, a **distraction** <note>original: *distractio*; here meaning a "drawing away" or detachment of the mind from physical sensations.</note> of the mind from the body is necessary, so that a union of the one and the other may be made. But since union requires perfection, it makes a transition from the perfect extreme (as I said above) through a medium to the imperfect, so that it might perfect it. Through this kind of distraction of the mind from the body—

# P H I L O S O P H I A.
<note>Philosophy</note>

...traction <note>original: *ctionem*; likely completing the word "abstraction," referring to the withdrawal of the soul from worldly concerns.</note> (which some call a voluntary death), the **spirit** <note>original: *animus*; the higher, divine spark.</note> and the **soul** <note>original: *anima*; the life-force or mediating breath.</note>, joined together, acquire power and dominion over their body—a power which the spirit alone did not possess before, because of the resisting medium through which the natural transition must occur. This is because enemies do not come together except through a neutral mediator who stands apart from the discord, but participates in the reconciliation of both parties.

By this principle, finally, **judicial astronomy** <term>astronomia iudiciaria; the practice of making predictions or judgments based on the stars, known today as astrology.</term> is weakened, which teaches that the alignment of the stars inclines the will but does not necessitate it. For the stars are far inferior to the minds of men; likewise, that which is ignoble can do little or nothing at all against what is more noble in itself. 

However, if human bodies conquer the soul and lead it back into the slavery of the corruptible body, I will not deny that the celestial bodies of the stars are then more noble than those of men, and are able to imprint their influences upon human bodies through like bodies. In short: **the wise man will rule the stars** <note>original: *sapiens dominabitur astris*; a famous medieval and Renaissance proverb suggesting that through wisdom and free will, one can overcome astrological fate.</note>; the foolish man is not only subjected to them, but even to lower things and to all sorts of hardships. 

He is wise who, having attained a **mind** <note>original: *mens*; the realized state of the spirit.</note>, cultivates it; but he is foolish who loves the body within the soul, or the soul within the body, while the spirit is neglected. This is also confirmed by the divine word: *He who loves his soul* (understand: within the body) *will lose it, and he who hates it* (as stated above) *will keep it for eternity.* <note>A reference to the Gospel of John 12:25, interpreted here as a philosophical hierarchy of the self rather than just a moral teaching.</note> He is said to love his soul in the body who follows his wicked appetites; on the contrary, he who does not follow them, but [rejects] vanity...

A union is not called "true" unless it is inseparable; nevertheless, the beginnings of a union can be laid which do not achieve their full effect. Those who attain a perfect mind are very few; those who have a beginning of it, many; but those who have none at all are the most numerous. [physi]cal, at least, corruption has been added. Less fit [for wisdom] are those who, besides innate corruption <note>original: *ortiuam corruptionem*; referring to "original sin" or the inherent flaws of human nature from birth</note> and accidental corruption, hinder reason through their own insolence and a dissolute way of life. These are the people who, agitated by worldly cares, neglect those eternal things that exist outside the world, and who use those things which nature provided for necessity more abundantly than is proper. Therefore, just as a thing is hindered by the addition of something [harmful], so it can be assisted by its removal.

Thus, **philosophical medicines** are created through the **alchemical art** <term>Alchymia</term> <note>The author views alchemy not just as a way to change metals, but as a "most excellent" method of purifying substances to create powerful medicines.</note>, which is the most excellent of all arts. These medicines are separated from their own bodies; they possess a natural power of acting which they hold within their own body, and even after the removal of their **passive part** <note>original: *partis patientis*; in alchemical theory, the "passive" or "suffering" part is the impure, heavy matter that must be stripped away to release the active, "spiritual" essence.</note>, they retain this power. They can then perform this same action upon an external body—and more excellently than nature had allowed them to perform within their own bodies.

Now, whatever exists in the nature of things, having **form and matter** <term>Form and Matter</term> <note>The author uses Aristotelian physics: "matter" is the raw stuff of the world, and "form" is the internal logic or blueprint that makes a thing what it is.</note>, desires to be perfected in its own kind. This natural desire is the most powerful cause of the perfection of all things. And since nature rejoices to be joined to a nature similar to itself and delights in it, once the mind prevails—aided by a medicine similar to itself—the body is forced to yield to that same [higher] nature. This is just as the soul was previously forced [to yield] when the body prevailed, having been strengthened by corrupting things similar to it.

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 83 concerning how matter is transformed by what it consumes. The previous page ended with the start of a word (physi-), which here completes as "constitution" (constitutionem), referring to the physical makeup of a living being.</meta>

-># SPECULATIVE [PHILOSOPHY]<-

[The constitutio]n of a nourished thing is changed into the same nature as that which was its nourishment. Nature teaches an example of this in those who feed on the flesh of wild animals: they regularly become most fierce themselves. Nothing is taken in as nourishment by nature except that which is more subtle; the coarse and dreggy part is rejected as waste. Furthermore, any surplus of nourishment is cast out by nature through the pores as sweat, or through the bladder, or as tears.

This moved our teacher **Theophrastus Paracelsus** <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist who argued that the body functions through chemical processes.</note> to say that every limb of the body has its own **stomach** <term>stomachum proprium</term> <note>In Paracelsian theory, each organ has an "Archeus" or internal alchemist (metaphorically a "stomach") that separates useful nutrients from toxic waste.</note>, in which the nourishment is "cooked" <note>original: *decoquitur*; referring to the vital heat of digestion</note>, and in which the separation of the superfluous from the necessary takes place.

If, through the weakness of the "stomach" of any particular limb, this kind of superfluity is not separated due to poor digestion, this surplus nourishment—or the flesh in such a limb—is forced to grow into a corrupt swelling, which they have called an <term>apostema</term> <note>An abscess or inflammatory tumor.</note>; or it causes the bones to extend beyond their natural state.

I have seen an example of this in many people: namely, that all the ribs on one side were, for this reason, extended beyond their natural order and raised a hump beneath the shoulder blades, in addition to causing pain. Because this superfluity could not be purged as an <term>apostema</term> due to the solid density of the bones, it dilated into an unnatural growth without any purging, and with great pain, for it was no longer suppressed.

In **philosophical medicine** <note>Refers to alchemical elixirs or "spagyric" preparations which were believed to be stripped of all impurities.</note>, nothing coarse is left behind, nor any superfluity. For this reason, nature, being uncorrupted, accepts this medicine in its entirety,

This incorrupt nature is called **natural balsam** <term>balsamum naturale</term> <note>In Paracelsian medicine, the "balsam" is a vital, preservative fluid inherent in all living things that protects against decay and disease.</note>, which is naturally implanted in all bodies. Through this balsam, their heat and moisture—likewise natural—are preserved from corruption as much as possible. When this balsam is lacking, the human body becomes leprous; however, when the balsam recovers through its own increase, that which was already corrupted (due to the previous scarcity of the balsam) is cast out as waste. 

In this regard, a **philosophical medicine** <term>medicamentum philosophicum</term> <note>A refined alchemical remedy, often the "Elixir" or "Tincture," believed to act directly on the life force rather than just the physical symptoms.</note> differs from a physical [or galenic] one. The latter, when cast into the stomach along with its "body" <note>original: *suo corpore*; referring to the bulk or material substance of the herb or drug</note>, is separated no differently than food into a threefold nourishment and a twofold waste. The former [the philosophical medicine], however, is transmitted directly into balsam, **mercury** <term>mercurium</term>, or **Vulcan** <term>vulcanum</term> <note>In this context, Vulcan refers to the internal "alchemist" or transformative fire of the body that purifies substances.</note>. 

For it is necessary that the three most powerful parts of the physical body—**blood**, **salt**, and **sulfur** <note>original: *sanguinem, sal, & sulphur*. This is a variation of the Paracelsian "Tria Prima" (Salt, Sulfur, Mercury), here substituting blood for mercury or perhaps linking them.</note>—be nourished by things similar to themselves. From this it follows that daily food and drink are consumed along with their "bodies" [material bulk], for any element is sustained by its corresponding element. Yet all things come together and are preserved in a balanced temperature by the **ethereal** <term>æthereo</term>, to which we make our balsam and its companions as conformable as possible. This is because there is in the human body a certain substance conformable to the ethereal, which preserves the remaining elementary parts within it and causes them to continue according to its strength. 

Therefore, the spirit of the philosophical medicine, joined with its own soul and separated from its material body, [restores] the mental par- <note>The text cuts off mid-word; likely *mentales partes*, meaning "mental parts" or "faculties of the mind."</note>

[...transforming] <note>original: *tes*; likely completing the word *transmutantes* from the previous page</note> these into a substance similar to itself, it preserves even the physical body from corruption and corrects that which is already corrupted. We do not deny that our medicine can possess a **metaphysical body**—namely, at the moment when its body has been made spiritual, and the spirit has "clothed" itself in this body. This is what the philosophers mean when they say the **fixed becomes volatile**, and eventually the **volatile becomes fixed** <note>These are core alchemical terms: 'volatile' refers to substances that evaporate or change easily (like spirit), while 'fixed' refers to stable, solid matter. The goal was often to marry these two states.</note>. Certainly, a physical body cannot be cured by a more excellent body than a metaphysical one, nor can the human mind be better structured than by a metaphysical disposition—provided it follows the gift of divine grace. From all these points, we conclude that **speculative philosophy** consists in the overcoming of the body through the achievement of a **mental union**.

But this first union does not yet make a **Sage** <term>Sophum</term> <note>From the Greek 'Sophos,' a title for a master of wisdom or a perfected alchemist.</note>; rather, it makes one only a mental disciple of Wisdom. A second union—of the mind with the body—brings about the Sage. This Sage then hopes for and awaits that third, complete, and blessed union with the **First Unity** <note>The 'First Unity' refers to God or the ultimate source of all being.</note>. May Almighty God grant that we all become such, and may He be the One in all. By His help, let us now come to the first step of this most happy philosophy. Sincere philosophers have found this to be the true path of study, though it cannot begin until the preceding matters are explained more clearly through a brief recapitulation.

The mind, like faith, is truly a free gift of God, as are all the virtues. From this it does not follow—

-># PHILOSOPHY #<-

[...it is believed] <note>original: *tur*; likely completing a verb like *arbitrantur* from the previous page</note>, as some think, that one should not labor to acquire these virtues, because God does not listen to the lazy and the sluggish; indeed, He rather rejects their prayers as blasphemies. It would certainly be a great madness for men to ask God for bread, yet be unwilling to plow and sow; it is no less a madness to desire the **mind** <note>The author uses 'mind' (*mens*) to refer to a state of enlightened or philosophical intellect.</note> and wait for it while applying no diligence at all toward its acquisition. 

But the greatest madness of all would be if someone were so ignorant as to think that this could be acquired by labor alone, without first imploring the **grace of God**. He fills the diligent with His blessing while they seek what is healthful, but God does not hear the negligent, even if they ask. One is said to truly "ask" not only with the mouth, but with the heart, or when one demands something with true devotion.

Therefore, before we proceed to the first step of **philosophy**, I thought the students should be warned to implore divine aid, and thereafter to use the most accurate **diligence** in disposing themselves to receive this kind of grace. This treatise discusses only this "disposition" <note>In this context, 'disposition' refers to the state of being prepared or "tuned" to receive wisdom.</note>; however, grace must be sought from above, from the Giver of Lights, **God, the Best and Greatest** <term>D.O.M.</term> <note>Abbreviation for *Deo Optimo Maximo*, a standard dedication to God in Renaissance and Early Modern texts.</note>. 

Nor should anyone think that I have defined the mind in a way other than how Paul teaches in the same words: that **faith** must be sought from God alone, not from one’s own strengths, nor from anywhere else but from God Himself. And because there are three principal parts of this crafted preparation—namely, **physics**, **moral**, and **metaphysical**—it was necessary to proceed by each path in this discussion, as will be more clearly seen by readers hereafter.

->### SPECULATIVE PHILOSOPHY ###<-

...it is [unfitting] for a man of an evil life to possess the treasure hidden for the sons of wisdom; such a person is of unsound health for seeking that treasure, let alone finding it. This is the reason why we speak of correcting corrupt morals and physical bodies through **metaphysical medicines**. Within the human body lies hidden a certain **metaphysical substance** <term>metaphysical substance</term> <note>In the context of alchemical medicine, this refers to a spiritual essence or "internal balsam" that sustains life and resists decay, similar to the concept of the *archeus*.</note>, known to very few, which requires no medicine whatsoever from the outside; rather, it is itself an incorruptible medicine. 

But because this substance is overwhelmed by the corruptions of physical bodies and is hindered from carrying out its perfecting work, philosophers—through a certain divine inspiration <note>original: *divino quodam afflatu*</note>—learned that this virtue and heavenly vigor could be liberated from its chains. This is achieved not by its opposite—as standard physical medicine teaches—but by its own likeness. Therefore, when something is found, whether within man or outside of him, that conforms to this substance, the wise have concluded that **likes should be strengthened by likes** <note>original: *similia similibus*; a core principle in both alchemy and later homeopathy, suggesting that a thing is healed by its own essence</note>, through peace rather than war, and that contraries should be driven out by contraries. 

From this, it is clear that there is a threefold medicine for those prone to failing: namely, the **metaphysical**, the **physical**, and the **moral**. For the prepared <note>original: *artificiata*; meaning a state improved by the "art" of philosophy or alchemy</note> disposition of the physical body, we employ a metaphysical remedy. Through this, we restrain its corruption and guide it toward natural digestion and expulsion; nature performs this work through a **form** that has been strengthened from within. This is the preparation of our physical body performed in a metaphysical manner, through which we arrive more easily at the separation...

It argues that this substance is freed from physical corruption not through opposing forces, but through the application of its "like," establishing a threefold system of healing: metaphysical, physical, and moral.</summary>

# PHILOSOPHY #

...the mind <note>original: *animus*; the OCR begins with the end of this word, likely completing a thought from the previous page about the spirit being freed from bodily corruption.</note> remains without the hindrance of corrupting factors. From this, the diligent reader will gather that a transition must be made from **metaphysics**, through **alchemy**, to **physics**, by working philosophically. 

For what does it benefit anyone to have heard of the arts in the Universities, or to have read of them in private, unless they are brought from potential into action? Is such knowledge any more useful than the "hello" <note>original: *χαῖρε* (chaire)</note> taught to a parrot? Certainly not: because all knowledge that is not proven by **experience** <term>experience</term> <note>In the 17th-century context, *experientia* often referred to "experiment" or practical alchemical laboratory work, rather than just general life experience.</note> is not a true art, but rather a clinging ignorance. 

Now let us proceed to **voluntary separation**, which is perfected through **moral philosophy**. Once the preparation of the body is complete, it will be easily separated from its remaining parts. This occurs when the soul—persuaded by the mind or spirit—denies the body whatever it hungers for beyond "philosophical necessity." (What constitutes "philosophical necessity" will be explained later). From this separation results the **first union**: namely, the union of the spirit with the soul, achieved through the continued study of moral philosophy. 

These things had to be proposed to students first, so that they might recognize the reasoning by which I have mixed metaphysics here with physics and ethics. It is important to know when moral matters should be compared to metaphysical ones, and when these should be compared to physics. I explain this now lest I seem to have blocked the entrance for my readers, 
->leaving them stuck in a labyrinth<-
->(as many authors are accustomed to do)<-
->rather than helping them grasp the matter.<-

# *On philosophical study, and the first stage.*
## CHAPTER II.

The study of philosophers is the diligent inquiry into what is **true** and what is **necessary**. The **True** is that essence from which nothing can be absent, to which nothing can be added, and which much less can be hindered. The **Necessary** is all that which we cannot be without. Truth, therefore, is the highest virtue and an impregnable fortress; it has very few friends dwelling within, but is besieged by countless enemies. It is now hated by almost the whole world, yet it remains an insuperable pledge to those who possess it. 

Within this citadel, the true and indubitable **philosopher's stone** <term>philosopher's stone</term> <note>In this context, the "stone" is presented as a spiritual or intellectual treasure—the ultimate goal of the philosopher's quest—rather than just a physical substance for making gold.</note> and treasure are contained. This treasure is not eaten away by moths, nor can it be dug up by thieves; it remains for eternity while all other things dissolve. It is placed as a ruin for many, but for others, it is salvation. 

To the common crowd, this matter is the most worthless thing, greatly despised and hated. Yet it is not truly hateful, but rather lovable and precious to philosophers, valued above gems and **refined gold** <note>original: *aurum obrizon*; a term for gold of the highest purity.</note>. It is the lover of all, yet an enemy to almost all; it is found everywhere, but found by very few—hardly any. It cries out to everyone through the streets: "Come to me, all you who seek, and I will lead you into the true path." 

This is that thing spoken of only by true philosophers, which conquers all things and is conquered by nothing. It penetrates every hard and solid body and heart, yet it consolidates <note>To "consolidate" here means to make firm or stable.</note> everything soft, strengthening it to resist all that is hard. It places itself in the way of us all, yet we do not see it, shouting and saying in a loud voice: "I am the way of tru-"
