->PHILOSOPHY.<-

...of truth, pass through me: because there is no other passage to life, and yet we do not want to hear her. She emits an **odor of sweetness**, but we do not perceive it. Daily she offers herself freely to you as a feast in all her sweetness, yet we do not taste her. Gently she draws us toward salvation, and though we resist her pull, we do not wish to feel it. For we have become like stones, **having eyes and not seeing, having ears and not hearing**, having noses that do not smell, equipped with a mouth and tongue but neither tasting nor speaking, and with hands and feet doing nothing and not walking. <note>This is a direct allusion to Psalm 115 (or 135), which describes the sensory-deprived nature of idols; here, the author applies it to people who ignore spiritual truth.</note> Oh, what a wretched race of men, which is no better than stones—nay, far inferior to them—because it is these men, and not the stones, who must give an account of their works!

"Be transformed," she says, "be transformed from dead stones into living **philosophical stones**." <term>philosophical stones</term> <note>In alchemical philosophy, the "living stone" represents a person whose soul has been perfected and purified, contrasting with the "dead stone" of unrefined material or an unawakened mind.</note> I am the **true medicine**, correcting and transforming that which no longer is, into that which was before **corruption**—and even better—and transforming that which is not, into that which ought to be. Behold, I am at the doors of your **conscience**, knocking night and day, and do you not open to me? <note>A reference to Revelation 3:20: "Behold, I stand at the door and knock."</note> Yet I wait mildly, nor do I depart from you in anger, but patiently I endure your injuries, desiring through patience to lead you by exhorting you to her. Come again, and come again more often, you who seek **wisdom**, and buy for free—not with gold nor silver, and least of all by your own labors—that which is offered to you of its own accord. This is a resonant voice, sweet and pleasing to those practicing philosophy...

# SPECULATIVE [PHILOSOPHY]

...ears. O inexhaustible **fountain of riches** for those thirsting for **truth and justice**! O solace for the imperfection of the desolate! Why do you seek further, anxious mortals? Why do you wretched ones agitate your souls with infinite cares? What madness blinds you, I ask? Since everything that you seek outside yourselves—which is not with you—is actually within you, though not *of* you.

It is usually the particular vice of the common crowd to despise what is their own and always crave what belongs to others. Here we take the term "one's own" <term>proprium</term> <note>original: "proprium"; in this context, it refers to an essential quality or property that belongs to a person's nature.</note> to mean what is appropriated for us. For from ourselves we have nothing good; but if we are able to have any good, we receive it from Him who alone is good. On the contrary, whatever evil we have, we have appropriated for ourselves from an external evil through **disobedience**. 

Therefore, nothing is truly a man’s own from his own self, except for the evil he possesses. Whatever good he has from the Good, he does not have from himself, but possesses as a contributed property from the Good, provided he receives it. The **light of men** <note>original: "lux hominum"; a reference to John 1:4. This "life" or "light" is presented here as the divine spark within the soul.</note> which is life, shines in us (though dimly) as if in the darkness. It is not from us, and yet it must be sought *in* us—though not *from* us, but from Him to whom it belongs. He planted it in us so that in His light—He who dwells in **inaccessible light**—we might see light, and in this way excel His other creatures. We were made like Him for this reason: that He gave us a **spark** <note>The "scintilla" or divine spark is a common theme in mystical philosophy, representing the part of the human soul that remains connected to the Creator.</note> of His light. Therefore, truth is not to be sought in ourselves, but in the **image of God** <term>imago Dei</term> which is within us. Furthermore, to satisfy the definition of the true, we say

<meta>This page continues the discourse of Philosophy from page 92. The text transitions from the previous page's discussion on the "inaccessible light" of the Divine to the nature of "Being" itself. # PHILOSOPHY

...**to be** indeed means that nothing else is present; for what, I ask you, is "present" to the One? What could it possibly lack, or what could strive against it? For nothing truly exists except for that one. Who is ignorant of the fact that nothing is perfect except that One? It is not diminished in any part, nor does it allow for anything superfluous; rather, it is so abundant in power and virtue that it can grant from its own abundance to those who are failing, and yet always remain equally full. 

For what else could be such a thing, except that alone which gives "being" <term>esse</term> <note>original: "esse"; the act of existing or the essence of being.</note> to everything that exists, yet receives its own being from no one else? Instead, it is Being itself in its entirety—all that can possibly be—which nothing can hinder, because outside of it, there is nothing. 

That which is "nothing" truly does not exist; and conversely, that which truly does not exist is nothing. Whatever stands outside of truth truly does not exist, because **Truth** is that "One" which is everything that truly is. **Falsehood**, therefore—which is nothing other than the contrary of true being, and which ought not to exist, nor does it exist except through the deprivation of true essence—is **nothing**, because it truly is not. 

From this, true philosophers conclude that **sin and death are nothing** <note>This is the classical philosophical argument of "privatio boni" (the privation of good). It suggests that evil and death do not have their own independent substance, but are merely the absence of life and goodness, just as darkness is the absence of light.</note>, because they do not proceed from true being, but from the deprivation of true essence. Sin, therefore, is the deprivation of the Good, which acts as the "efficient cause" of death; for death is the deprivation of life, and that life is the goal of the Good. 

Indeed, from the good of truth, a goad <note>original: "stimulus"; a reference to Acts 9:5, "It is hard for thee to kick against the pricks/goads."</note> arises, against which the stings of death shall not prevail, and against which it is hard for anyone, no matter how hardened they are, to kick. Whoever would wish to add anything to the truth would be forced to substitute a lie in place of the truth by using a kind of disguise, for the reason that truth...

### THEORETICAL PHILOSOPHY
<meta>catchword: worldly</meta>

...she [Truth] is not afraid, supported only by the cloak of perfection. But if that first parent of ours <note>Adam, the first man in the Genesis narrative.</note> had not stripped off this cloak of justice, he would not have been afraid to be seen naked—as he did not appear before, nor was he truly naked, but rather was fortified with every beauty. Therefore, Adam, once deprived of the eternal cloak of truth, sought a false covering <term>pallium</term> <note>original: "pallium"; a Greek-style cloak, here used metaphorically for a superficial covering or excuse.</note> from the leaves of trees. By this act, he confessed his error within himself and said before the Lord: "I am naked, Lord." 

God, taking pity on him because he feared and confessed that he had lost the truth, decided to restore to him freely, through his own Son, the wedding garment of eternal glory <note>A reference to the "Parable of the Wedding Garment" (Matthew 22:11–13), symbolizing the righteousness required to enter heaven.</note> in place of the lost cloak of earthly happiness. It was the Son who said and showed that he is the "way and the life" of truth for us, and by his own death, he took away from us the perpetual death by which we all deserved to be punished. 

See then, brothers, how great the effect of truth is: she is kind and peaceful even toward her enemies, provided they recognize her. We all sinned with Adam, but there are very few—and almost none—who openly confess their nakedness as he did. On the contrary, every person desires not only to appear, but to be "more perfect," even though he himself is the most imperfect of all. Such a person is most naked even when cloaked in fine linen and purple <note>original: "bysso purpuráque"; a classical and biblical reference to extreme wealth and high status (cf. Luke 16:19).</note>. 

We inherited this vice from the first appetite of our first parent: the fact that our flesh, more than anything else, desires to be wise, to possess, or to be honored—whether by right or by wrong. This is the most destructive pursuit of men in this age. Truth, once again, is subject to no power, or worldly...

-> # PHILOSOPHY <-

...to divine fortitude <note>The text likely began on the previous page with a phrase like "given over to divine fortitude."</note>; for this reason, nothing hinders her, for she herself is true fortitude, overcoming the power of the whole world. Because of this, if the body of one who possesses the truth is cut into a thousand pieces, his soul suffers nothing; rather, he rejoices in that martyrdom. This is the power of truth, which few have attained, but below you will hear more extensively of its excellence. 

Now we shall speak further on what properties truth possesses: namely, piety joined with justice in an inseparable bond. For piety is a grace given from God, which teaches each person to truly know themselves. Justice, indeed, is said to be the rendering to each person what is their own <term>suum cuique</term> <note>original: "retributio cuiusque cuique quod suum est"; a classic legal and philosophical definition of justice dating back to Roman law, meaning to give everyone their due.</note>. Whoever possesses these virtues is the wealthiest of all. It must also be known in this place that piety produces peace and mercy, which will be discussed in their proper place. 

It remains now for us to look at the other part of philosophical study, which is necessity. Indeed, we cannot do without food and clothing; therefore, they are necessary to us. However, anyone—even one who possesses them—is able to do without delicacies in food and drink, or the luxury of clothing made of fine linen, scarlet, or purple <note>In the ancient and medieval world, fine linen (byssus), scarlet, and purple dyes were the ultimate symbols of extreme wealth and high social status.</note>; therefore, these things are not necessary. 

There are few who obscure philosophical necessity; but who among the common crowd, or even any unlearned person, does not endure common necessity unwillingly, and desires to be without only that which he cannot attain? The common crowd judges all things superficially according to appearance; there are almost none who judge according to depth. From this it happens that truth is hated when she comes forth into the light beneath a torn husk <note>original: "lacero cortice"; a metaphor suggesting that profound truths often appear in humble, unattractive, or "broken" outward forms that the worldly-minded despise.</note>. More powerful...

Tell me, I pray, you men of judgment, how can it be that a Pagan, lacking entirely the true light, can bring light to others? -> # PHILOSOPHY # <-

[pseudo-philoso]pher, when comparing his lying self with a true speculative Wise Man <note>original: "Sopho"; from the Greek 'Sophos', meaning a sage or wise person</note>, immediately breaks out into insults, despising every good and true thing that he has not heard from his own teacher: as if there were nothing good or true except that which had proceeded from the Gentiles <note>In this context, "Gentiles" refers to the non-Christian classical Greek and Roman philosophers.</note> who were ignorant of the truth. For a speculative Mind, aided by the true light of truth, can achieve more in a single year or even a moment than the constant and tireless labor of a man's whole life without that light, seeking light from those who lack all light themselves. 

But let us see what the doctrine of the pagan philosophers is. They place the **highest good** <term>summum bonum: the ultimate goal of human existence</term> in **living well**, which they divide into the **honorable** <term>honestum: what is morally right or virtuous</term> and the **useful** <term>utile: what is practical or profitable</term>. They consider the ambition for honors and praise to be the height of honor; finally, the acquisition and preservation of wealth—attained by any right or wrong, by war or by sweat—they call "useful" things. From these, they expect nothing after death except to have left the memory and fame of themselves to posterity. 

Partly denying and partly ignorant of the **Resurrection**, they fashion a thousand trifles of poetry instead of the truth (of which they were indeed devoid), to the perpetual destruction of men and of themselves. Behold, because of this doctrine, the professors of the true arts must be most hated by those who bear the greatest reputation for having achieved the most in various arts and faculties. Even though experience places the truth before their eyes, [it remains hidden] unless it be through the inexperienced opinions of those who have never experienced the truth...

# SPECULATIVE KNOWLEDGE #

[proven], truth is cast aside as false, and he who wishes to profess the truth is judged a fool with a great outcry. Indeed, if someone rightly detests empty authors, their disciples in the **Universities** immediately threaten to afflict them with punishment or the greatest disgrace—what they call **donkey-riding** <term>equitatio asini: a ritual of public humiliation where a person was forced to ride backward on a donkey through the streets, often used to shame those who challenged institutional norms</term>. Yet meanwhile, they themselves do not see that it is their own daily comfort to ride donkeys through the streets—those very beasts of burden with which they attempt to disgrace others. <note>The author uses a clever pun here, implying that the academic "disciples" are themselves the fools or "asses" they use to shame others.</note> 

Shall truth then be kept silent because, as the **Gentiles** <note>original: "gentiles"; referring here to the pagan Greek and Roman philosophers</note> teach, "truth breeds hatred"? Not at all; rather, truth teaches a philosopher to face death rather than deny the truth. Truth must be observed in everything as the **highest good** <term>summum bonum: the ultimate moral or spiritual goal</term> among Christians. Blessed are those to whom it is granted from heaven to persevere in truth until the end—which (alas, how wretched!) happens to very few. Let us pray to God to make us partakers of this truth alone. Therefore, we must conclude that truth alone is to be desired and carefully sought in all things. Listen now to how one must leap further toward its summit.

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of traditional academic authorities, specifically targeting the followers of Aristotle. In the previous section, the author argued that truth is often suppressed by those in power; here, he explains that the true "chemical art" (alchemy) requires a foundation in actual truth rather than the logical word-games of the university Sophists.</meta>

# PHILOSOPHY #

[judged] by their character. Whoever wishes to learn the **chemical art** <term>chymicam artem: at this time, "chemistry" and "alchemy" were not yet distinct fields; the term refers to the study of the hidden properties of matter and nature</term> must learn philosophy—not the Aristotelian kind, but that which teaches the truth. Some disciple of **Aristotle** might say: "Am I making Aristotle out to be a liar?" Not I, but he has made himself one. Indeed, his entire doctrine consists of **amphibology** <term>amphibologia: a manner of speaking that is intentionally ambiguous or has a double meaning, allowing the speaker to avoid a definitive stance</term>, which is the finest cloak for lies. Since he himself had criticized **Plato** and the others for the sake of seeking fame, he could find no more convenient instrument than the very one he had used in his criticizing—namely, ambiguous speech. He saved his writings from an attack on the left by using a loophole on the right, and vice versa. One can see this kind of **Sophism** <note>sophismata: clever but fallacious arguments used to deceive or mislead</note> in all his writings; they can be interpreted in either direction by anyone even moderately learned. 

This has always been the aim of the **Sophists**: to devise certain loopholes by which, once they are defeated, they can cover up their falsehoods. They flee to these as if to a **sacred anchor** <note>original: "sacram anchoram"; an ancient idiom referring to the last resort or final hope in a moment of peril</note> when they are in danger. Such is that **shield of donkeys** <note>original: "asinorum ille clypeus"; a mocking term for a logical defense used by those the author considers foolish</note> where they say: **"One must not dispute with those who deny first principles."** <note>original: "Contra negantem principia non est disputandum"; a standard rule in medieval logic meaning you cannot argue with someone who rejects the fundamental premises of a debate</note> It was not without reason that they built this rampart, for their arts consisted of false principles. There is no better way to act against the Sophists than by examining their principles with great insight to see if they are false; they try to ensnare any unwary person in this **net** <note>original: "retilitio"; a term for a snare or web of words</note>. But let us set these matters aside until they must be debated and further proven in writing. Let us return to the **chemical study**.

E ii <margin>Signature mark for the book binder</margin>

<summary>The author rejects Aristotelian philosophy as a foundation for alchemy, accusing Aristotle and his followers of using ambiguous language and logical fallacies to hide their lack of truth. He argues that the student of nature must look past these academic "loopholes" to find genuine principles.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the transition from the previous critique of Aristotelian "sophistry" toward a positive definition of true knowledge. -> S P E C V L A T I V E  [M A T T E R S] <-

Truth is sought in the **chemical citadel** <note>original: "arce chymica"; the author envisions alchemy as a fortified stronghold of truth</note> through manual practice and **Pyromancy** <term>Pyromantiam: while often meaning divination by fire, here it refers to the alchemical art of using heat and furnaces to transform matter</term>, once the parts have been separated from their whole; this is the study of **Alchemists**.

### *On the second degree of philosophers, which is called Knowledge.* Chapter III.

**Knowledge** <term>Cognitio: a deep, certain understanding that goes beyond mere data</term> is the certain and indubitable resolution of opinions conceived about any truth, achieved through experience. **Opinion** is a doubtful presumption of truth clinging to the mind. **Experience** is the manifest demonstration of truth. **Resolution**, indeed, is the setting aside of doubt. We cannot become certain of any doubt in any way other than by experiencing it, and no better than within ourselves. Therefore, let us verify that which we have said above concerning truth, making our beginning from ourselves.

We said above that **Piety** <note>original: "Pietatem"; here, piety is not just religious devotion but an essential prerequisite for true philosophical insight</note> consists in the knowledge of oneself. From this, it follows that we should begin philosophical knowledge from [self-]knowledge itself. No one can truly know themselves unless they know *what*—and not just *who*—they are; upon whom they depend, or to whom they belong (for according to the law of truth, no one is their own master), and for what purpose they were made. Once these things are known, **Piety** begins, which revolves around two things: namely, the **Creator**, and the **creature** <note>original: "creaturam"; in the 17th century, this term referred to any created thing, specifically a human being in this context</note> similar to oneself. It is impossible for a creature to know itself by itself, unless its Creator is first known. How can the effect be known before the cause? Does not every beginning precede its middle and its end, or shall anyone approach a matter from the end?

<meta>catchword: God [Deus]</meta>

<summary>The author defines true knowledge as the resolution of doubt through experience, particularly the experience of the self. He argues that alchemy and self-reflection are the paths to truth, which ultimately requires an understanding of one's relationship to the Creator.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, pyromancy, knowledge (cognitio), experience, piety, self-knowledge, Creator and creature, causality</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the theological and philosophical foundation of the work. Having established that true knowledge begins with self-reflection and piety, the author now defines the relationship between God, the Creator (Artifex), and humanity, who is fashioned from "vile" matter yet destined for glory. This sets the stage for the alchemical metaphor of transformation from base to precious.</meta>

# PHILOSOPHY.

E iij

God is without beginning and end, existing through himself, filled with all glory, which, out of his own generosity <note>original: "liberalitate"; in this context, it implies a bountiful, overflowing grace</note>, he did not wish to possess alone, but to make us sharers in it, whom he created in his own image: the first exemplar and mystery of his generosity. How is it, then, that we are ignorant of that wondrous **Secret** <note>original: "Arcanum"; a term often used in alchemy to describe hidden, divine truths of nature</note> in truth? Listen, brother: just as we were created from the most vile [matter] by the most precious [Being], so, because of the matter from which we were made, we are more prone to anything vile than toward that which, after himself, made us most precious from the vile: indeed, we were made greater than the angels, had we remained in such a state of dignity.

Wonder, all you wretched mortals who are with me, that the highest **craftsman** <term>artifex: a term characterizing God as a master artisan or alchemist who "works" the material of the world</term> of all things wished to handle with his hands the most vile matter from the mud of the earth, and from it raise up the most precious creature. Let us not think this was done without purpose, but so that we might recognize what we truly are, and restrain ourselves from the pride of arrogance. When we act otherwise, every one of us hates the poor man whom God has chosen, even though it is certain he is made from the same **mass** <note>original: "massa"; referring to the "lump" of clay or primal matter from which all humans are formed</note> from which we all were made. From this it follows that God is no respecter of persons, but a lover of poverty and humility, and an enemy of lofty pride.

No one can know the Creator better than through the creature, just as the craftsman is known by his work. Let us see, then, who he is that can raise the most precious from the most vile; surely none besides him who created both. In no way can a **cupbearer** <note>original: "pincerna"; likely a metaphor for a mere servant or secondary agent compared to the Master</note> change wine into water...

### SPECULATIVA

[...to die], yet he who created wine and water is able to do this: something greater, when he **transmuted** <term>transmutavit: a term suggesting a fundamental change in nature or substance, central to both theology (the soul) and alchemy (metals)</term> earth into a living soul, he did it; and what is greatest of all, he endowed [humanity] with the supreme gift of blessedness, namely, with his own **image and likeness**. Through these gifts, it is granted to all to be saved who receive this gift, but to those unwilling to receive it freely, what shall be given? Certainly nothing: let them have, therefore, what is their own, so that the place of justice may be preserved. 

What truly belongs to each person is what they chose from the beginning, namely, damnation. The gift of eternal salvation is free for those who receive it with thanksgiving. Therefore, since we are a price both **vile** and **dear** <note>original: "vile carumque"; reflecting the paradox of man as both lowly mud and a precious divine creation</note>, let each of us see what he would rather choose: for the matter is clearer in itself to those who understand than it would be by needing further explanation. We know how vile we are, and how most excellent is He from whom we proceeded: it remains in this matter that we consider why, or for what end, we were created.

Almighty God created the first man, **Adam**, in his own image and likeness, so that he might exist for God himself in eternity for glory, and might be immortal, without anxiety, lacking nothing, always rejoicing in God, and not subject to earthly passions. But because he did not recognize so great a gift, he **held it cheap** <note>original: "vilifecit"; literally "to make vile," meaning to treat something of great value as if it were worthless</note>: he knew, nonetheless, that he had been made perfect and wise, yet he did not remain in that knowledge. Let us consider, therefore, how much good knowledge brings, and how much evil comes from the disparagement of this gift and the word of God, since from being immortal it rendered [man] perpetually... <note>The text cuts off here, continuing the thought of the transition from immortality to death on the following page.</note>

-># PHILOSOPHY<-

...[dying]. For he who dies eternally and lives always in the agony of death never ceases to die. From this, it follows that death is called "perpetual," just as that life is called "eternal" in which we live for God forever and never cease to live. Therefore, since our **first parent** <note>original: "primus parens"; referring to Adam</note> made all of us rebels and enemies against God along with himself, almighty God—the author of peace—was moved by **mercy** and wished to enter into peace with us, even though we were hardened. 

Who would be so stony-hearted that, when he has turned this mystery of divine goodness over in his mind, he would not be reconciled with his own enemy, even if he has received an injury from him? How does it happen at last that among mortals, neither the one who offends nor the one who suffered the offense is willing to approach the other for the sake of reconciliation? 

Certainly for no other reason than that no one knows himself, and consequently, neither knows God his creator, nor his neighbor—a **creature of equal worth** <term>æquipollentem creaturam: the idea that all humans share the same divine origin and dignity</term>. Thus, he who is ignorant of peace does not embrace it. For peace is the root of mercy. For he who does not have peace will not exercise mercy, nor even attain it, until he possesses peace. We should not confuse peace and mercy in this place; because peace is the cause of mercy, just as **piety** <note>original: "pietas"; here meaning religious devotion or dutifulness toward God</note> itself is the cause of peace. 

Therefore, if we have peace with God through the knowledge of piety, we must necessarily have it likewise with our neighbor, whether enemy or friend. This is especially true when we consider that the supreme **Architect** <term>artifex: a common Renaissance term for God as the craftsman of the universe</term> of all things reached out to man—who was but a little worm <note>original: "vermiculo"; a traditional expression of human humility before the divine</note> and a slave to the devil—through the embrace of peace...

### SPECULATIVE THEOLOGY

...has shown His mercy. O wretched mortals, as many of us as expect mercy from God, before whom we do not acknowledge our own wretchedness! From where, I ask, does **Mercy** <note>original: "Misericordia"; the author is playing on the etymology: *miser* (wretched) + *cor* (heart)</note> come, if not from the confession of wretchedness, born through contrition of heart leading to the amendment of one's life? 

If indeed we are not ignorant of our own wretchedness and that of our brothers, but weigh it with an equal balance, then even if we become wealthier in the goods of this world, we will not consider ourselves more abundant in eternal riches than those who are utterly destitute of worldly wealth. Who among mortals truly considers this? And who, by sharing the goods entrusted to him alone in this world, would not console a needy brother? Give me one who considers this, and I will soon show you one who recognizes such a **divine secret** <note>original: "divinum arcanum"; referring to the hidden wisdom of God's providence and the spiritual equality of all people</note>. 

In all these things, God did not yet wish to fully explain His love toward us. But while His **Justice** <note>original: "Iustitia"</note> accused us—a justice which can never be diminished any more than His mercy—He wished to spare us rather than His own Son, in whom **Peace** and **Justice** have kissed each other <note>A reference to Psalm 85:10, a popular verse in medieval and Renaissance theology to explain how God's demand for justice and His desire for mercy were reconciled through Christ</note>. 

Why then do we, having been made equal brothers in **Christ**, not embrace one another, since the supreme **Creator** <note>original: "artifex"; literally "craftsman" or "architect" of the universe</note> of all things embraces us, wretched little worms? In His only-begotten Son, He makes us the most excellent creatures out of the vilest ones, though we were made vile by our own evil. As many as acknowledge God in the Son, and likewise the Son in the Father through the **Holy Spirit** (whom they do not resist), they also acknowledge their brother, and no others.

# PHILOSOPHY

These are the true and indubitable foundations of **true philosophy** <note>original: "veræ philosophiæ"; here referring to a spiritual or Neoplatonic wisdom rather than mere secular logic</note>. However, because these truths are not yet known by everyone—even though they have been heard by many—a doubt might still linger in the minds of those who are not yet firmly resolved. They may wonder whether simply imagining these truths can actually stir a genuine love for the truth within us. 

Therefore, it would be a worthwhile endeavor for such people to set aside this uncertainty by conducting an experiment by themselves and within themselves, in the following manner: 

Let each person carefully consider within themselves those things that were discussed above. Once they have "tasted" these ideas, let them ruminate upon them frequently with a sincere mind. In doing so, they will perceive, little by little, certain sparks beginning to shine more and more each day before their **mental eyes** <note>original: "oculis mentalibus"; a common metaphor for the intellect's ability to perceive spiritual truth</note>. Eventually, these sparks will grow into such a great light that, over time, whatever things are necessary for them will become known. They will no longer hesitate regarding the truth in any way. 

Instead, by gradually becoming accustomed to the exercise of **piety** <term>pietas: a sense of duty and religious devotion</term> and **justice** <term>iustitia: right conduct toward others</term>, following the rite and the path previously described, they will feel a tranquility of the soul. Drawn in by this peace, they will finally understand that there is a far greater delight to be found in the spirit than in the physical body. 

**True knowledge** <note>original: "vera cognitio"</note> does not truly begin until a person—having compared the eternal with the fleeting, and life with death—chooses for the **Soul** <term>Anima: the life-giving soul</term> to be joined with the **Spirit** <term>Animus: the rational or higher mind</term>, being drawn by the greater delight of the latter over the **Body**. 

From this recognition, the **Mind** <term>Mens: the highest, divine part of human intellect</term> is born, and the voluntary separation from the body begins. This occurs when the soul looks, on one hand, at the foulness and death of the body, and on the other hand, at the excellence and perpetual happiness of the spirit, and with that...

(with the divine state so disposing) it desires to be joined, while the other is completely neglected, so that it seeks only what it sees has been determined by God for salvation and glory. The body is then compelled to descend into the union of these two that are now joined. This is that wonderful **transmutation of the philosophers** <term>transmutatio: the alchemical process of changing one substance into another; here used to describe the spiritualizing of the physical body</term> of body into spirit, and spirit into body, concerning which the Sages have left us this saying: **make the fixed volatile, and the volatile fixed** <note>original: "fac fixum volatile, & volatile fac fixū"; a fundamental alchemical axiom. In this context, it means making the physical body "fluid" or receptive to the spirit, and making the fleeting spirit "stable" or manifest within the body</note>, so that you may possess our **mastery** <term>magisterium: the "Great Work" or the highest achievement of the alchemical art</term>.

*(Understand this to mean)*: make from the stubborn body something manageable, so that through the excellence of the mind agreeing with the soul, it becomes a most stable body, able to endure all trials. For gold is tested by fire, by which everything that is not gold is rejected. O most excellent **gold of the philosophers** <note>original: "philosophorū aurum"; referring to spiritual perfection and divine wisdom rather than the physical metal</note>, by which the children of wisdom are enriched—not by that gold which is commonly craved! 

Come hither, you who seek the treasure of the philosophers with such varied effort: first recognize what that **rejected stone** <note>original: "reprobatū... lapidē"; a double reference to the "stone the builders rejected" from the Bible and the "Philosopher's Stone" of alchemy</note> is, before you seek it. It is a wonder beyond all miracles that anyone should desire something unknown to them. It certainly seems foolish for men to seek a thing whose true nature they do not understand while they are investigating it, for in such a search, no hope remains.

I therefore advise anyone searching to first understand the true existence of what they seek before they begin their search; in this way, they will not be frustrated in their labors. The wise man seeks what he loves, and he cannot love what he does not know; otherwise, he would be a fool. From knowledge, therefore, is born the love of all truth...

### Recapitulation of the Second Level. CHAPTER III.

No one should think that I wish to discuss theological matters here, but rather those things which should not be hidden from a common person <note>original: "rusticum"; literally a peasant or rustic man</note> or even children. Yet, for those who search deeper than the literal meaning, the **Sons of Wisdom** <term>Sapientiæ filii: a traditional term for alchemists or those initiated into divine knowledge</term> draw all science and art from these things—both in matters celestial and those that are worldly. In vain do you all labor, seekers of the hidden secrets of Nature, when you enter by a different path and try to discover the virtues of earthly things through earthly means.

Learn, therefore, to know heaven through heaven, and not through the earth; rather, learn to know the virtues of the earth through the heaven. For no one ascends into the Heaven which you seek, unless he who descended from the Heaven (which you do not seek) illuminates him. <note>A paraphrase of the Gospel of John 3:13, applied here to alchemical and intellectual illumination.</note> You seek an **incorruptible medicine** <term>incorruptibilem medicinam: the Elixir of Life or the Philosopher's Stone, believed to cure all disease and grant longevity</term> which might not only transform bodies from a state of decay into a true balance <note>original: "temperamentum"; in Renaissance medicine, the healthy balance of the four humors</note>, but also preserve that balance for the longest possible time. You will never be able to find such a thing anywhere else but in heaven.

Heaven, by its own power, penetrates all elements through invisible rays that converge from every direction into the center of the earth; it generates and nourishes everything made of the elements. Nothing can reproduce within itself, but only in something like itself which also comes from it. Even the mixed offspring retains the nature of both parents within it, so that both parents can be found there in both potential and in reality. Who but a "stone" <note>A pun on "lapis." It implies someone as dense as a rock, while also alluding to the "Philosopher's Stone" (Lapis Philosophorum) being discussed.</note> would hesitate any longer regarding this **philosophical generation**? If this knowledge is not yet enough for you, friend reader, since it...

<summary>This page explains that true natural philosophy requires looking to the celestial (heavenly) realm to understand the earthly one. It argues that the "incorruptible medicine" sought by alchemists is derived from heavenly influences rather than purely physical materials.</summary>

<keywords>Sons of Wisdom, secrets of Nature, celestial influence, incorruptible medicine, philosophical generation, alchemical theory, macrocosm and microcosm</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Recapitulation of the Second Level" from Chapter III and begins Chapter IV. The text emphasizes the microcosm-macrocosm relationship, asserting that the seeker must find the unity of heaven and earth within themselves before they can succeed in the "Great Work."</meta>

->**SPECULATIVE PHILOSOPHY**<-

...which was given in our **Key** <term>Clavis: a common title for alchemical or esoteric treatises, functioning as a "key" to unlock hidden meanings</term>, there is no reason for me to discuss it with you more extensively. But lest you have any handle <note>original: "ansam"; a pretext or opportunity</note> for complaining against me, I will speak further. Learn to know from yourself whatever is both in heaven and on earth, so that you may become wise in all things. Are you unaware that heaven and the elements were once one, and were separated from each other by divine craftsmanship so that they could generate both you and all things? 

If you know this, the rest cannot escape you, or else you lack all intellect. Again, in every generation <note>here meaning the act of bringing something into being</note>, such a separation is necessary—the same kind I said above must be performed upon yourself before you set your sail toward the studies of true philosophy. From other things you will never make the "one" which you seek, unless that which you have heard first becomes "one" within yourself; you shall hear more of this in the following pages. For such is the **will of God** <term>voluntas Dei</term>, that the pious may attain the pious work they seek, and the perfect may complete the other task upon which they were intent. To men of evil will, nothing is given to reap except what they have sown; indeed, what is more, their good seed is very often turned into weeds <note>original: "lolium"; likely a reference to the biblical "tares" or darnel that chokes out wheat</note> because of their malice. Therefore, ensure that you become such a person as you wish your sought-after work to be.

### *Concerning the third philosophical level. CHAPTER IV.*

**Philosophical love** is said to be the union of secrets from one, back into one through peace. Peace, indeed, is the harmony through the union of parts in the whole. God loves union above all, because He is one and what...

<summary>The author explains that the alchemist must mirror the divine process of separation and unification within their own soul to succeed. It concludes by introducing the Third Level of philosophy, defined by the concept of "Philosophical Love" and the restoration of unity.</summary>

->PHILOSOPHY. 109<-

...whatever He ever created, He enclosed under unity. From one determinate thing <note>original: "Ab vno determinato"; likely referring to the Monad or the First Cause</note>, made through one indefinite thing, proceed all things that are finite, and likewise each thing tends back toward its own unity by a natural instinct. If any things should decline from this unity, they are considered by philosophers to be held under the **secret binary** <term>binario: the number two, which in alchemical philosophy represents division, duality, and the separation of the soul from its divine source</term>, and they must be led back to unity through the **ternary** <term>ternarium: the number three, representing the reconciliation of opposites and the restoration of balance</term>. God created heaven and earth: and all things that are in them, united together in a hidden way, yet separate in a visible way. Under the name of "earth" are included the **Elements** <note>the four classical elements: Earth, Water, Air, and Fire</note>, which are contained by heaven, and are embraced by such a love of **covenant** <note>original: "fœderis"; a binding agreement or alliance</note> as one can hardly see among other creatures.

We see all brute animals joining themselves in pairs by a completely divine order; those which consist of purer elements, such as birds, are joined by a more sincere bond; the rest agree less on account of the lesser purity of the matter from which they are fashioned, yet they are joined, though in a more promiscuous manner. Let us come to the most excellent creature created from the vilest stuff, namely man, whom God created from the mud of the earth—an animal mobile by itself, which He then soon made one with a **rational spirit** <term>spiritu rationali: the higher intellect or soul that distinguishes humans from animals</term>. Who does not perceive that when man is moved by his animal nature alone, he is more irregular than all other living beings? Yet where he is made true and rational, no work among the works of God can be more excellent. From this man, specifically, while he was still in that most excellent state before the **Fall** <note>original: "lapsum"; the biblical Fall of Man in Eden</note>, God separated **woman** <note>original: "fœminam"</note>, bone from his bones, and flesh from his flesh, so that more firmly than the others...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 109 regarding the nature of unity. # SPECULATIVE MATTERS

...to be united with living beings: they are not said to be united unless they had previously been separated from a single whole, but are merely joined together. Since, therefore, it was not possible to beget within itself, it was necessary to beget in another. For this reason, **human generation** <note>original: "humana generatio"; referring here to the act of procreation and the continuity of the species</note> is to be revered above all others, because it occurs between two who were previously one, and are made one again through **divine marriage** <note>original: "diuino matrimonio"</note>, as Scripture testifies:

> And the two shall be in one flesh, and because of the other, each shall leave father and mother and cling to their other half.

One should not think otherwise than that the husband is to the wife, and she to him, an inseparable part of their own body. When we see those who behave otherwise, we judge them not to be united, but merely joined—not by true love, but either for the sake of base profit or the quenching of illicit lust. Once that fire has cooled, everything else grows cold at once. **Philosophical love** <term>amor philosophicus: in alchemical philosophy, this is the refined, spiritual attraction that binds the soul to the divine or the alchemist to their work, mirroring the "chemical wedding" of substances</term> is not like this. For, being kindled by an utterly unquenchable fire, it cannot be extinguished. Whatever spirits are united through the spirit remain in **union**; but bodies that are merely joined through the flesh are easily pulled apart. 

There is also a specific love of parents toward their children and children toward their parents, which can in no way be compared to philosophical love. For a father would not wish to suffer the loss of even his smallest joint <note>original: "iuncturę"; literally a knuckle or joint of the finger, used here to illustrate the limits of physical or worldly sacrifice</note> for his son, nor a son for his parent—much less a sister for her brother, a brother for his sister, or any friend for another. Who is there who would willingly undergo death for himself, let alone for someone else? Where finally...

->111<-

...is He to be sought? Certainly nowhere else than from the very place where love proceeded. Thus God so loved the world (understand: mankind) that He willed His own Son to be born as a man, so that He might die for men, and by His death pay the debt of death to that justice by which we were meant to be punished. For the Son of God could not die, but having been made man, He died as a perfect man—the Son of Man—and being just, He fulfilled the requirements of justice. O wondrous love of God toward men! Not from the empty philosophy of Aristotle <note>original: "Ariſtotelis inani philoſophia"; early modern alchemists often criticized Aristotle's reliance on logic over spiritual experience</note>, nor from the rest of the "Wise Men" of this world, do such happy waters flow; we pass them by, O wretched companions, while that worldly wisdom keeps us occupied with vain studies. This divine love calls our minds toward another philosophy.

*A dialogue in which the Spirit <note>original: "Animus"; often refers to the higher, masculine principle of the mind or intellect</note> endeavors to draw the Soul and the Body toward itself. The speakers are Spirit, Soul, Body, and the Philosophy of Love, designated by these letters: S, A, C, and Ph.*

**S. A**rise then, my Soul and my Body; rise now and follow your Spirit. Let us ascend this high mountain set before us, from whose peak I shall show you this "fork in the road" <term>bivium: a Pythagorean concept where the path of life splits into the narrow way of virtue and the broad way of vice</term>, of which Pythagoras spoke through a cloud and without light. To us, however, eyes are opened, and the Sun of piety and justice <note>original: "Sol pietatis & iuſtitiæ"</note> shines before us; with it as our guide, we cannot deviate from the path of truth. Turn your eyes first to the right, lest you see—

...[they show] vanity <note>original: "ant"; likely the end of "monstrant" (they show), referring back to the "wise men" of the previous page who show vanity before wisdom</note>, before they have perceived wisdom. Do you see that shining and invincible castle?

From this place, so pleasant and sweet, one must proceed directly to one even more pleasant, in which Wisdom <note>original: "Sophia"; in this context, Sophia refers to Divine Wisdom, a personification of the mind of God</note> dwells: from whose fountain also gush waters far more blessed than the first; if enemies taste them, they must enter into peace. Of those who arrive there, many are accustomed to strive higher, but not all attain the desired goal. There is a place beyond those mentioned, which it is scarcely permitted for mortals to approach, unless by divine power they have been raised to the rank of immortality: but before they are introduced, they are forced to strip off the world, leaving behind the spoils of this fleeting life. Once they have reached it, there is no reason they should fear death any longer; indeed, they embrace it more sweetly every day than anything in the world was ever judged sweet or worthy of their embrace. Whoever progresses beyond these three places vanishes from the eyes of men. But if you wish to see the second and third places, let us ascend higher. Look, above the first crystalline <note>Crystalline or glass-like; symbolizing clarity and the beginning of spiritual transparency</note> citadel, you see another of silver, beyond which is a third of adamant <note>original: "adamantina"; referring to the hardest, most indestructible substance, like diamond, representing the perfected and unbreakable state of the soul</note>; the fourth, however, does not fall under the senses until one has arrived beyond the third. This is the golden place of perpetual happiness, free from anxiety, and filled with every everlasting joy. Now to the left-

->113<-

...let us turn our eyes. Look, you see the world full of pleasures and riches, in which nothing can displease a fleeting eye <note>original: "oculo caduco"; refers to the physical eye which, like the world it sees, is subject to decay and transience</note>, but let us see the end of this journey. You clearly perceive a dark valley filled with sooty mist near the edge of the horizon.

**A. & C.** We see it indeed.

**S.** When those who enter this wide road <note>An allusion to the "Wide Gate" and "Broad Way" that leads to destruction from Matthew 7:13</note> have arrived there, their comforts are turned into torments, of which there is no end. Indeed, if you wish to lend a more attentive ear, you can perceive a wailing <note>original: "ululatum"</note> from here—unless the noise of the joy of those who so anxiously head toward their own ruin hinders you.

**A. & C.** We hear it truly, but how does it happen that these people do not return here?

**S.** Because they do not see this valley of misery until they have passed the last path of repentance <note>original: "pœnitentiæ"</note>, after which there is no return.

**A. & C.** Are there therefore other ways through which they might turn away from that grave danger?

**S.** Rather, there are only two side paths, turning toward the right here to us, as you see. For from this place where we now are, it is necessary for all who come to their senses <note>original: "resipiscunt"; implies a recovery of one's sanity or a spiritual awakening</note> to make the passage through the first castle, so that they themselves may see what you see now.

**A. & C.** We wonder why they do not see the valley of misery sooner.

**S.** It is no wonder, because the way is flat, easy, and pleasing, seducing men by enticing them; moreover, on any flat surface, the horizon is short. But if they had climbed here as we have, they would have perceived the outcome of both paths.

**A. & C.** By what name are they called?

**S.** The wide one indeed is called the path of Error <note>original: "erroris"</note>; of the side paths, the first is of Infirmity <note>original: "infirmitatis"; can mean physical weakness or moral frailty</note>, the second of Poverty <note>original: "paupertatis"</note>, and...

<summary>The Spirit describes the "Wide Path of Error" leading to a dark valley of eternal torment, explaining that those on the path are too distracted by temporary joy to see their destination. He identifies two side paths—Infirmity and Poverty—that lead upward toward spiritual clarity.</summary>

<page-num>114</page-num>
# SPECULATIVE MATTERS #

These paths in which we now stand are called the ways of truth. At the entrance of each one is placed an Angel of the Lord, who is named the "Drawing of the Divine Spirit" <note>original: "tractus divini spiritus"; this refers to a spiritual "tug" or attractive force that God uses to pull a person’s will toward what is good</note>. At this first step, he draws everyone toward him. If wretched mortals resist this pull, the reins are loosened for the stubborn ones, whom the Lord still waits for even after they have entered the way of error. He waits until they have abused worldly pleasures for some time and, through their own wantonness <note>original: "petulantiam"</note>, fall into bodily infirmities—and not undeservedly, for it was through the body that they had been drawn into error.

Only a very few, who happen to recognize their error, return to this place via the first path of recovery <note>original: "resipiscentiæ"; this implies a "returning to one's senses" or a spiritual awakening after a period of delusion</note>. The vast majority of the rest, along with those who did not come to their senses at the first opportunity, stubbornly proceed further. Some who refused to be corrected through illness, and others who have not yet been corrected, are permitted to fall into poverty by their own doing; of these, only a very few enter the later path of repentance. Most, however, continue blindly in their error until they fall into a pit from which it is not possible to rise.

**A. & C.** We see that only a few return through the paths of repentance.

**S.** The body hinders this.

**C.** But I would rather die a hundred times than follow the broad path all the way to its end.

**S.** You speak this way because you have seen the truth; but see that you are mindful of this in the future.

**C.** Wherever you go, I will follow most readily. But before we depart from here, tell me, I pray, how did it happen that we also did not enter the broad way?

**S.** To those who receive the drawing of the Lord from heaven <note>original: "cœlitus"</note> first...

The character representing the Body (C) expresses a newfound resolve to avoid the path of error after witnessing its end.</summary>

...it is given to receive, they should attribute the acceptance to God, not to themselves. **C.** Yet all are drawn. **S.** It is true, but not all receive the drawing <note>original: "tractus"; in this context, it refers to the "tug" of divine grace or an inner calling</note>. **C.** What is the cause of this? **S.** Because while they are standing at the crossroads <note>original: "biuio"; a reference to the Pythagorean 'Y', the traditional choice between the path of virtue and the path of vice</note>, they turn their eyes more toward left-hand vanities than toward this right-hand river of living water, flowing with such admirable skill from the peak of the mountain on which we now stand. **C.** What is the name of the mountain and the river? **S.** For both, the First Drawing of the Lord. **C.** Are there other drawings besides these three you have already explained? **S.** There are many intermediate ones between these, which are felt by everyone in their own conscience, but they are made small <note>that is, they are ignored or undervalued</note> by those who do not wish, as was said before, to receive them. **C.** What is it that you consider admirable in that river? **S.** Have you or anyone else ever seen such a great abundance of water falling from such a high place without a terrifying roar? Are you unaware that hanging water—beyond the fact that it exceeds all the forces of the world—can terrify even a fearless man, or even Mars himself <note>the Roman god of war, used here to represent the pinnacle of human courage</note> on earth (if such a one existed), by the arousal of its sound and wind, as can be seen in many places? **C.** Certainly, I have only been on certain hills from which water flowing down from a bridge far away was able to throw down passers-by, however strong, who were quite terrified and shaken by the wind; unless, through a certain diligence and regained strength, the minds of men overcame it and made a passage for themselves. **S.** The first drawing in this place is the wonder of the river, as well as the desire to taste the water.

<note>Vocabulary: drawing, conscience, river, crossroads, of the Lord</note>

...and the greatest love for seeing its origin follows: it entices the one tasting it—just as a magnet <note>original: "adamas"; while often meaning "diamond," here it refers to a lodestone or magnet attracting iron</note> attracts iron—toward this place, so that he may see the true distinctions of things.

Those who see things superficially, or only by what is obvious, do not believe there is anything else in the nature of things beyond what falls under the senses. It is not granted to any of those people to arrive here, unless their spirits feel and receive the "drawing of the Lord" <note>original: "tractum Domini"; the divine pull toward truth</note> through a special gift and the wonderful contemplation of the river and the mountain. 

**C.** There is one more thing I wish to know from you before we depart. What is the meaning of such a difficult approach to such pleasant places? I mean both the roughness and narrowness of the path, and the fact that both this path and the river are so perfectly straight that a line could hardly be drawn straighter with a taut string. 

**S.** I see, my Body, that you are happy to linger here so that you may feast your eyes longer on worldly masks and counterfeits <note>original: "mundanis laruis, & fucatis"; literally "worldly ghosts and painted things," referring to the deceptive nature of material reality</note>. The fact that you so often twist your eyes toward the left (even when you claim to seek the right) gives me reason to suspect that you ask these questions not so much out of a curious desire for knowledge, but because you realize that once you leave this place, you will be deprived of the sight of the material world. 

**C.** Let us set these accusations aside; at least answer me. 

**S.** Regarding the difficulty, so that you may be satisfied, understand first that there is no way to glory other than through the cross <note>original: "crucem"; referring to the spiritual necessity of suffering or self-denial</note>, just as toward...

...for all arduous things, no entry is open except through the greatest labor, so that things gained easily might be esteemed lightly, and those gained with difficulty might be valued deeply. The lightness of the roughness—just as the life of death <note>original: "mortis vita"; a paradoxical expression suggesting that spiritual life often requires the "death" of worldly desires</note>—is no less a goal than the fullness of smallness. Furthermore, everything that leads into the path of truth <term>veritas</term> <note>original: "veritas"; here meaning the ultimate divine reality that the travelers seek</note> ought to be straight and lacking in windings. **C.** <note>The letter "C" represents *Caro*, the Body</note> Let this be the last thing. 

**S.** <note>The letter "S" represents *Spiritus*, the Spirit</note> You have now heard everything that is necessary; I delay no longer. 

**C.** At least you, Soul <term>anima</term>, stay with me for a little while. 

**A.** <note>The letter "A" represents *Anima*, the Soul, acting as the mediator between Spirit and Body</note> I would not willingly desert either you or the Spirit; however, I remained silent until I had heard the reasons of both. **S.** Truly wretched; follow us, so that you may become happy with us. 

**C.** If only! But what can I do alone against the two of you? 

**S.** Be silent; after a few days, you will act alone with the Alone <note>original: "sola cum solo"; a likely reference to the Neoplatonic idea of the "flight of the alone to the Alone," describing the soul's ultimate union with God</note>. 

**C.** What is this I hear? Will you, who are our leader, perhaps desert us in these trackless places? 

**S.** Far be it; I will be with you as long as you exist. 

**C.** Explain then what this riddle <term>ænigma</term> means. 

**S.** Just as your eyes are, so is your intellect <term>intellectus</term>, and everything you possess is of the flesh <term>carnea</term>. 

**C.** At least make me understand. 

**S.** What if you do not perceive the things that are right before your eyes? When I speak of those things you cannot see, how will you perceive them? 

**C.** Besides the fact that this stony, narrow, and thorny path twists me in wretched ways, with your words you add weight to this mass <note>the "mass" refers to the physical body</note> which is already heavy by itself. 

**S.** I attempt nothing except to lift you up. 

**C.** You have not done so. 

**S.** How would you wish it to be done?

# SPECULATIVE PHILOSOPHY

**C.** <note>C stands for *Caro*, the Body</note> Speaking plainly.

**S.** <note>S stands for *Spiritus*, the Spirit</note> I have not learned what is yours.

**C.** I shall accomplish nothing with you alone. Hey, my soul <term>anima</term>, whom I have always held dear, won't you speak? Won't you provide help to your most faithful friend?

**A.** <note>A stands for *Anima*, the Soul</note> I am in doubt between the two of you; I take the part of each equally.

**C.** And yet, you follow the Spirit more willingly than me.

**A.** You are completely raving.

**C.** I wish I had remained at the crossroads <note>original: "itinere biuio"; literally a two-way path, often used as a metaphor for the moral choice between virtue and vice</note>.

**A.** You should have done so.

**C.** I could not without you, for without you I can accomplish nothing.

**A.** Nor am I able to set a foot without the Spirit; without whom, it is necessary that I be without you.

**C.** And have you learned to speak in parables <term>parabolice</term> in such a short time?

**S.** She speaks only simply: but because her speech is naked, and since you are wrapped in darkness, it seems ambiguous <note>original: "amphibologica"; meaning speech that can be interpreted in two ways</note> or allegorical to you.

**C.** I will achieve nothing with you both; I will be silent, lest you do as you did just now, and save these things for me for the future.

**A.** Nothing but good.

**C.** The Spirit has seduced you.

**S.** Not at all true; rather, I lead both her and you here toward salvation <term>salus</term>.

**C.** So you say.

**S.** You will not believe it until you see it.

**C.** Are you leading me as a blind man?

**S.** Exactly; you have never spoken more truly.

**C.** Woe! What an intolerable crime! Will you persuade me that I lack my own eyes?

**S.** Rather, I will teach you that you do.

**C.** I am listening, then.

**S.** Did you see us, drawn to the base of the Mountain of the Lord <note>original: "mons Domini"; a biblical reference to the place of divine encounter</note>, drinking from the river of the Fountain of Love <note>original: "fons amoris"</note>?

**C.** You didn't drink? <note>By asking this as a question, the Body reveals he did not actually witness the event, proving the Spirit's point about his spiritual blindness.</note>

**S.** Behold! Just now you have openly confessed your own blindness.

**C.** Why did you not command me to drink with you as well?

**S.** Because you will not be able to until you see with us.

**C.** When will that finally happen?

**S.** When you are one with us who are to be united.

<summary>In this dialogue, the Spirit proves the Body's spiritual blindness by pointing out that the Body failed to see the Soul and Spirit drinking from the "Fountain of Love." The passage emphasizes that the Body cannot experience divine grace until it is fully unified with the Spirit and Soul.</summary>

# PHILOSOPHY

**C.** <note>The Body</note> Again, when?

**S.** <note>The Spirit</note> When we have arrived together at the unbreakable citadel of truth <note>original: "veritatis arcem adamantinam"; adamantine refers to a legendary material as hard as diamond</note>.

**C.** Let us hurry then, lest I, though seeing, be called blind any longer.

**S.** Behold, we have arrived at the first citadel; I will knock.

**C.** What need is there? The gate lacks doors <note>meaning the entrance is open or has no physical barrier</note>.

**S.** It is not fitting for a traveling stranger to enter another's home without first asking permission. Command us to be brought in, whoever rules here.

**Ph.** <note>Philosophy</note> What very rare guest calls out?

**S.** Three disciples of Philosophy.

**Ph.** What do you seek here?

**S.** To learn Philosophy further.

**Ph.** What need is there to learn again those things you have already learned?

**S.** Until now we have fed on thistles; we long for more fruitful beets <note>original: "betas"; a metaphor comparing the "prickly" and useless knowledge of the world (thistles) to nourishing, useful knowledge (beets/chard)</note>.

**Ph.** Do you desire to fill your belly with beets?

**S.** With any food better than before: for the herbs we fed on, stinging now on the left, then on the right, have left very rough thorns between our teeth, which give us a loathing for such food.

**Ph.** I hear that you have been steeped in things more unrefined than our school allows: but because we are not accustomed to refusing whoever comes to us, you shall be introduced—but not before you lay down your horns <note>original: "cornua"; likely a reference to the "horns of a dilemma" or the pride and stubbornness of those who think they are already wise</note> before the gate.

**S.** We are ready to obey your commands.

**Ph.** It is the custom among us for newer disciples to be examined.

**S.** So be it.

**Ph.** What is Philosophy?

**S.** It is the love of Wisdom <term>Sophia</term>.

**Ph.** What is Wisdom?

**S.** It is the highest wisdom of all truth.

**Ph.** What is love?

**S.** It is an insatiable desire for known truth.

**Ph.** Where do you get these things?

**S.** From the Lord's drawing <note>meaning the internal guidance or "pull" of God</note>.

**Ph.** Through whom?

**S.** Through those whom he taught before so that they might teach...

<summary>The characters reach the Citadel of Truth where they are met by Philosophy, who challenges their motives and begins a formal catechism on the nature of wisdom and love. The Spirit explains their desire to move beyond the "thorns" of worldly knowledge toward the nourishing truth of Sophia.</summary>

<keywords>Philosophy, Sophia, Wisdom, Truth, Allegory, Discipleship, Citadel of Truth</keywords>

<page-num>120</page-num>

-> ### SPECULATIVE PHILOSOPHY <-

...others. **Ph.** <note>Philosophy</note> What did you hear <note>or study</note> in the Universities?

**S.** <note>The Spirit</note> The philosophy of **Aristotle**.

**Ph.** What does this teach?

**S.** To quarrel contentiously, and to argue stubbornly "for and against," as they say.

**Ph.** "For and against"—what kind of **Chimera** <note>A mythical beast made of parts from different animals; here, Philosophy uses it to describe a monstrous or illogical way of thinking</note> is this?

**S.** I know not what kind of **sophistry** <note>Sophismata: arguments that seem clever but are actually deceptive or false</note> it is; one can twist it now to the right, now to the left, like a man with a **nose of wax**. It was perhaps devised by some "clever-clogs" <note>original: "nasutulo"; literally a person with a large nose, a Latin idiom for a mocking or overly critical person</note> who, having undeservedly criticized everyone else with that snare, sought a way to escape the hands of others.

**Ph.** For what purpose?

**S.** For the sake of showing off one's learning.

**Ph.** Is the teaching then deceptive?

**S.** At least the kind we heard; those who are most steeped in it seem to possess the least wisdom.

**Ph.** In this you are truly wise: to realize that those who understand less about things perceived <note>through the senses or academic debate</note> are often the most truly wise. Before you enter here, your names must be inscribed in the **register** <note>original: "albo"; a white tablet used for official lists</note> of our School.

**S.** My name is **Spirit** <note>or Mind</note>, hers is **Soul**, and the third is **Body**.

**Ph.** Companions closely enough related.

**S.** Indeed, three brothers born of a single birth.

**Ph.** Having first invoked the true light, speak with me thus:

> O Almighty Father, eternal God, Creator of all things that are in heaven and on earth, who created us in your image and likeness, not so that you might reduce us to nothing, but so that we might exist for your glory: give us the grace of your Holy Spirit, by which we may be able to understand and know what your will is, so that by your help we may do it, and in your light we may see light, and [pursue] the truth with true love... <note>original: "profe-"; the text continues on the next page as "prosequentes" (pursuing)</note>

<summary>Philosophy critiques the empty academic debates (scholasticism) of the universities, comparing them to a "nose of wax" that can be twisted any which way. The three travelers—Spirit, Soul, and Body—enroll in her school by beginning a humble prayer for divine guidance.</summary>

<header>PHILOSOPHY.</header>

...[so that we] may follow. Amen. Enter now, for the glory of God and your own salvation, the gates and the **school** <term>gymnasium: in the classical sense, a place of training for both the mind and body</term> of **philosophical Love**, in which perpetual love is taught. Sit down to the feast, so that each may be refreshed by his own food and drink. First of all, let us pray to God: "The eyes of all look to You in hope, Lord, and You give them their food in due season; You open Your hand and fill every living thing with blessing." <note>A translation of Psalm 145:15-16, commonly used as a grace before meals</note> Man does not live by bread alone, but by every word that proceeds from the mouth of the Lord. <note>Matthew 4:4</note>

**Body**, eat and drink the physical things set before you. Your companions <note>the Spirit and the Soul</note> drink in their food not with the mouth, but with their ears; I shall now take care of them. **Spirit** and **Soul**, lend your kind ears. 

Although I do not doubt that you first heard of the power of philosophical study and knowledge through the Lord's guidance and through His angel, you were then more burdened by the **weight of the body** than you will be from now on, and you could hardly bear the weight of it all. It will be well worth the effort, before you come to the **fountain of love**, to briefly review those things which will seem necessary here. 

Before the **fall of Adam** <note>original: "lapsum Adæ"</note>, neither the "broad path to the left" <note>a reference to the easy path to destruction</note> nor the **valley of misery** existed; instead, an entrance stood open on all sides for everyone to these most happy places. After man's first disobedience, the Lord narrowed this most spacious way into a very narrow and difficult path (as you see). At its entrance, He placed a **Cherub** angel holding a **two-edged sword** in his hand, to keep everyone away from entering that happy homeland. From here...

## ->SPECULATIVA<- ##

...turning away, the children of Adam, because of the sin of their first parent, built for themselves a broad path to the left—the very one you have avoided. After a long passage of time, God, the Best and Greatest <note>original: "D.O.M." (Deo Optimo Maximo), a traditional dedication to God</note>, entered into the secret of His own mysteries. There, while Mercy loved and Justice accused, He decided to snatch the sword of His wrath from the hands of the angel. In its place, He substituted a golden three-pronged hook, while the sword itself was hung upon a tree. 

Thus, God's wrath was transformed into love, while Justice was still upheld. Before this occurred, that river <note>referring to the "fountain of love" or "water of grace"</note> did not exist as it does now, gathered into itself; rather, before the Fall, it was spread evenly across the whole world like dew. Afterward, however, it returned to its source. 

At last, as Peace and Justice have kissed each other <note>a reference to Psalm 85:10</note>, the water of grace flows down more abundantly from on high, now washing over the entire world. Those who turn toward the left-hand path see the sword hanging from the tree and know its history, yet because they are too deeply rooted in the world, they simply pass it by. Some see it but neglect to investigate its power; others do not see it at all, nor would they want to. All of these direct their pilgrimage straight toward the valley <note>the "valley of misery" mentioned on the previous page</note>, unless some are pulled back toward Mount Zion <note>the symbolic mountain of God's presence</note> by the hooks of repentance or penance. 

In our current age—the Age of Grace—the sword has been transformed into Christ our Savior, who ascended the tree of the Cross for our sins. All these things signify the various eras: the time of the Law of Nature, the Divine Law, and the Law of Grace. 

Now, listen to the condition of the Path of Errors.

<meta>This page continues the discourse of Philosophy, who is personified as a guide. <page-num>123</page-num>
<header>PHILOSOPHIA.</header>

### The Structure of the World.

**Philosophy** continued: God said to **Adam** after the Fall: *“By the sweat of your brow shall you eat your bread, and in sorrow shall your wife bring forth children.”* <note>A reference to Genesis 3:16-19.</note> He cursed the earth for man’s sake, and it was made sterile—though previously it had produced the most abundant fruit without any cultivation, but only through celestial bounty. Yet, although the earth is now stirred by human labor, it seems barely able to bear even thistles and thorns. 

From the time when man cast off the truth, **necessity** itself rendered him anxious about seeking food and clothing. This necessity is the "second mother" of studies <note>A variation of the proverb "Necessity is the mother of invention."</note> and the inventor of the first <term>arts</term> <note>In this context, "arts" (ars) refers to practical skills or technologies, not just fine arts.</note>, such as **agriculture** and the **pastoral life**. 

Thus, the first and sincere men began to seek those things which they could not do without. The impure, however, not only sought those things, but by right or wrong <note>original: *per fas et nefas*</note> they usurped everything that was weaker than their own strength. **Nakedness** taught the first man to cover his bare flesh with leaves. At last, as industry gradually increased (as is the custom) because of necessity, they covered themselves with the skins of animals. 

Soon after, **hunger** taught them to dig, to sow, and to gather the harvest in its season; to care for the flock, the herds, the cattle, and the pastures; to provide for the nourishment of the family; to instruct them in the customs and laws of **nature**; to preserve **peace**; and to protect themselves and their herds from the injury of the rains and the weather with huts and roofs. This was the entire anxiety of that first necessity. The first fathers of the age lived contented and joyful with these arts in times of peace. But after the earth was filled, it could not suffice, so that...

<sig>F ij</sig>

<vocab>Structure of the world, Adam, necessity, agriculture, arts, nature, peace</vocab>

<summary>Philosophy explains how the Fall of Man transformed the earth from a self-sustaining paradise into a sterile wilderness, forcing humanity to invent agriculture, clothing, and animal husbandry out of sheer necessity. She describes these practical "arts" as the foundation of early human society and peace.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Philosophy’s explanation of human development. On the previous page, she described how necessity led to the invention of agriculture and the use of animal skins for clothing. She now moves into the specialization of labor, the rise of trade, and the eventual corruption of society through greed and conflict.</meta>

<page-num>124</page-num>
<header>S P E C V L A T I V E</header>

...so that each might have a portion of it, and all would be farmers or shepherds; but it was necessary for some to serve others in various ways, particularly those who possessed nothing. **Curiosity** produced diverse kinds of clothing and craftsmanship; from this, the **tailors**, **shoemakers**, and **smiths**—those whom the needle and the hammer define—took their origin. 

The wealth of these men, earned through labor, allowed for leisure among some who acquired for themselves only as much land as was needed to provide food and clothing. For others, however, there was a surplus. These men, observing the scarcity of goods in one place and the abundance in another, practiced **trade** <term>mercimoniam</term> <note>Specifically, the business of buying and selling goods for profit.</note>. From this arose porters and the drivers of pack animals. 

Nor did the system of food lack its own <term>arts</term> <note>Practical skills or trades.</note>: for it devised bakers, oven-keepers, butchers, cooks, and wine-sellers <note>original: *oenopolas*</note> to provide assistance for travelers. In the beginning, all these things were managed in good faith, until **private gain** and **brotherly hatred** broke in. From 그 time onward, the characters of men were changed; the hunger for possessing grew just as much as wealth has increased to this very day, in which no one trusts another, nor even themselves.

For his own convenience, each man changed huts into splendid houses, which at last grew into villages, towns, fortresses, cities, and suburbs into metropolises—yet they were not immune to hatred. Later, through the corruption of time, this produced idle men who wish to live not by their own labor but by the labor of others; at the same time, it brought forth **war**, especially among neighbors. This, in turn, led to ditches, walls, and <meta>catchword: earthworks</meta>

<summary>Philosophy describes the transition from a simple pastoral society to a complex economy of specialized trades and commerce, concluding that private interest and greed eventually corrupted these advancements, leading to the rise of cities and the outbreak of war.</summary>
<keywords>division of labor, history of trade, urbanization, greed, origin of war, craftsmanship, social corruption</keywords>
