<header>P H I L O S O P H Y.</header>

...ramparts and strongholds, giving rise to the defense against injuries. On the other hand, the nurturing of every kind of vice—which is known to stem from idleness—brought forth from the underworld that execrable and diabolical invention: **cannons** and **artillery** <note>original: *bombardarum & tormentorum*. The author views gunpowder weapons as "diabolical" inventions that ended the "construction" of the world's arts.</note>. By these, the very society of the human race would fall, were it not for the protection of divine help. This was the last of the "new arts," and it completed the structure of the world. 

Because of the variety of the things I have mentioned, those who love the world call it "beautiful" <note>In Latin, the word *mundus* means both "the world" and "clean" or "elegant." Philosophy is playing on this double meaning.</note>; but you, my listeners, judge for yourselves what kind of beauty is found in things that, the more they increase, the less peace and the more anxiety they bring. Everything concerning the world, as you have heard, is fleeting; there is no one, rich or poor, who carries even a hair's breadth of these things with them after death. See, therefore, where wretched mortals place their hope, and with what great care they acquire "nothing"—a nothing that ruins those who are not content with their lot. Now, I pray you, hear the story of this transitory age’s pilgrimage.

### *The Pilgrimage of the World, which we call*
### *the Way of Error.*

Once twenty-one years of age have passed <note>original: *ter septeno*, literally "three times seven." This was traditionally considered the age of full legal and moral responsibility.</note>, the majority of people reach the **crossroads of choice** <term>bivium electionis</term> <note>A classic philosophical metaphor, often associated with Pythagoras, depicting a fork in the road where a youth must choose between the difficult path of virtue and the easy path of vice.</note>. The path of every person, drawn by the Angel of the Lord, is slowed by a hook. However, most people crawl under it, others leap over it, and the largest part—while only a very few allow themselves to be pulled—break out between the hook and the entrance of the road. They all flee from salvation, as many as escape this...

...they attempt the path, and enter the world without any restraint. The greatest part of these, having abused luxuries and worldly pleasures, are permitted by our Lord God of mercy to fall into **poverty**, so that they might be drawn back to the former way of repentance <note>original: *resipiscentiæ*, a "returning to one's senses" or spiritual awakening</note>. But the human race is utterly useless; since resisting God has now become innate to it, it does not stop seeking means by which—through its own effort—it might escape the snares it set for itself, not seeking help from Him on whom alone depends every gift of **mercy**.

From this it has happened that they have built for themselves a very large **workshop** <note>original: *officinam*. In this allegorical context, it represents the busy-ness of human civilization and the mechanical arts.</note> on the left side of the way, in which indeed every kind of duty is practiced. Over this house presides **Diligence** <term>Diligence: in Latin *industria*, which means purposeful activity or hard work</term>, who receives everyone except the idle and the lazy. 

On one side, the **literary workshops** of various studies are placed, to which those people retreat who had abandoned their learning through negligence. These people mostly betake themselves to the **manual workshops** situated on the other side, in which they experience through practice those things which were formerly committed to writing by wiser men; they do this lest, as before, being inexperienced in practical matters, they be led into deceptive opinions by the "letter" <note>i.e., by book-learning or theory alone</note>. 

Others, who practiced **trade** to their own loss, and those who, devoting themselves to the **manual** arts, have fallen into poverty, dream day and night of "new forms of things" and unheard-of inventions. These, the merchants display to other merchants, and the mechanics display to anyone everywhere for a price, so that they may repair their losses and then arrive at wealthier circumstances. Once they have achieved this, departing from diligence into...

<header>PHILOSOPHY.</header>

...they tend toward the second region of the world: making a crossing across the **bridge of infirmity**, before which a second "pulling" from the Lord <note>original: *tractus*, referring to God's drawing or attraction of souls toward the divine</note> attempts to bring back the lost sheep to the true sheepfold. Of these, only a very few enter the path of repentance; the rest, stubbornly crossing the bridge, use the goods they have now acquired through **industry** <note>original: *industria*; referring to the purposeful labor described on the previous page</note> even more freely than they did before. 

But because the good God desires to draw them back, He allows infirmities to rule over them; then, seeking a remedy for themselves once again as they did before, they flock to a **hospital** <term>hospital: the Latin *xenodochium* refers to a house for pilgrims, strangers, or the sick</term>—also built on the left-hand side and very large—over which **Medicine** presides. There, a great abundance of pharmacists, surgeons, and physicians can be found, each performing their specific duty. Once their health is recovered, they depart; yet they are not made wealthier as before, but go away diminished in their possessions. 

The greatest part of them passes over the **bridge of old age** despite the Lord’s pull, with scarcely one or two entering the final path, which is called **repentance**. When they have reached the third region of the world, one can see nothing but the bent backs of the elderly, the wrinkles on their faces, limping, lamentations, and sighs; one can hear a whole heap of such weariness. Each person's deeds remain with them until they arrive at the final lodging-house of **death**. In this place, a most severe host, with a hand that spares no one, releases the sting that separates the body from the soul from his deadly bow. What happens after that, I am content to remain ignorant of, leaving the judgment to God, to whom it belongs. The structures of the world now, [wandering]...

...and having understood the state of this pilgrimage, let us see what the outcome of the path of truth may be. By making a proper comparison between these opposites, you may recognize which is the path of error and which is the path of truth and salvation.

->*The Path of Truth.*<-

**Philosophy.** You heard previously that the sword of the angel of the "first pulling" <note>original: *tractus*; referring to the initial divine attraction or impulse that draws a soul toward God</note> was suspended from a tree above the riverbank. In that same place—now in this time of grace—there hangs before our eyes God and the Son of God, Christ, the restorer of the human race. With his manifest love, veiled by no symbols or figures, he attracts those passing by more effectively than a magnet attracts iron—unless, perhaps, the dregs of the world and "garlic" stand in the way. <note>In ancient and medieval folklore, it was commonly believed that rubbing a magnet with garlic would destroy its power to attract iron; here, it symbolizes the worldly sins that block divine attraction.</note> 

Whoever has been drawn here to us—whether by wonder at the river appearing from a distance (as was mentioned) or by the example of inexhaustible love offered up close—we, as ministers of truth, gladly undertake their instruction, as you will hear more fully hereafter. However, before that happens, now that we have taken food and drink, let us give thanks to God, who has given all things necessary to all people.

> We give you, O God the Father, immortal thanks, because you have deigned to nourish us both with your Word and your creatures, and to lead us even to this place in the hope of a better life. May your will be done, so that what was begun by you alone may be perfected in us. Amen.

Now let us betake ourselves to the **fountain of love**, so that you may drink. Behold, quench your thirst: no longer the Mind or Spirit, nor the Soul, nor...

<summary>Philosophy describes how Christ acts as a spiritual magnet on the Path of Truth, replacing the threatening sword of the Law with an invitation to the "fountain of love." The passage includes a prayer of thanksgiving for divine sustenance and guidance.</summary>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues Philosophy’s guidance to the seekers. While the previous page established the presence of Christ as the new "magnet" of the soul, this page outlines the specific stages of the journey ahead—a ladder of spiritual and philosophical progression leading from the struggle with the physical body to the ultimate joy of eternal life.</meta>

<page-num>229</page-num>
<header>P H I L O S O P H Y.</header>
<sig>F v</sig>

From this point forward, you shall be called two entities but one Mind <note>original: *Mens*; referring to the unified spiritual intellect of the seekers</note>, so that, having been made stronger, you may be able to resist your enemy, the Body <note>original: *Corpus*</note>. You must wrestle manfully with the physical self until it completes its natural course and is actually separated from you. Afterward, you may hope that this same body will be joined to you again—more purely and refinedly through a divine mystery—to the glory of Him who created all things. 

How you must fight will be explained in the following fourth step, namely *Frequency* <term>Frequency: *Frequentia*; in this context, likely referring to an assembly or the practice of frequenting spiritual exercises</term>. I will accompany you as you approach the first milestone; meanwhile, I will briefly sketch the progress of the entire Path of Truth <note>original: *via Veritatis*</note> before your eyes. 

Leaving *Frequency* and having been instructed, you will arrive at the camps of *Sophia* <term>Sophia: Greek for Wisdom</term>. Once received there, you will be refreshed by a food far more potent than before. Up to this point, you have only tasted milk; for until you are made strong enough to bear more robust things, you are still but infants. 

For *Virtue* <note>original: *Virtus*</note> will teach you the great fruit of true love, and how much delight the submission of the body brings to the mind—a joy no less than the victory and the palm of honor <note>The "palm" was a traditional symbol of victory and martyrdom in the classical and Christian traditions</note> given to a soldier who has triumphed over his enemies. 

Further on, you will pass to *Power* <note>original: *Potentia*</note>, the sixth step. From her, you will learn not only how to recognize the exercises of the Virtues, but also how to perform them. This step will finally lead you to the seventh and last stage of philosophy: the *Miracle*. In this stage, the martyrdoms of the world are transformed into joys. In short, the end of this path is the very beginning of eternal life and perpetual joy. May He who has led you this far guide you further toward that end.

<vocab>Philosophy, Mind, Body, Frequency, Truth, Wisdom, Virtue, Power, stages of philosophy</vocab>

<summary>Philosophy outlines the seven-stage ascent of the soul, describing the transition from struggling with the physical body to achieving spiritual victory and eternal joy. The journey progresses through milestones of assembly, wisdom, virtue, and power, culminating in a miraculous transformation of worldly suffering into divine delight.</summary>

### *A Dialogue, in which Mind and Body are the speakers.*

**Body:** Oh, who has now become my companion? Where are you, my soul?

**Mind:** Behold, I am here; what do you want of me?

**Body:** I do not see you.

**Mind:** Now once again, as before, you confess that you are blind.

**Body:** Where then is the Spirit?

**Mind:** Do you seek me when I am already present before you?

**Body:** Good God, do you both now speak with a single mouth?

**Mind:** What is so strange about that? Have you forgotten what I said above: that you would have to deal with only one thing, though you had complained that you had to struggle with two?

**Body:** What do I hear? Have you perhaps cast a spell upon <note>original: *fascinasti*; to bewitch, enchant, or cast a "fascinum" (evil eye)</note> my eyes?

**Mind:** Far be it from me to use a spell. Rather, because we have taken a drink together from the fountain of love, we have been brought back into a single unity, so that you should no longer think you must battle against two, but against a single Mind.

**Body:** "Mind" <note>original: *Mens*</note> provides the beginning for so many things: for the table, for measure, and for the months. <note>The Body is making a linguistic argument based on the Latin root *men-* or *mens-*, which connects *mens* (mind) to *mensa* (table), *mensura* (measure), and *mensis* (month). This suggests the mind is the source of all worldly order and timing.</note>

**Mind:** If only you had learned to measure your table <note>referring to physical appetite and consumption</note>, and would remember that there will be an end to your months <note>referring to the Body's mortality and the limited span of life</note>.

**Body:** You say a strange thing: you know it is impossible for me to live without food.

**Mind:** I know this and more: that man does not live by bread alone—are you unaware of this?

**Body:** On what then do you live? I have not yet seen you eating.

**Mind:** Again you bring your blindness into the open.

**Body:** How so?

**Mind:** I live by every word that proceeds from the mouth of the Lord.

**Body:** Would that I too were allowed to live in such a way.

**Mind:** In the future...

<meta>catchword: ...[the future] years</meta>

**Body:** You speak of something far too harsh.

**Body:** You make death pleasant? 

**Mind:** Indeed, to those who have known her. 

**Body:** Have you known her? 

**Mind:** I have known her very well. 

**Body:** Describe her to me, I pray—she who shows herself as something to be feared by everyone. 

**Mind:** Most willingly will I comply with you in this matter; no one fears her except you. 

**Body:** Not you? 

**Mind:** Not in the least. 

**Body:** You are more insane than I can say because of too much wisdom. 

**Mind:** It is you <note>The Mind implies the Body is the one who is truly mistaken or "insane" for fearing the natural order.</note>. 

**Body:** Yet I know that everyone abhors her. 

**Mind:** So you think. <note>original: *Rere*; a concise Latin form of *reor*, meaning to think, deem, or suppose.</note> 

**Body:** Speak so that you may be understood. 

**Mind:** I will speak for your benefit <note>original: *E re*; literally "from the matter," meaning to speak to the point or in a way that is useful to the listener.</note> so that you may understand: the mind of man is immortal; therefore it does not fear death, but rather manfully overcomes it. The body is entirely subject to death, which is why it dreads it above all. 

**Body:** How do you know that the mind is not subject to death? 

**Mind:** It is known from this: everything that took its origin from death is mortal; that which draws its beginning from life does not die, nor does that which is the medium between life and death, if it clings to life. 

**Body:** You speak too obscurely. 

**Mind:** No, but clearly. 

**Body:** What is life? 

**Mind:** It is the soul of the body. 

**Body:** What then is death? 

**Mind:** It is the deprivation of life. 

**Body:** I have not come away any more learned from this. 

**Mind:** Listen: what you consider to be death is for me the beginning of eternal life, and nothing more pleasant can happen to us, if only it were not troublesome to you. But you detest it because you know that by it you are deprived of worldly delights; yet you are ignorant that between these and eternal joys there is less comparison or similarity than there is between gall and honey some-

...when I know you have tasted it. Finally, so that you may better recognize this difference, you should consider what you already know: that the constant pursuit of all the <term>worldly pleasures</term> <note>original: *voluptatum mundi*</note> that can ever happen to you brings <term>weariness</term> <note>original: *tædium*</note>—or rather, I should say, <term>nausea</term> <note>original: *nauseam*</note>—and what is far worse, it brings weakness and sometimes <term>death</term> <note>original: *mortem*</note>. If you desire food now, after you have filled your belly with it several times, you will seek something else; and when you have been satisfied with that, you will seek another again. You will never cease seeking in this world, only to find that your desires have not been satisfied.

What about drink? While it passes through the <term>channel of the throat</term> <note>original: *gulæ canalem*; the esophagus</note>, it brings delight; but when it can no longer enter, it causes great torment. Yet the body cannot abstain from what kills it—and still you fear the very death that you bring upon yourself! O wretched and insatiable body, which does not know how to guard against what harms it, and yet fears its own nature! You see then that the human body, when out of harmony with its mind, is more miserable than the beasts. For irrational animals desire nothing beyond what is necessary for them, but rational beings seek out what is necessary the least; instead, they anxiously procure what kills them.

Let us now turn to those things sought for the body's use outside of itself, such as <term>riches</term> <note>original: *diuitiæ*</note> of every kind. For the sake of these, the bodies of men who have not followed the mind are daily tortured in strange and diverse ways. What do they not plot? What dangers do human hearts not undergo, through which they drag the <term>soul</term> <note>original: *animam*</note> to its destruction and separate the wretched <term>body</term> <note>original: *corpus*</note> from the spirit: no other

We have now arrived at the **Gardens of the Multitude** <note>original: *Frequentiæ hortos*; likely a metaphorical location representing the many or the common world in this philosophical journey</note>; there you will be allowed to hear more on this matter.

### Recapitulation of the fourth Chapter and the third degree.

Know, brothers, that all which has been said above and will be said in the future can be understood in the context of **alchemical preparations**. For in the same way that we spoke of the separation of the parts of man through <term>speculative philosophy</term> <note>original: *speculatiuam Philosophiam*; the use of reason and meditation to distinguish the soul from the body</note>, so must we understand the separation of all other bodies performed by fire. Unless such a separation occurs, the **Alchemists** labor in vain when seeking the <term>universal medicine</term> <note>original: *medicinam vniuersalem*; the legendary Panacea or Philosopher's Stone believed to cure all illness</note> that is meant to cure all diseases without distinction. All things must be considered most diligently, which <gloss>must be considered</gloss> <note>original: *Consideranda*; this is the catchword for the following page</note>

...which has been said concerning man in this book. For there is not a single little word in it that does not refer to the **chemical art** <note>original: *artem chymicam*; referring to alchemy, which at the time was the study of both material chemistry and spiritual transformation</note>, and was not placed there for its explanation. Such is the **philosophical love** <note>original: *amor philosophicus*; in an alchemical context, this refers to the natural affinity or attraction between different substances</note> and enmity between the parts of inanimate things, just as it exists in the parts of man.

But in the former, no more than in the latter, a true union cannot be achieved unless the **corruption** <note>original: *corruptione*; the presence of "impurities" or "dross" that prevents perfect blending</note> of the said parts is first removed before their conjunction. Therefore, you must create peace among enemies so that friends may come together as one. In all imperfect bodies—those falling short of their ultimate perfection—friendship and enmity are innate together. If this enmity is removed by human wit or industry, it is necessary for the other [friendship] to return to its ultimate perfection through the **art**, which we have explained in the union of man.

Separate therefore the parts of each body by fire; cleanse the impure ones; those that are pure do not need cleansing. Mix pure with pure, yet first **sublimate** <term>sublimation</term> <note>original: *sublimato*; the process of heating a substance until it vaporizes and then becomes solid again, used here metaphorically for raising "heavy" matter to a "higher" state</note> the heavier things with the lighter ones; thus will happen what was said above: the **fixed** becomes **volatile**, and the bodily also becomes **spiritual** <note>original: *fixum volatile, corporeum quoq; spirituale*; a central alchemical axiom where solid matter is made to rise (volatized) and spirits are "fixed" into solid form</note>.

Thus far we have touched upon the comparison of **physical alchemy**. Therefore, we must pause here until we have followed through with what remains in **metaphysical alchemy**. However, the reader must be warned here to understand what has been said through a clear comparison, or else they will necessarily be confused. Therefore, it will be no trouble to set down the difference between those things about which there might be doubt. As of-

I have spoken of **inanimate bodies** in this way above so that no one might think I wish to contradict the **alchemists** <note>original: *alchymistis*</note>, who attribute **spirit, soul, and body** <note>original: *spiritum, animam & corpus*</note> to all natural bodies in their own fashion. Indeed, they are able to demonstrate this visibly to those who deny it, whenever there is a need.

Likewise, I use the term **sensitive beings** <note>original: *sensitiua*</note> for **irrational brutes** <note>animals</note>. I do not include **rational beings** <note>humans</note> in this term; for although humans possess a sensitive soul along with a rational one, I shall use the more important term—**rational**—to signify them.

Finally, concerning the matters mentioned above, one must not be ignorant of the fact that the **separation** of rational beings is twofold: it is either **voluntary**, which we discussed earlier, or **natural**, which does not concern the chemists. The separation of sensible and inanimate things is also twofold: **natural** and **artificial**, the latter of which concerns **chemistry** <note>original: *chymiam*; here meaning the alchemical art of laboratory separation</note>. 

A **voluntary separation** occurs while the parts remain within the whole, but natural and artificial separations do not. The instrument of voluntary separation is the **spirit** and the **breath of life** <note>original: *spiraculum vitae*</note>; the instrument of natural separation is **death**; and the instrument of artificial separation is **fire**. Nothing shall now stand in your way from being able to equate the **alchemy of rational beings** <note>the spiritual transformation of man</note> with those separations and unions that can be performed upon **sensible** and **inanimate** things in the future. Now, as for the reason why I have placed **rational alchemy** first, it is [not]...

You might think this effort is in vain, but because if this process and **natural separation** are not understood, you will necessarily remain ignorant of the **artificial** kind. But so that I do not seem to have failed you regarding **natural alchemy** <note>original: *Alchymia naturali*</note>, you will hear only as much about it now as suffices for the understanding of the rest; and I shall begin my discourse with **separation**. 

You know that once a human has completed the lifespan appointed for them by nature, they are resolved into their parts (which we stated above are **Spirit, Soul, and Body** <note>original: *Spiritum, Animam, & Corpus*</note>). This occurs through the operation of the **corruption of death**: by the extinction of the **natural fire** <note>The innate heat that medieval physicians believed sustained life</note>, as the **radical moisture** <term>radical moisture</term> <note>original: *humore radicali*; the vital fluid or "oil" that was thought to fuel the body's internal heat; its exhaustion resulted in old age and death</note> fails, the Spirit and Soul depart, leaving the body behind. The body is then separated once more into its **elemental parts** through **putrefaction** <note>decomposition</note> occurring in the earth. Every element returns to its own: the earthy part is devoured by the earth, the watery by water, and so on for the others. 

The Spirit and Soul return to from whence they proceeded—not so that they might be separated from the body forever, but so that they may be joined into one in a better composition by **divine artifice** <note>A reference to the resurrection or the glorified body in alchemical/theological terms</note>. After this union, they shall be separated no more. This is the greatest power of union: the inseparable compaction of parts into one. 

Furthermore, unless the elements of rational bodies are nourished by other elements, they cannot retain the spirit and soul. Food and drink cast into the stomach are "cooked" into **digestion** <note>original: *digestionem*; medieval science viewed digestion as a form of internal heat-based cooking</note>. In no other way can the separation of the parts of bodies occur except through **corruption** or **putrefaction**. ## *On Frequency, the fourth philosophical grade. Chapter V. In which Mind, Body, and Frequency converse.*

**Mind:** Many greetings to you, O **Frequency** <note>original: *Frequentia*; likely representing the concept of persistence or the frequent application of study and practice</note>. 
**Frequency:** I also bid you greetings, most welcome newcomers and guests. From where do you come, I ask? 
**Mind:** From this philosophy of love, we make our way toward the camps of virtue. 
**Frequency:** Rightly so; I ask that you stay with me for a while, until the labors undertaken in your journeying may be eased, refreshed by some rest. 
**Mind:** We shall most willingly obey you in this matter. 
**Frequency:** Enter, then, [constantly]—

In this place there is no rest; hence it happens that no place here seems built for sluggishness, but under the open sky they look for watchful **cultivators** <term>cultivators</term> <note>original: *colonos*; those who tend or inhabit the land, used here as an allegory for those who tend the "garden" of their soul</note> of the garden of virtues. ->P H I L O S O P H Y.<-

The <term>responsibility</term> <note>original: *ratio*; here meaning the power of reason or the moral account of one's actions</note> is now yours, not its, which it has cast aside: you must consider the **knowledge of God** to be entrusted to you (as it indeed is). For this reason, you shall not be excused on its account, since you stand in the place of a <term>tutor</term> <note>original: *paedagogus*; historically, a guardian or teacher who supervised a child's conduct</note> to it; nor will it be excused because of you, since it previously made itself a slave from being free.

Observe, then, how I deal with these gardens; I care for them in two ways, namely by **weeding** and by **watering**. In the former, I pull out the useless, overgrowing weeds by the roots, lest they stifle the growth of the good ones. You ought to do the same with your body: by first casting away evil thoughts and enmity from yourself, you shall restrain it from every evil work. It is not fitting for one teacher to teach one thing and do another. For if you give the body the occasion for sinning, why, I ask, would you blame it?

The body observes nothing; it is the mind that carries out the silent operations of the imagination, which the body then attempts to perform. The conception belongs to the mind, but the **execution** belongs to the body. What if I were to pull out the <term>darnel</term> <note>original: *lolium*; a weed often found in grain fields, used here as a metaphor for a bad habit</note> weed and in the meantime sow <term>tares</term> <note>original: *zizanium*; another type of invasive weed, representing a different kind of vice</note>? Would I have corrected anything other than that which I myself committed? 

See to it, then, that you do not attribute more to the body than it is capable of. It is true that the body is more prone to carrying out wicked concepts and takes more delight in them than in good ones; but if you cast away all evil thoughts from yourself and substitute good ones in their place, the body will be able to do nothing but good, or at least desist from all evil. This is one **method** of conquering the body; now hear the other. If I were only to protect my gardens from useless things, certainly...

# S P E C U L A T I V E #

...I would have accomplished little or nothing, since another far more necessary care must be applied to those things which have been sown, namely **irrigation**, the other half of all plant nourishment, without which they cannot grow. In this way, you shall also water your body with **living water**, which is the **Word of God**; meditate on this day and night, and on nothing else, so that the body may be able neither to speak nor to act upon anything other than that very Word. 

You see now that bodies can be perfectly compared to fields or gardens, which we read that Christ our Savior also did in the Gospel. He says: "The good seed which falls into well-cultivated ground brings forth good fruit"; likewise, the Word of God which falls upon a soft heart will nourish the body of the one whose heart has been <term>contrite</term> <note>original: *contritum*; literally "ground down" or "crushed," referring here to a heart broken by repentance</note> and humbled. It is certain that from the time of the first <term>fall</term> <note>original: *lapsus*; referring to the Fall of Man in Genesis</note>, the hearts of men have been hardened like stones; unless they are softened by the Word of God, they remain hard for eternity. 

Pharaoh’s heart was hard from his mother's womb (as are the hearts of all men); but because it was not softened by the Word of God, it remained hardened. We are all born rebels and enemies to God, and we remain so until that enmity is uprooted by the **Great Husbandman** <note>original: *summum agricolam*; literally the "highest farmer," referring to God as the cultivator of souls</note>, to whom you, O **Mind**, must show yourself a servant. You have the seed, the field, and the water; nothing more is lacking except for tireless, uninterrupted labor. You must never cease to turn over in your thoughts <note>original: *revolvere*; to revolve or ponder</note> those things which you have already heard and will yet hear; otherwise, through neglect, you will give an opening to your enemy. These are the things that Lady **Virtue** <note>original: *hera Virtus*; here personified as a mistress or noble lady giving instructions to the Mind</note> has commissioned me to say to you...

->PHILOSOPHIA.<-

[...continued] **practice**, by which you will be taught more extensively. We give great thanks to God, who deigns to illuminate our hearts with His light and to make them softer by His word. Amen. Go happily, brothers, and seek Virtue.

### Recapitulation of the fifth chapter, and 
### the fourth step.

It is impossible for any artisan to become ready and fit in his craft unless he gains the habit for himself through frequency and diligence of work. What would it have profited a baker to have seen a baker working often and very frequently, unless he himself, putting his hand into the dough, repeated the act so many times that, having now become a practiced baker, he was able to teach another? 

This is what usually happens to certain people desiring to learn the <term>Chemical art</term> <note>original: *artem Chymicam*; referring here to alchemy, which at this time was considered the "higher" chemistry involving both physical substances and spiritual refinement</note>: some think they can achieve it by reading alone; others, by only a few operations. Yet, among all the arts that **Nature** has ever given, it is considered the most difficult and the least understood; it is certainly a <term>Herculean labor</term> <note>original: *Herculeus labor*; an allusion to the legendary Twelve Labors of Hercules, signifying a task requiring immense strength or effort</note>. It surpasses the Mind, Body, and the lifespan of men, unless it be granted to one among hundreds of myriads to conquer—that is, to reach the perfect goal of the art. 

Indeed, some are able to uncover many secrets of nature with the greatest study and labor, which they contribute to medical matters more than any other things formerly discovered outside the said art. But to find that **universal Medicine**, of which was spoken above, has until now [befallen] very few...

# SPECULATIVE [PHILOSOPHY]

...[it is granted] only to those who have first been able, by the grace of a merciful God, to bring themselves back to that very thing they desire: that is, those who have purged their bodies of impurity and removed the clouds of natural bodies. I advise all seekers of this art <note>original: *artis huius*; referring to the alchemical art discussed in the preceding section</note> to pray to God constantly, to study, and to work; it is impossible that they should not attain what they have asked in the name of Jesus from the <term>Giver of Lights</term> <note>original: *datore luminum*; a James 1:17 reference to God as the source of all spiritual and intellectual illumination</note>. But first let them consider what it means to ask the Father in the Son: it is certainly nothing else than to ask only for that which is for the glory of God the Father and the Son. Many are accustomed to desire what seems useful to them, but they only receive from God what is truly useful. I say this in few words to many: God aids diligent and frequent efforts and despises the negligent. Let us love the good, so that we do not long for the evil.

**M.** <note>Mind</note> **DI**d <note>original: *AVdistin'*; the OCR reflects the decorative drop-cap or emphasis on the first two letters</note> you hear what <term>Diligence</term> <note>original: *Frequentia*; literally "Frequency," personified here as the habit of repeated practice discussed on the previous page</note> said?

**M.** You must live in this world in such a way that you may live for eternity.

### PHILOSOPHY.

**M.** You are dying more every day. 
**C.** You are raving. 
**M.** Listen: he lives in this world who dies to the world and lives for Christ. 
**C.** Who understands this? 
**M.** Have you not heard in the Gospel that unless a <term>grain of wheat</term> <note>original: *granum frumenti*; a reference to John 12:24</note> falling into the earth has died, it remains alone? 
**C.** What is that to the matter? 
**M.** You are that grain which fell into the earth, because you are nothing but earth. 
**C.** But I heard that <term>Diligence</term> <note>original: *Frequentiam*; personified here as on the previous page</note> had referred the "grain" in the Gospel to the Word of God. 
**M.** Now I hear that you have been paying attention. 
**C.** Indeed. 
**M.** You are not a good theologian. 
**C.** Then you be one for me. 
**M.** This word, "has died," has an active meaning if the "grain of wheat" is taken to mean the Word of God—that is, it "has <term>mortified</term>" <note>original: *mortificauerit*; in alchemy and theology, "mortification" refers to the death of the old self or the breakdown of a substance to allow for new life</note>. And the sense would be this: unless the Word of God, falling into the heart of man, has mortified it [the heart]. And it can also be understood passively: unless the body and heart of man, which have been sown in the earth—that is, having become earthly—have been mortified and died, and have received the grain of the Word of God, and converted it into utility and edification, or rather multiplication, it remains alone and will not bear fruit. Or, in a more Christian sense: unless the grain is joined through union with the dead body, both remain separate without fruit. 
**C.** And are you making a <term>nose of wax</term> <note>original: *nasum cereum*; a common 16th-century idiom for twisting the meaning of a text or grammar to suit one's own purposes, as wax is easily molded</note> out of Grammar? 
**M.** You are still a rebellious Body; I see that we must hasten toward <term>Virtue</term> <note>original: *Virtutem*; likely referring to a personified figure or a specific stage in the allegorical journey</note>. But we have now arrived at an opportune time.

## On Virtue, the fifth grade of philosophy.
### CHAPTER VI.

<margin>Mind</margin> O Holy One, command your gates to be opened to us.
**Virtue** <note>original: *V.*; the text identifies this speaker as the personification of Virtue, the gatekeeper of this fifth level of study</note> Who are you who wishes to approach us?
**Mind** we are two <term>young apprentices</term> <note>original: *tyrunculi*; literally "little recruits" or "novices" in a craft or military sense</note> of philosophy.
**Virtue** What do you seek?
**Mind** To learn Virtue.
**Virtue** You seek a just thing, which should in no way be denied to you. Whom have you had as teachers before?
**Mind** <term>Philosophical Love</term> and <term>Diligence</term> <note>original: *Frequentia*; personified here as "Constant Attendance" or "Diligence," as seen on the previous page</note>.
**Virtue** I am glad such ones gave you your start. What moved you to take up these studies?
**Mind** The desire to know the truth.
**Virtue** The knowledge of truth is the root and mother of all things that God established. But to what end do you desire to know it?
**Mind** So that we may be able to practice it.
**Virtue** Toward whom?
**Mind** Toward God and neighbor.
**Virtue** A good intention indeed, to which I pray God give a happy outcome. What are your names?
**Mind** My name is Mind; "Body" is the name of this one.
**Virtue** From whom did you receive these names?
**Mind** I was called "Mind" by Philosophical Love; among the common people, he retains the name "Body."
**Virtue** What agreement is there between the Mind and the Body?
**Mind** Nothing at all; rather, everything between us is in dispute.
**Virtue** What is this I hear?
**Mind** The Body finds it hard to leave the world behind.
**Virtue** Everything that has once clothed itself in the world flees from salvation, and it cannot be pulled back from the world except with the greatest labor. Enter, brothers; and what I am able to provide for your sake, I pray you judge it with kindness. Body, lie down among your own things and look after your belly; the Mind shall be fed by me. O Heavenly Father, who [guides] all...

<page-num>145</page-num>
<header>PHILOSOPHY.</header>

...you restore all things, may you deign to nourish us into eternal life by your word of truth. Amen. Listen, **Mind**: The <term>virtue</term> <note>original: *virtus*; in this context, it refers to both moral goodness and the inherent "power" or "potency" of a thing</note> of any thing is the **truth** of that thing, and truth is **effectiveness** known through experience, and effectiveness is a **heavenly influence**. Whatever does not come from heaven cannot be called a virtue, but rather its false image.

The philosophers of old, who were ignorant of God, depicted virtues for us because they did not understand His son, in whom all the virtues of God were revealed. More recent thinkers are also accustomed to divide virtues into "**Theological**" and "**Ethical**," than which nothing can be more absurd among learned men: because every virtue is from God, and therefore theological. That which is called "ethical" <note>pertaining to human morals or customs</note>, unless it comes from God, cannot be a virtue.

The **bravery** of the Romans against their enemies was actually **stubbornness**; their **magnanimity** was **pride**; their **prudence** was **fraud and deceit**; their **restraint** was an **ambition for honor** (and so with the others). These were vices rather than virtues, because they were performed not for the glory of God, but for the glory of men and for themselves.

Christian philosophers hold only **truth** as virtue, which they divide into **Faith**, **Hope**, and **Charity**. In these three, and within them, the whole of truth consists. Whatever is not found in these is false, whether in rational and sensible things, or in the inanimate. For **faith** is the certainty of those things which are heard and not seen. For this reason it is said that "faith comes from hearing," <note>A reference to Romans 10:17</note> because what is seen is not faith, but certain knowledge demonstrated through the eye. Holy Scripture provides its testimony: "Belie..." <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely heading toward a quote such as "Believe and you shall be saved" or a similar passage</note>

<margin>
**Vocabulary:**
virtue, truth, effectiveness, theological, ethical, Faith, Hope, Charity, rational things, Holy Scripture
</margin>

# SPECULATIVE PHILOSOPHY

...you believed because you saw. <note>original: *didisti quia vidisti*; the end of the word *credidisti* from the previous page</note> > **Blessed are those who have not seen, and yet have believed.** <note>original: *Beati qui non viderunt, & crediderunt*; a quotation of Jesus's words to Thomas from John 20:29</note> 

There is a certain **truth** in natural things that is not seen with external eyes, but is perceived by the mind alone. The **Philosophers** <note>Refers here to "Natural Philosophers," the predecessors of modern scientists and alchemists</note> have gained experience of this, and they discovered its <term>virtue</term> <note>original: *virtutem*; here meaning an inherent power, potency, or medicinal property</note> to be so great that it performed miracles. For this reason, it is not to be wondered at that men have performed miracles through faith, since even inanimate things can provide the same results through the power bestowed upon them.

The whole **art** <note>Referring to the "Great Art" of alchemy or the mastery of nature's secrets</note> consists in this: that a spirit of this kind be freed from its shackles, just as it was said above that the mind must be made free from the body. Therefore, the **body** is a **prison** by which the powers of the **soul** are hindered, and the spirits of natural things cannot exercise their strengths. The spirit of such inanimate things possesses the same effectiveness—relative to its own nature—as undoubting **faith** does in a human being. Therefore, the virtue of humans is true faith, while the virtue of all other things is the **truth of their effectiveness**.

For example, a **physician** <note>original: *physicus*; a student of nature or medicine</note> who is inexperienced in the nature of things would be unwilling to believe that the carcass of any venomous animal—even if it is completely dry and parched—can draw all the poison entirely to itself when placed over an infected wound or a pestilential abscess. This fact is very well known to the **chemists** <note>original: *chymicis*; early practitioners of chemistry and alchemy</note> who scrutinize the powers of nature, and they have discovered it through the **magnetic force** of nature. Since such people do not believe that nature can provide what it is capable of, they are unworthy of its powers; they do not deserve to attain from the very storehouses of nature the secret tru... <note>original: *arcanum ve-*; likely *arcanum veritatis*, the secret of truth</note>

-># PHILOSOPHY<-

at the very least. If they should even see some experiment performed by another, they will not inquire further as to the reason why such a thing happens; instead, being content with the experiment itself, they will merely use it. In this ignorance, the greatest part of the **Physicists** <note>original: *Physicorum*; referring to natural philosophers or those who study the physical world</note> remain stuck, saying, "it possesses this," without curiously searching out from where it comes. However, the **physical alchemist** <note>original: *chymista physicus*; a practitioner who combines chemical experimentation with the study of natural laws</note> is not content with the experiment alone, nor with the simple fact that it comes from heaven, but seeks to know from *which part* of heaven it originates. He investigates the **anatomy of the great creature** <note>A reference to the Macrocosm, or the universe viewed as a single living organism</note> by making a comparison with that which can be understood regarding the **microcosm** <note>The "lesser world," referring to Man as a reflection of the universe</note>. 

This he strives to accomplish by four methods and instruments, namely: **Geomancy**, **Hydromancy**, **Pyromancy**, and **Astronomy**. **Geomancy** <note>Usually divination by earth, but here meaning the study of the element of Earth</note> concerns the lands in natural things; **Hydromancy** concerns the waters; **Pyromancy** concerns the fires; and **Astronomy** concerns the celestial virtues. These are the inquiries which the practitioner, moved by **philosophical love**, willingly undertakes, relying on **hope** (by which he is also nourished) to arrive at the truth of the matter in which his **faith** is rooted. 

It is clear, therefore, that **Hope** is the stimulus of faith, or the certainty of a future truth. Unless one knows for certain that he will acquire what he pursues, his effort grows cold; but when he has confirmed his opinion through faith—aided, naturally, by diligent practice and natural genius—there is nothing he cannot achieve. Truly, in this place it must not be ignored that faith is proven through **charity** <note>original: *charitatem*; referring to *caritas*, or selfless, divinely-inspired love</note>, just as faith can work for good or for ill depending on whether it is moved by a good or evil impulse. **Charity**, therefore, is **faith put into action**. Who [can] judge concerning true faith... <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the final word "po-" likely begins "potest" (is able)</note>

-># SPECULATIVE PHILOSOPHY<-

...[mat]ters otherwise than he who is Faith himself taught, that it is to be known by its works? Some, understanding this matter, have said that charity <note>original: *charitatem*; in this context, it refers to divine or brotherly love</note> must begin with oneself. I believe this should be understood thus: a Christian man ought first to acquire the habit of virtue through frequent practice within himself, before he is able to exercise it toward others in action. Therefore, let the **Mind** <note>original: *Mens*; the higher intellectual or spiritual faculty</note> exercise charity toward its own **Body** by restraining its vain appetites; in this way, the body will be ready to join you in all things. I shall strive to make this happen, so that it may drink with you from the **fountain of virtue**, and then, both having been made one, you may have peace in union. 

Approach this fountain, O **Body**, so that you may drink to your fill with your **Mind**, and in the future, you will no longer thirst for vanities. Oh, the wonderful efficacy of the fountain, which makes one out of two and brings peace between enemies! The fountain of love can create a mind out of spirit and soul, but here it produces one single man from mind and body. We give thanks to you, Father, that you have deigned to make your children partakers of your inexhaustible fountain of virtues. Amen. Go now, having become a wise man, toward **Power** <note>original: *Potentiam*</note>, from which you shall learn **Constancy** <note>original: *Constantiam*</note>, and may you fare well and happily.

Every single thing possesses its own virtue and **celestial influence** <note>original: *influentia cœlestis*; the subtle energy or "pouring in" of power from the stars and heavens onto earthly objects</note>, which can by no means be seen with external eyes; rather, only its effects are visible. <meta>This page continues the discussion from page 148 regarding the hidden virtues and celestial influences present in all things. The previous page ended mid-sentence, likely discussing the visible effects of these invisible virtues, leading into the example of the magnet.</meta>

<page-num>149</page-num>
-># PHILOSOPHY<-
->### G iij<-

[of the attraction] of iron made by the Magnet; yet we cannot see the magnetic power hidden in the stone itself, because it is a spirit <note>original: *spiritus*; in this context, an invisible, active force or essence rather than a ghost</note> and does not fall under the senses; so it is with other things. Wine has various powers within itself, which have been known by philosophers through the experiment of the chemical art <note>original: *artis chymicæ*; referring to alchemy or early laboratory chemistry</note> in its separation: Its spirit heats and dries, yet its body produces the complete opposite; for it moistens with cooling. The experience of these can be seen in the "burning water" <note>original: *aqua ardentem*; distilled alcohol or brandy</note> and in vinegar. These faculties are entirely contrary due to separation, but before it they were not so different, because then they participate more in the mixture through a natural union. Who (unless they are out of their mind) would deny that such virtues exist in all other natural bodies, to a greater or lesser degree? 

He would be no philosopher who denied that bread or grain has a spirit within itself no less effective than the spirit of wine, even if it is not as abundant in bread as it is in wine; yet, for an equal quantity of its spirit, it is just as effective. If such a spirit can be separated from bread and wine artificially <note>that is, by human skill or alchemy</note>, how much more naturally does its separation occur within a human being? From these things, philosophical transmutations <note>the process of one substance changing into another</note> can be understood: do we not know that the purer substance of both bread and wine is transmuted into flesh and blood? and that from an invisible vegetable form, a visible animal form is made? For this reason, the species <note>the essential nature</note> of things are not changed, but their forms. Who still doubts—

<page-num>150</page-num>
<header>SPECULATIVE [PHILOSOPHY]</header>

[under]stand that philosophical transmutations <note>original: *transmutationes philosophicas*; the alchemical process of changing one substance into another</note> can be performed through art <note>original: *artem*; here referring to the "Great Art" of alchemy or human skill</note> within a human being much more effectively than nature itself performs them through the transformation of bread and wine. This is because there are other bodies in the natural world that possess greater natural virtue and celestial influence <note>the historical belief that stars and planets transmit specific powers into earthly materials</note>. From these bodies, spirits can be separated—in the same way they are from wine and bread—and changed into human substance, flesh, and blood, even more excellently through art than nature is able to achieve. 

But why should I repeat so often what is clearer than daylight to philosophers? Whoever refuses to believe this should test it through the chemical art <note>original: *artem chymicam*; alchemy or early chemistry</note>. If they are ignorant of it, let them learn before they experiment; for if they attempt such things through common distillation techniques, they will **lose both their oil and their labor** <note>original: *oleum & operam perdet*; a common Latin idiom meaning to waste one's time and resources on a fruitless task</note>. No one should be forced to act for their own benefit, but only warned; if a person ignores the warning, they do so to their own ruin, not the warner’s.

[...do you] believe? **Seeker:** By power. **Philosophy:** By whose power, exactly? **Seeker:** I do not doubt that all truth is situated in the virtue <note>original: *virtute*; in this context, it refers to both moral excellence and the inherent "virtue" or essential potency of a being</note> of the Most High alone.

**Philosophy:** Now you have shown yourself worthy of confirmation; therefore, pay attention to those things I am about to explain to you. Power is the Steadfastness <note>original: *Constantia*; the quality of remaining firm, stable, and unchanging</note> of the virtue received from the Lord. There is no reason for anyone to think they can have even the smallest spark of virtue from anywhere else than from God. But if anyone seeks it from themselves, or from anyone other than the one I have named, they will attain vice instead of virtue, to their own destruction.

In this way, the Pagans <note>original: *Gentiles*; referring to the ancient Greeks and Romans who lived before the Christian era</note> were once accustomed to hunt for virtues through "heroic works" (as they called them) for no other reason than to leave a reputation of themselves for posterity, wretchedly placing their highest good and happiness in that fame. But how well they succeeded, the outcome of such "memorable" deeds has clearly taught us. Because their kingdom was founded on sand, and not upon the rock <note>a reference to the biblical parable of the wise and foolish builders in Matthew 7:24–27</note>, it necessarily fell; indeed, no greater trace of that ruin remains than for their foolishness to be exposed to those who came after them. But if the Pagans had built upon the rock of truth, their kingdom would have been stable.

It is not the footsteps of men that should be followed, but those of truth, in which all things are stable. You could not be better confirmed, brother, than through virtue itself, which is nothing other than truth. If you strive with all your might to observe this truth in all your works and words, it will strengthen you more every day, until, having become powerful in battle like a lion, you are able to overcome all the forces of the world,

...do not fear death, nor whatever violence diabolical tyranny can devise. Just as pure gold in the fire always remains purer and rejoices <note>original: *iubilat*; to shout for joy or shine brightly, often used in a spiritual sense</note> most gloriously for a very long time without being diminished, so too do the confirmed witnesses <note>original: *professores*; those who openly profess or "bear witness" to the truth, even unto death</note> of truth rejoice in every martyrdom—of which you will hear more below. 

But this is worthy of note: just as a habit or readiness <note>original: *habitus & promptitudo*; the internal disposition and the skill to act quickly and correctly</note> is acquired through the frequent practice of all arts and works, so too it comes from the diligent observation of truth, in both deeds and words. The **Steadfastness** <note>original: *Constantia*; continuing the theme from the previous page of power being found in spiritual stability</note> of virtues is infused from heaven into power, which finally, through uninterrupted observation, passes into a miracle. This is what you must now strive for; travel by this path. Farewell.

---

### *Recapitulation of the seventh chapter, and of the sixth grade.*

Although virtues <note>original: *virtutes*; here referring to the active medicinal or spiritual "powers" or "essences" within a substance</note> are extracted from natural bodies—even inanimate ones—as has been said, they will not be suitable for any excellent work unless they are further led through the **Chemical art** <term>Chymicam; the early modern term for alchemy and chemistry, focused on the purification and separation of substances</term> to their extreme power. The more they are purified and refined through diligent **Chemical** observation, the more they approach their original heavenly influence. Consequently, they penetrate and act more effectively, so that the work they perform in the **Medicine** <term>Medicina; here used in the broad sense of a curative agent or "universal medicine" that heals both metals and the human body</term> of any infirmities whatsoever should truly be regarded as a miracle.

<summary>The text concludes a lesson on the spiritual strength of 'Steadfastness' (Constantia) as a requirement for miracles, then summarizes how the Chemical art purifies natural substances to restore their heavenly power for use in healing.</summary>
<keywords>Steadfastness, martyrdom, alchemy, Chemical art, Medicine, purification, heavenly influence, virtues, transmutation</keywords>

<header>PHILOSOPHY.</header>

## *Concerning the miracle, the seventh and final Philosophical grade. CHAPTER VIII.*

A miracle is the result of the constancy of Truth. In the declaration of this, we shall use only a single example, by which the miracle will be clearly revealed. A certain Philosopher, having been singled out by a tyrannical Emperor for his profession of the truth, was subjected to this martyrdom: specifically, that he was to be crushed alive with a pestle inside a hollowed-out stone of iron. He said, "Crush, tyrant, crush this little body <note>original: *corpusculum*; a diminutive form of 'corpus,' suggesting the philosopher's view of the physical body as small or insignificant compared to the soul</note>, for you will never crush my mind." When this greatly disturbed the Emperor—who did not wish him to speak the truth any longer, as he could not bear to hear it—he ordered the executioner to cut out the philosopher’s tongue. Truly, the philosopher bit it off with his own teeth and spat it into the Emperor’s face before his body could be ground into a pulp. Oh, what admirable constancy in a man! One who, undeterred by such a great martyrdom and sustaining death with the highest joy, conquered the tyranny of the world!

### *Recapitulation of the seventh grade, the eighth chapter, and the conclusion of the entire preceding work.*

Just as there are seven philosophical grades through which the entrance to the heights of the mind lies open, there are also seven chemical operations <note>original: *operationes chymicæ*; the physical laboratory steps that mirror the spiritual progression of the soul</note> through which the craftsman can arrive at that most excellent secret of medicine. Chemical **putrefaction** <term>putrefactio; the process of breaking down a substance through decay to release its inner essence</term> is compared to the study of the philosophers; for just as philosophers are prepared for knowledge through study, so the things of na-

<header>SPECULATIVE MATTERS</header>

[na]tural things proceed through putrefaction <term>putrefaction: in alchemy, the breaking down or "death" of a substance, often characterized by darkening, which allows for new growth</term> to solution <term>solution: the process of dissolving a solid into a liquid</term>: to which philosophical knowledge is compared. For just as bodies are dissolved through solution, so are the doubts of philosophers resolved through knowledge. In no other way than from philosophical love does the frequenting of studies follow; likewise, from the congelation <term>congelation: the thickening or solidification of a liquid into a mass</term> of the chemists, the first union occurs through diligence and the repetition of operations. Just as the wits of philosophers are sharpened by frequent use, so the parts of bodies are refined by chemical washing <term>ablution: the cleansing or purification of a substance to remove impurities</term>. In the same manner that philosophers are united through virtue, elements are joined together in their smallest parts through composition. Through power, the philosophical virtues are confirmed; likewise, through fixation <term>fixation: the process of making a volatile substance stable so that it remains solid even under intense heat</term>, spirits are fixed in their bodies so that they may no longer flee. Just as philosophical works show their power through a miracle, chemical medicines reveal their perfection through projection <term>projection: the final step of the Great Work, where the alchemical "medicine" or Philosopher's Stone is cast upon base metals to transform them into gold or silver</term>. 

Now you have, best of readers, the whole intention of our work. Unless you have progressed as much in true metaphysical philosophy as in natural and chemical philosophy from these writings, there is no reason for you to complain about me, because whatever was obscured by others, I have most clearly declared to you. Fare you well, and look kindly upon this little work <note>original: *opusculum*; a humble term for a short book or treatise</note>, such as it is.

<page-num>155</page-num>
<header>PHILOSOPHY.</header>

[I intend] to add the philosophical tinctures of our teacher Theophrastus Paracelsus <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), known as Paracelsus, was a revolutionary Swiss physician and alchemist who advocated for the use of chemical medicines rather than just herbal ones.</note>, by which cures—both internal and external—can be achieved through the renewal of the blood. These are the simplest and most powerful of all medicines drawn out through the alchemical art. I have described their method of preparation, along with how to administer them, from the aforementioned Paracelsus’s *Great Surgery* <note>original: *Chirurgia magna*; Paracelsus's most significant medical publication (1536), which earned him great fame.</note>, so that the things previously said regarding philosophical medicines might be more easily understood.

->*On the Tincture of the Sun—that is, of Gold.*<-

Once the tincture of the Sun <note>In alchemy, the "Sun" (Solis) is the standard symbol for Gold.</note> has been extracted, its "body" <term>body: the physical residue or dross of a metal that remains after its spiritual essence or "tincture" has been removed</term> remains white, while the tincture itself is pure and separated from the impure (that is, from its body). This separation is necessary. The tincture must be clarified or "exalted" <term>exaltation: an alchemical process that increases the potency, purity, or "grade" of a substance</term> in its degree. This degree is doubled five times by twice twenty-four <note>original: *duplatur quinquies bis 24*; likely a symbolic calculation referring to the 24-karat purity of gold being refined through repeated cycles</note>; it does not progress any higher. Then it can be administered to anyone for the renewal of the blood to its original healthy state, as will be discussed in the section on administration.

->*The extraction is as follows.*<-

The body must be "corrupted" <term>corruption: the process of breaking down a substance's metallic form to release its inner virtues</term> from its metallic nature by hammering it with salt water <note>original: *aquam Salis*; likely a brine or acidic solution used to chemically break down the gold leaf</note>. Finally, the residue should be washed with "sweet" <note>i.e., fresh or distilled</note> water. Afterward, the tincture is extracted using spirit of wine <term>spirit of wine: high-proof alcohol or ethanol, which alchemists used as a powerful solvent to "pull" the essence out of a prepared material</term>. Lastly, the spirit is "elevated" <note>meaning the alcohol is evaporated or distilled away</note> from the tincture, which then remains at the bottom.

### *Extraction of the water of salt.*

Water of salt <note>original: *Aqua salis*; a concentrated saline solution or a spirit derived from salt, used as a solvent.</note> is made from salt which is naturally very pure and white, without the boiling or artificial preparation usually used to make salt. Let it be liquefied several times; then, once powdered, let it be mixed with the juice of radish <note>original: *raphani*; the sharp juice of the radish was often used in alchemy for its perceived corrosive or solvent properties.</note> roots and shaken within it. Then, let it be distilled after it has dissolved. Mix this with equal parts of "green blood" <note>original: *sanguinea viridi*; in the Paracelsian tradition, this likely refers to green vitriol (iron or copper sulfate), which was considered the "green blood" of minerals.</note>, and let everything be distilled five times. In this liquor, a plate of the Sun <note>original: *lamina Solis*; a thin sheet of Gold.</note>, which has been purified by antimony, is dissolved into a powder. Let it be washed finely with pure distilled water until the saltiness recedes; for salt allows itself to be washed away and does not mix deeply with the substance, but is separated from it.

### *Spirit of wine is made in this way:*

Take one measure of the best pure wine, unmixed, whether red or white. Place it in a circulatory vessel <note>original: *circulatoriũ vas*; a specialized flask, like a pelican, designed for continuous refluxing or "circulation."</note> that is very well sealed with lute <term>lute: a clay-based cement used to airtightly seal alchemical apparatus</term>. Submerge the vessel in a water bath <note>original: *balneo maris*; also known as a bain-marie, providing gentle, even heat.</note> to the depth of the wine and let it boil for ten natural days. Finally, once cooled, let it be distilled in a gourd-shaped flask or vial using cold distillation until the spirit ascends.

When, however, it gives its sign <note>A visual change in the vapor or a specific smell indicating the purest part of the distillate has passed.</note>, you shall stop immediately: for what follows is the "water of life" <note>original: *aqua vitæ*; here meaning the less concentrated aqueous alcohol rather than the pure "spirit."</note>, not the spirit. Pour this spirit of wine over the residue itself—which is like a fine powder <note>original: *alcool*; at this time, "alcohol" referred to any extremely fine, purified powder (from the Arabic *al-kuhl*), rather than specifically the liquid spirit.</note>—so that it floats six fingers deep above it. Place the well-sealed vessel in a warm bath, where it should remain for a month; then, into spirit

the tincture of the Sun <note>original: *Solis tinctura*; a medicinal or alchemical extract derived from gold.</note> will rise, and a white powder will remain at the bottom of the vessel. These two must be separated from one another. Melt the powder, and from it a white metal will be made. Allow the spirit to evaporate, as is the custom of the art <note>The "art" refers to the practice of alchemy or early chemistry.</note>; a juice will remain at the bottom like a liquid. You shall graduate <term>graduate: in alchemy, this means to refine or increase the potency and "grade" of a substance through repeated processing</term> this liquid five times in a retort <note>original: *retorta*; a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distillation.</note> large enough for the quantity of the liquid. Such graduation is performed only through elevation <note>original: *eleuationem*; the process of vaporizing a substance so it "rises" to be condensed.</note>, which refines the matter, but it must not be allowed to be refined beyond the quintessence <note>original: *quinta essentia*; the purest, most concentrated essence of a substance, stripped of its elemental impurities.</note>—that is, 2. 4. o. <note>These numbers likely refer to specific degrees of heat or proportions in the graduation process.</note>; otherwise, it will be burnt by proceeding further.

### *The method of administration is:*

You shall give one dram of this, mixed with one ounce of Venetian theriac <note>original: *theriacae Venetae*; a famous and complex herbal "cure-all" produced in Venice, often containing dozens of ingredients including opium and viper flesh.</note>, on an empty stomach, using one scruple <term>scruple: a small unit of weight, roughly 1.3 grams</term> at each time. Afterward, let the patient be covered in bed so that they may sweat; let this be continued for 10 or 12 days.

### *There is also in coral a most excellent tincture for purging the blood.*

Let its body <note>The "body" refers to the physical substance of the coral as opposed to its extracted essence.</note> be ground finely into alcohol <term>alcohol: in this historical context, this refers to an extremely fine, "impalpable" powder, rather than a liquid spirit</term>, and let its tincture be extracted just as it is from gold, by using spirit of wine <note>original: *spiritum vini*; distilled ethanol.</note>. Let them rise together sixteen times; afterward, let them be separated by distillation. The "rising" <note>distillation</note> must be done over an entirely open fire; thereafter, the liquid must be separated six times in a Mary’s bath <note>original: *balneo Mariae*; a water bath used for gentle, even heating.</note> and thrown back again over the dregs. This oil should be administered with theriacal water <note>a liquid infusion of theriac</note>, namely, by drinking one scruple of the oil with one dram of the same water.

<page-num>158</page-num>
<header>SPECULATIVE [PHILOSOPHY]</header>
<meta>catchword: minet</meta>

Theriac water is made with five ounces of spirit of wine <term>spirit of wine: high-proof distilled alcohol, often used as a solvent in alchemy and pharmacy</term>, two and a half ounces of theriac <note>original: *theriacæ*; a famous and complex medicinal "cure-all" paste</note>, one ounce and two drachms of Roman myrrh, and two drachms of oriental saffron. Let all these be mixed and distilled together at once; they should be administered as has been stated.

->*The dosage for administration is:*<-

One ounce and two drachms of it should be mixed with ten ounces of chicory water or germander <note>original: *gamandreæ*; a medicinal herb of the *Teucrium* genus often used for fevers</note> water. Give two drachms of this mixture in the morning and in the evening. Afterward, the patient must fast for five hours after taking the dose, and for the same amount of time after dinner. Let this be continued for six or seven days. Distilled waters of fumitory <note>original: *fumo terræ*; literally "smoke of the earth," an herb used historically to treat skin conditions and "purify" the blood</note>, chicory, and similar herbs should also be given.

->*Tincture of Balsam.*<-

Take half an ounce of balsam and twenty ounces of spirit of wine. Place them together in a pelican <term>pelican: a specialized alchemical vessel with two curved arms that return condensed vapors to the main body, allowing for continuous "circulation"</term> or a double vessel, and let them circulate in the Sun for a month. Once this is done, the mixture allows itself to be distilled along with the spirit through an alembic <note>original: *alembicum*; a distillation apparatus consisting of a boiling vessel and a cap with a delivery tube</note>. In this instance, this distillation is better than one performed with a retort <note>original: *retortam*; a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck</note>, so that the mixture remains together in one composition. To this, you shall add half an ounce of another balsam, and let the closed vessel be allowed to digest so that the added balsam can be mixed with the others, and that which emerges from its body may be perfected. For whatever must be graduated <term>graduated: in alchemy, to increase the potency or "grade" of a substance through repeated refinement</term> should terminate its substance into a single body.

->*Tincture of Antimony.*<-

It has been discovered in this world that nothing purifies gold better than antimony <note>Alchemists used antimony to "devour" base metals mixed with gold, leaving the pure gold behind</note>. For this reason, if it is prepared into a drink for a human, it can perform the same service, and even better. Just as the Sun holds primacy among the stars, and gold among the metals, in no different a way does man hold primacy among the animals; for the sake of this perfection, all these things coincide together as one in a metaphysical sense. Therefore, just as Antimony thoroughly cleanses gold and consumes other metals, so it [cleanses] <meta>The text breaks here at the catchword "re-", likely for "repurgat" (cleanses) or "renovat" (renews)</meta> man, by that same reasoning we have stated...

...it purges without a discharge of the bowels, but by a radical purgation <note>radical: meaning "from the root" or fundamental</note>, through which it spiritually eradicates every superfluity: this is the most excellent secret [arcanum], whose construction is as follows. Take the best antimony reduced to a fine powder <term>alcool: in this period, "alcohol" referred to any substance ground into a fine, impalpable powder, rather than a liquid</term>, which you shall reverberate <note>reverberate: to heat a substance in a furnace so the flames are reflected back onto the material</note> in a well-closed reverberatory furnace for a month; from this it will become volatile, first white, then yellow, and finally a light red or saffron-colored powder, which you shall extract from its "essence of the lily" <note>original: *ex lili*; the "Lily" is an alchemical term often referring to the fixed state of a mineral or the "tincture" of antimony</note> through spirit of wine <term>spiritus vini: distilled wine or ethanol</term>, so that it floats twenty finger-breadths <note>original: *viginti digitis*; a measurement of depth in the vessel</note> above it, and allow it to sit, well-closed, until it is colored, as you know: this is the highest preparation of the lily, which purges the human being from the roots, both internally and externally.

### *Its administration is:*

As in a cask of fresh wine in September or October, for every twenty measures let half an ounce of this tincture be added, and allow both to digest and be purified together. Patients should drink from this wine for three or four weeks, or longer, in preference to all other drinks, or even exclusively.

### *Tincture of the Salt of the Philosophers.*

Take salt of gold, salt of antimony, salt of lemon balm <note>original: *melissæ*</note>, half an ounce of each; eight ounces of pure rock salt <note>original: *salis communis puri petrosique*</note>, mix them together, and let it be eaten spread upon a piece of bread on an empty stomach. Nor should you marvel that we place here the salt of gold and the salt of antimony, which are not common salts, but philoso- <note>The text continues as "philosophical" on the next page.</note>

...sophical <note>original: *phica*; completing the word *philosophica* from the previous page</note>: after you have learned those things which it is fitting for you to know in this art, you will wonder no longer.

### *Another Philosophical Salt.*

Take one ounce each of salt of germander <note>germander: a medicinal herb used historically to treat gout and stomach issues</note>, salt of chicory, and salt of valerian; half an ounce of salt of wormwood; one drachm <term>drachma: a small unit of weight, roughly 3.9 grams</term> of salt of vitriol <note>salt of vitriol: likely zinc or iron sulfate, used in alchemy for its purifying properties</note>; and one pound of pure common salt and saltpeter; mix them together, and let it be consumed as described above.

### *The Secret through which those things placed above are clarified.*

Heaven and earth, through man, generate man, who is their son, daughter, and offspring: from heaven he has intellect, but from earth he has a body. And because he is of the earth, he exists within his body, to which he shall return; yet because of the heaven which is within him, he is of the firmament <note>firmament: the sphere of the stars; in this context, it refers to the celestial influence within the human soul</note>. Furthermore, he possesses yet one thing more by the gift of God, through which he can rule the stars, once he has learned it from God. Heaven and earth are together; so also are heaven and man. But just as man is from the earth, for that reason he must be known according to the earth and from it; but insofar as he is from heaven, he must also be judged according to heaven. Therefore man, heaven, and earth are one—as are even air and water. If man knows how to transmute things in the greater world <term>macrocosm: the universe at large, as opposed to the "microcosm" of the human body</term> (as for instance, turning all things into salt), how much more should he know how to do this in the microcosm—that is, in himself—that which he can do outside himself, provided he knows the [the-] <meta>catchword: the-</meta> <note>The catchword "the-" likely begins the word *thesaurum* (treasure) or *theoricam* (theory/principles) on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page provides a recipe for a multi-herbal "Philosophical Salt" and outlines a foundational alchemical theory: that Man is a "microcosm" containing the elements of the entire universe (Heaven, Earth, and the stars), which allows him to perform transmutations within himself.</summary>
<keywords>Philosophical Salt, Microcosm, Macrocosm, Firmament, Alchemy, Transmutation, Medical Astrology, Saltpeter</keywords>
